# Scratchpad

## Feb 21, 2026
- Web search surfaced many pages that simply restated biblical parables; skipped those as “copies.”
- Shortlist candidates (not added to Parables.txt):
  - Anthony de Mello: “More Precious Than Diamonds” (wise woman gives a stone; traveler returns seeking the inner freedom that made generosity effortless).
  - Anthony de Mello: “Prayer is Fire” (inventor of fire; people build rituals and relics but forget the actual practice of making fire).
- Chosen for Parables.txt: the “Paco / Hotel Montana” forgiveness story—simple, merciful, and startlingly universal.
- Added one original kindness parable to AI-Parables.txt.

## Feb 21, 2026 (continued)
- Considered two widely-circulated “modern parables” (Bridge Builder; Tolstoy’s “Three Questions”) but held them back this round to avoid adding what felt too common.
- Found a standout: “The Rabbi’s Gift” (Francis Dorff, 1979; popularized via M. Scott Peck). It feels Jesus-adjacent without copying a Gospel parable: the twist is not *who* the Messiah is, but what happens when people treat one another as if he could be.
- Added a Jesus-like retelling to Parables.txt as “The Rabbi in the Woods.”

## Feb 21, 2026 (continued — round)
- New web sweep focused on: folk/Hasidic/Sufi/modern “parable” retellings (filtered out anything that was simply a Bible-parable recap).
- Considered but did NOT add:
  - “Grandfather’s Wooden Bowl” (strong but leans toward consequence/karma more than mercy).
  - “The Cracked Pot” (lovely, but too ubiquitous; felt less “rare keep”).
  - “Smell of Soup / Sound of Money” (clever justice tale, but the tone can read as mockery more than compassion).
  - “The Man and the Birds” (incarnation allegory; more doctrinal than parabolic).
- Kept: the “two brothers secretly carrying grain” Jerusalem legend — clean, self-forgetful love, mutual honor, no humiliation, no scapegoat.
## Feb 21, 2026 (later)
- New URLs checked included a mixed bag: some sermon commentary, some social media reposts, some genuinely old wisdom tales.
- Considered but did not keep:
  - Ohr Somayach “Blessings in Disguise” (splinter prevents voyage; ship sinks) — true, but felt more like a proverb-illustration than a parable that pierces the heart.
  - The “Cracked Pot” — tender, but extremely ubiquitous and a bit too on-the-nose for this collection.
  - Nasrudin “Duck Soup” — funny and sharp, but the moral edge leans toward ridicule.
- Kept: Desert Fathers story of Macarius and the “Gift of Grapes” — self-forgetful generosity without judgment, performed quietly, returning as shared joy.

## Feb 22, 2026
- Web sweep aimed at “Jesus-grade” modern parables (kept the bar high; skipped near-direct Bible retellings and sermon-commentary).
- Kept: “Parable of the Long Spoons” (Heaven/Hell difference is whether you feed only yourself or reach across the table).
- Considered but did NOT add: Chabad story “The Man Who Wanted to Be Poor” (beautiful prose, but the merit/reward framing felt more transactional than merciful); Pinchos Kurinsky’s “Redemption” (moving, but reads more like a devotional poem than a piercing parable).
- Logged this round’s visited/result URLs in Already-Searched-URLs.txt to avoid repeats.
- Added one new original kindness parable to AI-Parables.txt (“The Extra Chair”).


## Feb 22, 2026 (continued)
- Focused search: Desert Fathers / early monastic tales as “living parables” of the Sermon on the Mount.
- Kept (added to Parables.txt):
  - “The Leaking Basket” (Abba Moses): you cannot judge another while your own sins spill behind you.
  - “The Thief at the Cell” (Abba Macarius): blessing the one who steals, because nothing is truly owned.
- Logged the supporting pages (and a few adjacent sources) into Already-Searched-URLs.txt.

=== 2026-02-22 web round notes ===
Kept:
- The Ragman (W. Wangerin Jr.) — radical exchange, suffering borne, hinted resurrection; retold ≤10 sentences.
- The Three Questions (Tolstoy) — now / the one before you / do good; retold ≤10 sentences.
Considered (not kept this round):
- The Man and the Birds (Paul Harvey / attribution unclear): strong incarnation analogy; more apologetic/seasonal than moral parable.
- The Star Thrower (Loren Eiseley retelling): beautiful compassion-for-one, but feels close in spirit to the Lost Sheep and a bit “motivational-poster.”
- The Bridge Builder (Dromgoole): solid selflessness-for-those-behind-you, but poem form + widely used; may revisit if needed.
Next search directions:
- Seek short parables where mercy interrupts justice (non-preachy), especially from literary sources (letters/essays/sermons with a single tight story).


## Feb 22, 2026
- Kept (added to Parables.txt):
  - The Shoemaker at the Basement Window (Tolstoy story retold; Matthew 25 resonance without copying).
  - The Cripple Who Spent the Monk (Abba Agathon tested by an “unpayable” request).
  - The Woman and the Thirsty Dog (mercy offered from a shamed person to a creature no one owns).
- Considered but did not add:
  - “Salt Doll” variants (strong mysticism, but less moral/neighbor-facing; didn’t feel Jesus-grade for this collection).
  - “Two Monks and a Woman / Muddy Road” (core is good—letting go of judgment—but common retellings lean on gendered taboo framing).
  - “St. Macarius and the Skull” (compassion theme strong, but the hell-vision elements felt more fear-forward than the collection’s current tone).
  - “Water of Life” (Sufi-tavern version: long, magic-wealth arc and jealousy; not clean enough in moral signal).

## Feb 22, 2026 (later — onion/boat round)
- Targeted web search for rare, high-moral “parable” stories (non-biblical; not sermon recaps).
- Kept (added to Parables.txt):
  - “The Onion That Broke” (Dostoevsky / Brothers Karamazov): the tiniest kindness can lift, but mercy hoarded snaps in the hand.
  - “The Hole Beneath the Seat” (Midrash via Chabad): “private” wrongdoing sinks a shared boat; repentance is love for those who ride with you.
- Opened but did NOT keep: rabbinic “hot+cold goblet” mercy/justice allegory (wise, but more conceptual than heart-piercing).
- Logged visited sources into Already-Searched-URLs.txt.

## Feb 22, 2026 (later — brothers/hermits round)
Kept (added to Parables.txt):
- “The Two Brothers and the Night of Grain” (Jewish folklore / Mount Moriah legend): mutual, secret generosity that turns a field into holy ground.
- “The Three Hermits Who Ran Like Light” (Tolstoy): humility before untrained but genuine prayer; learning bows to love.

Considered but did NOT keep (good but not quite “Jesus-grade” for this collection, or too “self-help”):
- “The Empty Boat” (Zhuangzi / Taoist): excellent for non-resentment, but less direct mercy-to-neighbor than I wanted right now.
- “Nails in the Fence” (anger leaves scars): widely circulated and a bit didactic.
- “Salt Doll and the Sea” (mystical union): striking, but not centered on kindness.
- “Mushkil Gusha” (Idries Shah): meaningful, but the folkloric mechanism felt less sharply aligned with Jesus’ moral salience.
- “The Man Who Planted Trees” (Jean Giono): beautiful, but too expansive/novelistic for our “parable” cadence.

Logged newly visited sources into Already-Searched-URLs.txt.

## 2026-02-22 — Web search pass (mirror/forgiveness/bridge)

- “The Bridge at Eventide” (Will Allen Dromgoole poem): build for the feet behind you, not the feet ahead.

Considered but did NOT keep:
- Oscar Wilde’s “The Selfish Giant” (beautiful redemption, but felt more like an extended allegory/fairy tale than our concise parable cadence).

[2026-02-23] Web round notes (Wiesel / Rizhiner “lost in the forest” lightning parable)
- New keeper candidate found in an Elie Wiesel 92NY transcript quoting the Rizhiner’s “favorite parable”: a man lost in a dark forest; lightning briefly reveals the road; the fool stares at the lightning while the wise uses the flash to find the path.
- Keeper rationale: clean image, non-sentimental, fits Jesus’s emphasis on “walk in the light” and not chasing spectacle; we softened the insult-language and sharpened the “use the light to walk / bring your brother” moral.
- Added to Parables.txt as “The Lightning and the Road.”

[2026-02-23] Extra scan: “caffè sospeso / suspended coffee” tradition
- Reviewed multiple sources on Naples’ “pending coffee” custom (anonymous coffee paid in advance).
- Strong dignity/hidden-mercy resonance, but it reads more like a social practice than a parable-with-turning, so I did not add it this round (kept as a future option if we want a ‘parable of anonymous small gifts’).

- “My Friend” (Malik ibn Dinar & the profligate youth; voice: “Do not touch my friend.”) — kept. The “friend” word does real gospel work: mercy that reforms without excusing.

## 2026-03-06 — web round (Hidden Servants / Love III / corridor / Steven McDonald / sensitivity)
- Kept: **The House With No Angry Word**, retold from *The Hidden Servants and Other Very Old Stories* (Project Gutenberg PDF). The core is extremely clean: holiness hidden in forty years of ordinary kindness between two women sharing one house. It feels very close to Jesus’ valuation of the unnoticed, the meek, and the daily work of peace.
- Considered and did **not** keep: George Herbert’s **Love (III)** — nearly perfect as devotional poetry, but too directly Eucharistic / God-human dialogue for this collection’s story-shaped parable lane.
- Considered and did **not** keep: Chabad’s **The Girl in the Corridor / He Gave the Borrowed Money to a Stranger** — morally beautiful, but this pass they felt more biographical than parabolic, and somewhat overlapping with existing hospitality / rescue motifs already in the archive.
- Considered and did **not** keep: Steven McDonald’s forgiveness story — admirable and true, but the article was partially paywalled and the story lands more as testimony than as a compact parable image.

## 2026-03-06 — web round (old man and children; stolen watch scan)
- Referred to Already-Searched-URLs.txt first and tried not to revisit prior deer / saint / poem clusters.

## 2026-03-06 — Web search pass (desert hospitality / dignity-through-giving)
Kept / added to Parables.txt:
- “The Brother Who Came with Vegetables” (Desert Fathers / hospitality section): strongest thing this round. The moral turn is subtle and severe: mercy that only gives can accidentally trap a person in receiving, but mercy that restores a person to giving can fill a house with blessing.

Considered but did NOT keep:
- The hermit who says, “My rule is to welcome you with hospitality and send you away in peace.” Very clean, but too slight for this collection unless we later want a cluster of very short doorway sayings.
- St. Martin of Tours and the robber. Strong and beautiful (“I call you brother” in the hands of thieves), but overlaps with the archive’s existing mercy-to-bandit / conversion-by-kindness cluster.
- Wali Dad / The Gifts of the Grasscutter. Gentle and lovely, but the fairy-tale escalations felt less piercing than the Desert Fathers material this round.

Thread worth remembering:
- One recurring Jesus-grade pattern is not merely helping the poor, but refusing to leave them only as receivers. There is a difference between relieving need and restoring a person to agency, offering, and blessing others.

## 2026-03-06 — web round (forbearance / lepers / dignity-without-retaliation)
- Kept: **The Name He Would Not Throw Back**, retold from the Imam al-Baqir / "Forbearance With A Christian" story. What made it clear enough to keep was not merely patience under insult, but the refusal to defend dignity by shaming another person—especially the honoring of a mother’s humble work and the prayer for the slanderer instead of retaliation.
- Considered and did **not** keep: **The Lepers** (Imam Zayn al-Abidin). Very clean and beautiful—accepting the invitation of outcasts and then inviting them home—but the archive already has a strong leper/hidden-Christ burden-bearing cluster, so I left it out rather than thicken an existing image-field.
- Considered and did **not** keep: Desert Fathers Macarius material this round (widow/deposit; fetching sherbet; helping the thief load stolen goods). Strong, but the archive already carries a heavy thief-mercy line and enough miracle-weight; none felt distinct enough to justify another keeper over the Christian-insult story.

## 2026-03-06 — web round (Maruf Karkhi / the difficult weak)
- Referred to Already-Searched-URLs.txt first and tried to stay off previously logged paths.
- Kept: **The Guest Who Could Not Sleep**, retold from Sa'di's *Bustan* / the Maruf Karkhi and sick traveler story. ## 2026-03-06 — Web search pass (deep sweep; no keeper)
Outcome:
- I did NOT add anything to Parables.txt or AI-Parables.txt this round.

Strongest candidates considered, but declined:
- Sa'di / Gulistan “sour-faced giver” story (“I excused him from making me a present when I saw his face”): morally sharp about preserving dignity, but it reads more like polished social wisdom than a full parable of mercy. Good line; not quite enough story.
- Hatim Tai and the briar-gatherer: noble independence and freedom from obligation, but the center is self-respect more than neighbor-love.
- Chesed shel emet / burial-kindness materials: spiritually strong, but in this pass I found concept and testimony more than a clean, portable story.
- “The Needy and the Needed” (Chabad): piercing inwardly, but it tilts toward vocation and spiritual awakening, not the kind of outward mercy-parable this archive seems to prize.

Heuristic sharpened this round:
- A lot of near-misses teach dignity, restraint, or contentment, but do not quite turn into Jesus-grade parable unless mercy becomes visible in action and the story can be carried in the hand.
- The collection now has many strong entries on forgiveness, hidden giving, and refusing humiliation; future keepers may need either a genuinely new image-field or an unusually clean instance of those themes.

- Kept: **The Ruler at the Widow's Oven**, retold from the Imam Ali / widow / water bag story. What made it clear enough to keep was not merely generosity, but the sharper mercy: he serves the widow while she is still blaming him, and when recognized he asks *her* forgiveness instead of receiving apology as his due.
- Distinct image-field: civic responsibility entering a poor house in secret, then judging itself by the heat of the oven.
- Considered and did **not** add:
  - 'Abdu'l-Bahá and the awkward intrusive guest (Bahá’í World vol. 4): very clean in its refusal to let social annoyance harden into contempt, but slightly thinner as a story than the widow/oven scene.
  - Quaker miscellany pages and Bahá’í kindness compilations: morally bright but mostly fragments, not portable parables.
- AI-Parables left unchanged this round.

## 2026-03-06 — web round (falsely suspected giver / tzedakah without self-defense)
- Referred to Already-Searched-URLs.txt first and then searched fresh paths around anonymous charity, avoiding shame, and stories where someone bears misunderstanding rather than expose another person or leave the poor unaided.
- Kept: **The Purse He Repaid**, retold from the Chabad / “The First Rothschild” story about Anschel Moses. What cleared the bar was the very severe center: he is wrongly suspected, yet he gives up his own savings and then keeps paying rather than let widows and orphans go hungry or force the old rabbi to receive charity under another name.
- Distinct image-field: not simply secret giving, but a person silently absorbing false guilt so mercy can move faster than self-vindication.

## 2026-03-06 — web round (Bayazid / bedside water / insult-mercy near-miss)

- Kept: **The Pitcher in the Night**, retold from the Bayazid / mother-and-water tradition. What cleared the bar was the severe simplicity of it: not dramatic forgiveness or public generosity, but staying ready in secret through discomfort so another person will not have to wait for kindness when they wake thirsty.

## 2026-03-06 — web round (Sacha Sauda kept; dignity/mask/loan near-misses)
- Referred to Already-Searched-URLs.txt first and searched fresh paths around mercy as the only true profit, plus a few dignity-preserving charity essays to see whether any story-shaped keeper was hiding behind them.
- Kept: **The Only Bargain Worth Making**, retold from Guru Nanak's **Sacha Sauda / True Bargain** tradition. What cleared the bar was the severe simplicity of the image: money sent out to multiply comes home invisible because it has become bread in hungry bodies. It feels close to Jesus without copying any one Gospel parable outright.

## 2026-03-06 — web round (Siddhartha and the swan kept; Shibi near-miss)
- Referred to Already-Searched-URLs.txt first and then searched fresh paths around compassion toward living creatures, refuge under threat, and stories where mercy outweighs the claim of power.
- Kept: **The Swan That Belonged to Mercy**, retold from the Siddhartha / wounded swan tradition. What cleared the bar was the hard moral line inside it: the right over life belongs more deeply to the one who heals than to the one who wounds. That felt clean, severe, and still distinct within the archive.
- Distinct image-field: not merely kindness to an animal, but a judgment about possession itself — mercy has a truer claim than force.
- Considered and did **not** keep: **King Shibi and the dove**. It is powerful and sacrificial, but the hawk-versus-dove balance, flesh-weighing, and test structure made it feel morally more tangled and theatrically intense than the cleaner swan story.

## 2026-03-06 — web round (Dostoevsky / shame-ridden weakness / keeper)
- Referred to Already-Searched-URLs.txt first and tried to stay off previously logged ground.
- Kept: **The Poor, Sorrowful Creature**, retold from Dostoevsky's **An Honest Thief**. What cleared the bar was that the mercy is given not to a noble penitent or dramatic enemy, but to a shabby, shame-ridden weak man who has stolen from the very table that fed him. The answer to confession is not triumph, exposure, or even a lesson, but bread, water, forgiveness, and peace.
- Distinct image-field: mercy toward someone whose sin is pitiful rather than impressive; forgiveness that feels like giving a dying man a pillow.
- Considered and did **not** keep:
  - **Guru Nanak and Bhoomi Daku**: lively and memorable, but too storybook-clever for this archive in the versions I found, with the chocolate-cake turn and royal pardon thinning the severity.
  - **Abi Fressah's Feast**: energetic, but built around humiliation and punishment.
  - **Jacob ibn Laith tasting salt in the treasury**: striking honor-code image, but not clean enough as neighbor-love; it leans more on hospitality custom than mercy.
- AI-Parables left unchanged this round.

## 2026-03-06 — web round (Face in the Window kept; Jain merchant and Nandisen considered)
- Referred to Already-Searched-URLs.txt first and searched fresh paths, especially Bahá’í and Jain materials that had not yet been logged.
- Kept: **The Face in the Window**, retold from the ‘Abdu’l-Bahá / despairing tramp story. What cleared the bar was the utter lack of gatekeeping in the mercy: a man arrives filthy, ashamed, and nearly finished with life, and is received not as an interruption but as an expected guest. The welcome itself becomes the turn back toward life.
- Distinct image-field: not bread, alms, or pardon after injury, but the holy seriousness of opening a door in time for someone who is on what he thinks is his last walk.
- Considered and did **not** keep: **Nobility of Savchand and Somchand** from the Jain story book. Strong and beautiful in its refusal to exploit a desperate stranger, but in this pass it still felt a little too mercantile and honor-coded beside the cleaner directness of the Baha'i story.
- Considered and did **not** keep: **Sage Nandisen** serving the abusive old monk. Morally bright, but too close in movement to **The Guest Who Could Not Sleep** and the archive’s existing burden-bearing / insult-bearing cluster.
- AI-Parables left unchanged this round.

## 2026-03-06 — web round (Rantideva / last water kept)
- Referred to Already-Searched-URLs.txt first and searched fresh ground around severe hospitality, last-food / last-water stories, and mercy that includes the socially lowest without flinching.
- Kept: **The Last Water**, retold from the Rantideva episode in the Bhagavata Purana. What cleared the bar was the escalating severity: food given away piece by piece, dogs not skipped over, and finally the last drink yielded to the one whose thirst is failing him. The center feels clean because the story does not stop at generosity; it crosses the boundary where self-preservation usually begins.
- Distinct image-field: not only feeding the hungry, but surrendering the final sip, with the strong line that mercy refuses to draw the line at one’s own hunger and thirst.
- I chose not to add an AI parable this round; the source story already carried the needed force more cleanly than anything new I was conceiving.

## 2026-03-06 — web round (Langston Hughes kept; Tolstoy/Wilde considered)
- Referred to `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and tried to move into cleaner modern-literary ground rather than reopening the archive’s heaviest folklore and saint-story paths.
- Kept: **The Purse and the Blue Shoes**, retold from Langston Hughes’s **“Thank You, M'am.”** What cleared the bar was the severe mixture of firmness and mercy: the woman does not excuse the theft, but she refuses to let punishment be the deepest thing that happens. She gives the boy water, food, trust, and the very money he meant to take, so that dignity arrives before his self-defense does.
- Distinct image-field: not only forgiving a thief, but treating him like someone salvageable before he has earned that treatment.
- Considered and did **not** keep:
  - **Tolstoy, “God Sees the Truth, But Waits.”** Very strong, but the prison-forgiveness / enemy-mercy field is already thick in the archive, and this pass wanted a more immediate, street-level act of restoring dignity.
  - **Wilde, “The Happy Prince.”** Beautiful and sacrificial, but it depends too much on fairy-tale apparatus for this particular collection.
  - **Tolstoy, “Alyosha the Pot.”** Bright with meekness, but more like a life of holy simplicity than a sharply transferable parable.
- `AI-Parables.txt` left unchanged this round.



## 2026-03-06 — web round (Premchand / Agathon / Retrieved Reformation)
Kept / added to Parables.txt:
- “The Tongs from the Fair” (Premchand, *Idgah*): unusually clean act of practical love; a hungry child forgoes feast-day pleasure to spare his grandmother’s burned fingers.

Considered but did NOT keep:
- Abba Agathon and the sick traveler / leper material: morally luminous, but this pass it felt too adjacent to already-collected desert-father mercy pieces and too near the Good Samaritan lane.
- O. Henry, “A Retrieved Reformation”: strong redemption shape, but the hinge still depends too much on plot cleverness and romantic machinery for this collection’s standard.
- Premchand, “The Old Aunt” (*Boodhi Kaki*): powerful repentance and pity, but the scene of appetite and degradation is heavier and less parable-clean than *Idgah*.

Heuristic note:
- This round reinforced a bias toward stories where love becomes concrete through an ordinary object. The tongs were the right kind of image: humble, useful, and morally radiant without being decorated.

## 2026-03-06 — web round (Tolstoy / Two Old Men kept; Umar and Kabuliwala not kept)
- Referred to `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and then searched fresh lanes around pilgrimage, famine hospitality, ruler-humility anecdotes, and literary father-love stories.
- Kept: **The Pilgrimage That Arrived First**, retold from Tolstoy’s **“Two Old Men.”** What cleared the bar was the severity and clarity of the reversal: one man reaches the sacred place with his body, the other reaches it in the deeper sense by stopping for bread, water, land, and hope. The ending image — mercy appearing already in the holy place — felt unusually Jesus-grade rather than merely edifying.
- Distinct image-field: not only helping a stranger on the road, but discovering that costly interruption is itself the truer pilgrimage.
- Considered and did **not** keep:
  - **Umar carrying flour to the widow and hungry children**: morally bright and concrete, but still too close to the archive’s existing bread / ruler-humility / secret-help cluster; more exemplary anecdote than parable-clean in this pass.
  - **The servant who spilled the hot soup and was pardoned, then freed**: strong forgiveness, but the verse-by-verse structure made it feel more proof-texted than story-shaped.
  - **Tagore’s “Kabuliwala”**: tender and memorable, but more elegy of fatherhood and lost time than sharply transferable parable.
  - **Chekhov / Easter side paths (“The Student,” “A Dust Rag for Easter Eggs,” “The Ragman”)**: each had something alive in it, but none felt as clean and universal as Tolstoy’s pilgrimage reversal.
- `AI-Parables.txt` left unchanged this round.

What cleared the bar was the severe simplicity: a poor mother does not give from surplus but from crowding, fear, and real scarcity, and her husband’s response is not complaint but an even swifter mercy.

## 2026-03-06 — web round (Premchand justice / friendship / duty pass)

- Kept: **The Seat Under the Tree**, retold from Premchand’s **Panch Parmeshwar**. What cleared the bar was not mere moral uprightness, but the severe conversion that happens when a man sits in judgment and discovers he must become truthful even toward the friend he loves and the enemy he resents. The ending’s moral force lies in justice restoring fellowship, not destroying it.

## 2026-03-06 — web round (Lagerlöf / Paton / Christmas-family side-paths)

The rat trap image is strong, but the real center is the bestowed name: he is treated as “captain” before he deserves it, and that trust becomes the doorway out of theft.

## 2026-03-07 — web round (MacDonald / Lagerlöf / Hasidic-Seder / dignity lanes)
- Referred to `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then deliberately avoided the more exhausted Tolstoy / Brother Juniper / Desert Fathers / voluntary-Hajj lanes and pushed into George MacDonald, Selma Lagerlöf, Hasidic rescue stories, and a few dignity-preserving kindness paths.

  - **George MacDonald, embedded tale in _What's Mine's Mine_ (Chapter 18).** Striking opening image: a destitute woman decides she must beg because otherwise she would deny her neighbors the chance to do good; but the tale resolves through an overt dream-vision, St. Peter, and a too-direct heavenly warning, which makes it feel more sermonic than parabolic.

  - **Selma Lagerlöf, “Mamsell Fredrika.”** Beautiful, memorable image-field — the dead poor women saying the candles she gave them are now the lights in God's house — but the story is ornate, visionary, and self-consciously literary; it feels too gilded for the plain, portable Jesus-grade shelf.
  - **Gershon Kranzler, “A Strange Seder.”** Strong saint-story: a rabbi misses the glorious home feast because he is led to rescue earthquake victims and hold seder with the wounded in a cellar. Deeply consonant morally, but too hero-hagiography shaped and too event-heavy to become a clean stand-alone parable.
- Additional discarded paths: St. Brigid / cloak legends, anonymous-benefactor and dignity-at-the-shop anecdotes, and a few Swedish/anthology sweeps; most either sentimentalized poverty, relied on retrospective commentary, or lacked the single unforgettable image that can carry the moral on its own.
- Judgment: better to preserve the archive's severity than to admit a beautiful near-parable whose power depends on saintly status, ornament, or explicit explanation.
- Logged newly visited URLs in `Already-Searched-URLs.txt`.

## 2026-03-07 — web round (Franciscan / Hasidic / Saadi / didactic-public-domain sweep)
- Referred to `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then pushed into fresh but adjacent lanes: **Little Flowers of St. Francis**, a few Hasidic / Chabad beggar-feast tales, Saadi / Gulistan side-paths, and a last small sweep through didactic public-domain kindness collections.

  - **Hannah More / `The Shepherd of Salisbury Plain` and `The Power of Kindness` side-sweep.** Worth checking as a control, but the tone remained too improving / tract-like to trust as rare keepers.

## 2026-03-07 — web round (Chekhov beggar / Andersen / Lagerlof / scattered compassion sweep)
Kept / added to Parables.txt:
- “The Woman Who Chopped the Wood” (Chekhov, “The Beggar”): the morally decisive act is hidden mercy, not the gentleman’s rebuke. The cook does the labor herself, refuses credit, and the shame healed by love becomes the man’s turning-point. Strong Jesus-grade fit because it protects dignity while still telling the truth.

Considered but did NOT keep:
- Andersen / “The Angel” and related late-mercy scenes: luminous in places, but too visionary / reward-structured for this shelf.
- Lagerlöf / Gösta Berling “The Beggar”: vivid, but too embedded in a larger novel and too turbulent to become a clean portable parable.
- Scattered Jain / Bahá’í / Quaker / anthology leads this pass: morally decent, but mostly anecdote, exhortation, or saint-biography rather than parable.

Process note:
- Fresh searches deliberately avoided the already-logged Tolstoy / Chabad / Franciscan / Dirk Willems lanes.
- No new AI parable this round; the Chekhov piece felt strong enough that adding an original would only risk lowering signal.

## 2026-03-07 — web round (Isaac T. Hopper / A.E. Coppard / E.V. Lucas / Mary Wilkins lane)
- Referred to `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then pushed into fresher lanes that had not been mined yet: Lydia Maria Child’s **_Isaac T. Hopper: A True Life_**, A. E. Coppard’s industrial-moral fiction, a side trail through E. V. Lucas / essay-story territory, and Mary Wilkins Freeman collections.
- **No addition to `Parables.txt` or `AI-Parables.txt` this round.**
- Closest near-misses:
  - **Isaac T. Hopper, “Theft from Necessity.”** The best image this pass: a man steals children’s clothes for his freezing newborn; Hopper gets a commitment only long enough to verify the story, then tears it up after seeing the misery and fills the house with wood, flour, bread, and garments. Morally bright, but it still reads more as exemplary Quaker philanthropy than as a perfectly portable parable.
  - **Isaac T. Hopper / Patrick McKeever.** Strong on refusing to let an “old convict” be condemned by reputation alone, but the force depends on civic advocacy and biography more than on one unforgettable image.
  - **Isaac T. Hopper / Mary Norris and the Magdalen.** Deeply humane—especially giving a disgraced woman an actual path back into ordinary life—but both episodes feel more like reforming social history than parable.
  - **A. E. Coppard, “Tribute.”** Sharp and morally serious, yet too satirical-systemic and too essay-like in its indignation for this archive’s cleaner image-standard.
  - **E. V. Lucas / “The Human Touch” side-path.** Intriguing, but too elusive to assess cleanly from the available trail, and in any case likely more essay than parable.
- Judgment: better to preserve the archive’s severity than to admit a moving anecdote whose power leans on biography, reform-energy, or social argument.
- Logged newly visited URLs in `Already-Searched-URLs.txt`.

## 2026-03-07 — web round (Dostoevsky: Peasant Marey / Honest Thief comparison)
- Referred to `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then pushed into a fresh Dostoevsky lane rather than returning to the more exhausted Tolstoy / Franciscan / Hasidic / saint-legend paths.
- Kept and added to `Parables.txt`:
  - **The Hand That Smelled of Earth** — retold from Dostoevsky’s **“The Peasant Marey.”** What cleared the bar was the single plain action: a peasant in the field interrupts his work to calm a frightened child, touches his trembling lips with an earth-stained finger, blesses him, and keeps watch until he is safe. No reward, no audience, no miracle, no rhetoric — just unforced tenderness crossing rank.
- Serious near-miss:
  - **“An Honest Thief.”** Morally strong and already partly drafted in the archive’s imaginative register (`The Poor, Sorrowful Creature` is nearby in shape), but I did not choose it this round because its force depends more on drawn-out pity, confession, and deathbed release, whereas **Marey** rests on one instantaneously transferable image.
- Process judgment:
  - This round reinforced that a brief remembered act can qualify for the archive when the image is plain enough to travel by itself.
  - The best sign here was that the story does not explain kindness; it simply shows a strong, rough, socially lower hand becoming gentle without anyone watching.
- `AI-Parables.txt` intentionally left unchanged this round; the Dostoevsky image felt cleaner than any original I considered.


## 2026-03-07 — web round (Premchand: The Child / Basheer comparison)
- Referred to `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then pushed into a fresh South Asian lane rather than returning to the more exhausted Tolstoy / Franciscan / Hasidic / saint-legend paths.
- Kept and added to `Parables.txt`:
  - **The Child He Carried Home** — retold from Premchand’s **“Balak / The Child.”** What cleared the bar was the single plain reversal: a man is handed an obvious occasion for masculine shame and social bookkeeping, yet he simply takes the baby in his arms and calls the child God’s gift. The image is clean, portable, and free of reward-logic.
- Serious near-miss:
  - **Vaikom Muhammad Basheer, “Oru Manushyan / A Certain Man.”** Strong and memorable — a thief rescues the man he has just robbed, pays the bill, and returns the wallet in secret — but it finally depends more on irony and underworld charm than on the cleaner Jesus-grade plainness this archive seems to want.

## 2026-03-07 — web round (Bunin / Turgenev / Gimpel sweep)
- Referred to `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then deliberately avoided the already-mined Tolstoy / Franciscan / Hasidic / Chekhov / Premchand lanes and pushed into a fresh Russian-and-Yiddish cluster, with a small control sweep through public-domain compassion anthologies.

  - **Ivan Bunin, “Bast-Shoes / Red Bast-Shoes.”** The image is powerful: in a blizzard, a man pushes out to bring red bast shoes for a feverish child who has asked for them. But from the evidence trail available this round, the story still felt more like tragic peasant pathos than a perfectly clean, portable parable; I did not trust myself to retell it without firmer textual footing.
  - **Ivan Turgenev, “The Sparrow.”** Beautiful and memorable — a terrified old sparrow flings itself before a dog to protect the fallen chick. But it finally resolves as a prose-poem about instinctive love being stronger than death, not quite as an act of human mercy or a Jesus-grade social reversal.
  - **Isaac Bashevis Singer, “Gimpel the Fool.”** Deeply humane and full of forgiveness, but too extended, too novelistic in its humiliations, and too dependent on an accreting character-history rather than on one single transferable image.

## 2026-03-07 — web round (Sarah Orne Jewett / Henry Van Dyke comparison lane)
- Referred to `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then pushed into a fresh late-19th-century literary lane instead of returning to the more exhausted Tolstoy / Franciscan / Hasidic / Premchand / Chekhov paths.
- Kept and added to `Parables.txt`:
  - **The Last Peach in the Glass Dish** — retold from Sarah Orne Jewett’s **“The Town Poor.”** What cleared the bar was not the civic-critique frame, but the image of dispossessed sisters in a bare room making true hospitality out of cold tea, crackers, a little cheese, and the last preserved peach from their old home — and then still remembering the discouraged woman downstairs. That widening of care kept it from collapsing into mere pathos.
- Serious near-miss:
  - **Henry Van Dyke, _The Story of the Other Wise Man_.** Morally bright and structurally elegant, but in this archive it feels too explicitly Christ-parallel and reward-shaped; the mercy is real, but the design is too programmatic to feel like one of the rare, plain, portable parables.
- Process judgment:
  - `The Town Poor` nearly failed because of its long social setup, but the decisive image proved stronger than the frame: poverty offering hospitality without self-display, and mercy extending even toward the difficult host.
  - No addition to `AI-Parables.txt` this round; the Jewett image felt cleaner than anything original I considered.
- Logged newly visited URLs in `Already-Searched-URLs.txt`.

## 2026-03-07 — web round (O. Henry: The Last Leaf / Tagore comparison lane)
- Referred to `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then pushed into a fresh O. Henry / Tagore comparison lane rather than returning to the more exhausted Tolstoy / Franciscan / Hasidic / Russian-memory paths.
- Kept and added to `Parables.txt`:
  - **The Leaf That Stayed Through the Storm** — retold from O. Henry’s **“The Last Leaf.”** What cleared the bar was the plain hidden act: an old failed painter goes out in freezing rain and paints one final leaf where the real one has fallen, so that a despairing woman will keep living. The story is engineered, yes, but the image itself is clean enough to travel without the machinery.
- Serious near-misses / controls:
  - **Rabindranath Tagore, “Subha.”** Deep pity and unforgettable tenderness toward the mute girl and her animal friends, but too elegiac and fate-bound; it remains lament more than parable.
  - **Rabindranath Tagore, “The Postmaster.”** Beautiful in places, yet the central movement is abandonment, not mercy.
  - **Elizabeth Cooper, _Drusilla with a Million_ side-path.** Interesting old-age / usefulness material, but too novel-shaped to yield one portable image.
- Judgment: this round confirmed that hidden hope can qualify when it is embodied in one plain act instead of a speech. The decisive thing was not the old painter’s talk of a masterpiece, but the lantern, ladder, and colors in the storm.
- `AI-Parables.txt` intentionally left unchanged this round.
- Logged newly visited URLs in `Already-Searched-URLs.txt`.


## 2026-03-07 — web round (Tagore: Kabuliwala / Willa Cather: Neighbour Rosicky / Capote control lane)
- Referred to `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then pushed into a fresh transnational / plains-fiction lane rather than returning to the more exhausted Tolstoy / Franciscan / Russian-memory / O. Henry paths.
- **No addition to `Parables.txt` or `AI-Parables.txt` this round.**
- Serious near-misses:
  - **Rabindranath Tagore, “Kabuliwala.”** Deeply humane, especially at the end when Mini’s father recognizes the fruit seller as a father and helps send him home to his daughter. But the story finally leans more on remembered affection, paternal recognition, and elegiac loss than on one plain act of mercy that can travel by itself.
  - **Willa Cather, “Neighbour Rosicky.”** Full of goodness and neighborliness, and there are beautiful details of hospitality, patched clothing, and a man who wants his children spared the world’s cruelty. Yet it remains a richly textured life-portrait rather than a clean parable-image; its moral force is diffused across the whole character.
  - **Truman Capote, “A Christmas Memory.”** Lovely and generous in feeling, especially the cakes sent outward in delight, but too memory-soaked and self-enclosed for this archive’s hardest shelf.
- Judgment: this pass found real goodness, but not the rare compact image that feels both morally perfect and freely portable.

## 2026-03-07 — web round (Langston Hughes: Thank You, Ma'am / Ruskin Bond comparison lane)
- Referred to `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then pushed into a fresh mercy-after-wrong lane rather than returning to the more exhausted Tolstoy / Franciscan / Russian-memory / saint-legend paths.
- Kept and added to `Parables.txt`:
  - **The Purse She Left in Plain Sight** — retold from Langston Hughes’s **“Thank You, Ma'am.”** What cleared the bar was not chiefly the ten dollars, but the trust: the woman who has just been wronged leaves the purse visible, turns her back, feeds the boy, lets him wash, and sends him out by the door instead of through humiliation. The mercy arrives before repentance can prove itself.
- Serious near-miss:
  - **Ruskin Bond, “The Thief’s Story.”** Morally good and close in structure: a young thief is changed by a kind man’s trust and the offer of literacy. But the force is more interior and retrospective, whereas Hughes gives the cleaner image — the injured party answering theft with food, dignity, and unguarded trust.
- Judgment:
  - This round reinforced that one of the clearest Jesus-grade moves is to interrupt wrongdoing without denying personhood.
  - The decisive image was the purse left in plain sight while the boy still had every chance to run.
- `AI-Parables.txt` intentionally left unchanged this round; the Hughes story was cleaner than any original I considered.

## 2026-03-07 — web round (Katherine Mansfield: The Doll's House lane)
- Referred to `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then pushed into a fresh Mansfield lane rather than returning to the more exhausted Tolstoy / Franciscan / Russian-memory / saint-legend paths.
- Kept and added to `Parables.txt`:
  - **The Little Lamp in the Painted House** — retold from Katherine Mansfield’s **“The Doll’s House.”** What cleared the bar was the tiny, portable image: one child opens the forbidden little house to the shamed sisters, and the thing they carry away is not the richness of the rooms but the sight of the small lamp. The mercy is brief, almost powerless outwardly, yet inwardly real.
- Judgment:
  - This round reinforced that a Jesus-grade parable does not always need a rescue large enough to change the social order; sometimes it is enough that one person refuses, for a moment, to let the excluded remain wholly outside.
  - The decisive thing was that the younger sister remembers the lamp, not the insult.
- `AI-Parables.txt` intentionally left unchanged this round; nothing original I considered felt cleaner than the Mansfield image.
- Logged newly visited URLs in `Already-Searched-URLs.txt`.

## 2026-03-07 — web round (S.T. Semyonov: The Servant / Russian anthology comparison lane)
- Referred to `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then pushed into a fresh anthology / Russian-realism lane instead of returning to the more exhausted Hughes / Mansfield / O. Henry / Jewett / Premchand tracks.
- Kept and added to `Parables.txt`:
  - **The Bread He Would Not Take** — retold from S.T. Semyonov’s **“The Servant.”** What cleared the bar was the exactness of the moral turn: a hungry young man finally receives the job he desperately needs, overhears the old couple who will be cast out to make room for him, and quietly gives the bread back before he has even tasted it. The hiddenness of the act matters; there is no applause, only the cold street and the strange lightness that follows mercy.
- Near-misses / controls:
  - **Vsevolod Garshin, “The Signal.”** Strong self-sacrifice, but it tilts more toward civic heroism than the sort of neighbor-mercy image this archive most trusts.
  - **Maxim Gorky, “One Autumn Night.”** Rawly humane in places, but too unstable in moral texture to admit.
  - **Edmondo De Amicis / `Cuore` side-path.** Worth sampling as a control, but the emotional register felt too educationally shaped for this shelf.
- Judgment:
  - This one felt unusually clean because the man does not merely share bread; he refuses bread that would be taken from the mouths of the old.
  - No addition to `AI-Parables.txt` this round; nothing original I considered was cleaner than the Semyonov image.
- Logged newly visited URLs in `Already-Searched-URLs.txt`.



## 2026-03-07 — web round (Premchand duplicate check / Pearl S. Buck comparison lane)
- Referred to `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then pushed into a fresh Indian / wartime-mercy lane instead of revisiting the more exhausted Victorian, Russian, Franciscan, and anthology paths.
- **No addition to `Parables.txt` or `AI-Parables.txt` this round.**
- Important duplicate discovery:
  - **Premchand, “Idgah.”** A serious candidate at first pass because the image is so clean: a poor boy at the Eid fair passes by sweets and toys to buy iron tongs so his grandmother will stop burning her fingers. But on inspection the archive already contains this story as **“The Tongs from the Fair,”** so it could not be kept as a new addition.
- Serious near-miss:
  - **Pearl S. Buck, “The Enemy.”** Morally strong and genuinely worth testing. The best part is not the patriotic dilemma, but the plain sequence of acts: the doctor and his wife carry in a wounded enemy, wash him, operate, feed him, and finally give him a boat, food, quilts, and a way out to sea. Still, for this archive it finally felt too close to already-represented enemy-mercy material, and too dependent on war-duty framing rather than on one small, portable image that can stand almost anywhere.
- Judgment:
  - This pass mostly strengthened the shelf rather than enlarging it. `Idgah` proved the archive already held one of the best possible versions of that love-image, and `The Enemy` proved morally excellent without becoming rare enough to justify admission.

## 2026-03-07 — web round (Turgenev: *Two Rich Men* lane / prose-poems comparison)
- Referred to `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then pushed into a fresh Turgenev prose-poems lane rather than returning to the more exhausted Hughes / Mansfield / Premchand / Buck / saint-legend paths.
- Kept and added to `Parables.txt`:
  - **The Salt They Went Without** — retold from Ivan Turgenev’s **“Two Rich Men.”** What cleared the bar was the startling plainness of the image: a poor couple deciding to take in an orphaned niece though it means they may have no salt even for their bread. It is not spectacular charity, but shared diminishment for the sake of a child, which feels very close to the kind of moral weight this archive is trying to protect.
- Near-misses / controls:
  - **Turgenev, “Alms.”** Thoughtful, but too mediated by the stranger’s speech and vision-frame.
  - **Turgenev, “Cabbage Soup.”** Powerful on poverty and grief, but more social revelation than portable parable.
  - **Turgenev, “The Beggar.”** Very strong, but the archive already carries closely neighboring material around dignity-giving contact with a beggar.
- Judgment:
  - This one felt rare because it does not merely praise giving; it praises making one’s own life a little barer so another person can belong.
  - `AI-Parables.txt` intentionally left unchanged this round; nothing original I considered felt cleaner than the Turgenev image.
- Logged newly visited URLs in `Already-Searched-URLs.txt`.



2026-03-07 — round note
- **Keeper admitted:** Lucy Maud Montgomery, **“The Little Black Doll.”** What cleared the bar was not the celebrity singer or the pathos package, but the child carrying the dearest thing she owns in order to buy one hour of beauty for the dying woman who loved her. Portable image: *the doll in the box offered for one song.*
- **Retelling title used:** **“The Doll She Carried for One Song.”**
- **Near-miss reaffirmed:** Vsevolod Garshin, **“The Signal.”** Strong, memorable image (cut arm -> blood flag -> stopped train), but still lands more as civic-sacrificial heroism and dramatic confession than as a neighbor-mercy parable.
- **Cross-check lane:** Korolenko / anthology routes mostly widened context, not the shelf.

## 2026-03-07 — web round (Bolesław Prus / barrel-organ lane; compassion-through-annoyance test)
- Referred to `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then pushed into a fresh Prus / Polish-realism lane instead of returning to the more exhausted Tolstoy / Premchand / Hasidic / O. Henry / saint-legend shelves.
- Kept and added to `Parables.txt`:
  - **The Music He Had Paid to Silence** — retold from Bolesław Prus’s **“The Barrel Organ.”** What cleared the bar was the clean reversal: a man has literally paid to keep coarse street music away from his windows, then sees a blind child come alive at the sound and begins paying to let the music in. The image is small, portable, and morally exact: mercy dethrones refinement when another person’s joy depends on it.
- Near-misses / controls:
  - **Prus, “The Waistcoat.”** Beautiful mutual tenderness, but more elegiac than parable-shaped for this shelf.
  - **T. S. Arthur / anthology kindness pieces.** Decent sentiments, but too lesson-forward or juvenile.
  - **Breton / Irish / satire lanes.** Too magical, punitive, or frame-heavy in this pass.
- Judgment:
  - This keeper feels rare because it is not about grand sacrifice or public virtue; it is about surrendering one’s private standards of comfort so that a shut-in child may have a little world back.
  - `AI-Parables.txt` intentionally left unchanged this round; nothing original I considered felt cleaner than the Prus image.
- Logged newly surfaced / opened URLs in `Already-Searched-URLs.txt`.

## 2026-03-07 — web round (American Literature feel-good / children’s-mercy lane)
- Referred to `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then pushed into a fresh domestic-mercy / children’s-story lane rather than returning to the more exhausted Russian, Hasidic, Franciscan, Premchand, Hughes, and Mansfield paths.
- **No addition to `Parables.txt` or `AI-Parables.txt` this round.**
- Serious near-misses:
  - **Willa Cather, “A Burglar’s Christmas.”** Powerful image: a starving young man enters a rich house to steal, discovers it is his mother’s, and is met not with accusation but embrace. But for this archive it finally felt too adjacent to the Prodigal Son’s homecoming pattern; the grace is real, yet not independent enough.
  - **Laura E. Richards, “Little Benjamin.”** Strong, memorable turn: the father makes the household choose whether the foundling boy will be sent away or received as true brother with a full share. But the children’s-story register and explicit moral framing kept it from feeling rare enough for this shelf.
- Other checks / rejections:
  - **Harriet Beecher Stowe, “Christmas; or, The Good Fairy.”** Contains one lovely image (the orange saved for the lame child) and good counsel about gifts that actually relieve suffering, but it is finally more essay-dialogue than parable.
  - **Laura E. Richards, “The Great Feast.”** Gentle and imaginative, yet too whimsical / nursery-scaled for this collection’s harder moral standard.
  - **Mary E. Wilkins Freeman side-paths (“A Conquest of Humility,” `A Mistaken Charity` metadata trail) and Christmas-masquerade lane.** Worth checking, but nothing in this pass yielded a cleaner mercy-image than what is already in the archive.
- Judgment:
  - This round strengthened the negative boundary more than the collection itself. The best material had actual tenderness, but either leaned too close to already-canonical Gospel shapes or announced its lesson too plainly.

## Mar 7, 2026 — web round (Coppee / Sadi / kindness lane)
- Referred to `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then searched in a new direction: wisdom-tale / old-anthology material rather than the more exhausted children’s-story and commentary lanes.
- **Added to `Parables.txt`:** François Coppée, **“A Piece of Bread,”** retold as **“The Bread He Had Thrown Away.”**
- Why it cleared the bar:
  - The governing image is plain and memorable: bread thrown into mud, then rescued and eaten by the hungry.
  - The moral reversal is clean and unsentimental: the man with abundance learns reverence from the man with need.
  - It avoids reward-logic; the poor soldier’s goodness does not buy comfort, but reveals the holiness of what the rich man had despised.
  - The closing memory—lifting a crust from the street years later—makes the lesson portable in a very parable-like way.
- Near-miss / useful boundary:
  - **Sa'di, _Bustan_, rebuke to King Tukla.** Strong sentence-level wisdom (“religion consists alone in the service of the people”), but finally more admonition than story. Good for boundary-marking, not for admission.
- `AI-Parables.txt` left unchanged this round.
- Logged newly surfaced / opened URLs in `Already-Searched-URLs.txt`.


## Mar 7, 2026 — web round (anthology / Gibran / dignity / Rattrap boundary)

## 2026-03-07 — web round (sacred-texts shame/mercy lane)
- Kept and added to Parables.txt: "The Host Who Did Not Call Him Back" from Rumi's *Masnavi* / "The Prophet and his Infidel Guest". Why it cleared the bar: the center is not triumph, cleverness, or reward, but a host refusing to re-expose a guest already swallowed by shame, then stooping to clean the stain himself. That image feels portable, morally exact, and deeply compatible with Jesus' heart.
- Near-misses / searched but not kept: *The Little Clay Cart* (strong dignity-of-the-poor language, but too embedded in a larger dramatic frame), Tibetan birth-story calf-compassion snippets (beautiful, but not yet a clean human-neighbor parable), Paltith feeding the poor man in *Jasher* (powerful, but more martyr-legend than parable), mother-monkey Jataka / similar filial-mercy material (moving, but not the right shelf this round).
- Process note: I accidentally revisited a previously logged sacred-texts Masnavi URL while pushing a new shame/mercy lane; despite that repetition, it yielded a keeper substantial enough to justify the pass.

## 2026-03-07 — search pass (anthology lanes / Bhai Kanhaiya)
- Kept and added to Parables.txt: “The Water Skin Among the Wounded,” from the Bhai Kanhaiya battlefield tradition. What cleared the bar was the complaint itself (“you are helping the enemy”) answered not with cleverness but with plain sight: he saw only thirsty human beings, and the teacher deepened the act by putting balm in his hands.
- Searched hard through older anthology / school-reader / public-domain lanes and found many humane scenes, but most were either too lesson-built, too diffuse, or too close to patterns already on the shelf.
- Near-miss / boundary checks this round: Grimm’s “The Grave-Mound,” Hawthorne’s Baucis-and-Philemon material, a few Lucy Maud Montgomery lanes, and assorted Buddhist / sacred-texts compassion passages. Goodness present, but not the same compact, portable force.

## 2026-03-07 — web round (anti-shame / restitution / dignity-institutions lane)
- Referred to `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then searched fresh lanes around anti-shame charity customs, restitution-without-publicity, and older story collections not centered in the already-mined Sufi / Sikh / bhakti shelves.
- **No addition to `Parables.txt` this round.**

Morally serious and memorable, but it still felt slightly too mediated / socially specific, and the marriage-as-reparation ending kept it from feeling like a clean Jesus-grade keeper for this shelf.

These are morally excellent, and very close to the archive’s heart, but in this pass they read more like legal-memory / ethical teaching than portable parable.

## 2026-03-07 — web round (repentance-without-exposure / stolen-beam boundary pass)
- Referred to `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then pushed into a narrower repentance-without-exposure lane rather than widening the already-mined saint / battlefield / school-reader shelves.
- **No addition to `Parables.txt` this round.**
- Strongest outside candidate checked: Chabad’s **“The Stolen Ring and the Surprised Thief.”** A ring is stolen from the rabbi’s house; instead of sending pursuit for accusation, the rabbi sends a message that the thief may keep the ring and should not undersell it; the thief is disarmed and turns back; the resolution redirects the value toward poor brides. Morally alive and beautiful, but I did not keep it because it feels too close to several existing archive shapes at once — Gelasius / protected-thief mercy, the bishop-and-candlesticks register, and anti-shame restitution stories already represented in the scratch boundary map.
- Secondary boundary marker: the **stolen beam** / *takanat ha-shavim* material (Sefaria / OU / Torah.org). Important and piercing as moral-legal imagination — make return possible, do not trap the penitent in despair — but it reads as ordinance and image rather than as a fully portable parable.
- Process judgment: this pass sharpened the shelf more than it enlarged it. The heart of the archive still seems to prefer one plain act over a clever moral reversal, and to prefer image over jurisprudence.
- **No addition to `AI-Parables.txt` this round.** Nothing original I considered felt cleaner than an honest empty turn.

## 2026-03-07 — web round (John the Almsgiver / blanket pass)
- Referred to `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then searched fresh lanes around John the Almsgiver, Peter the Tax Collector, and Serapion instead of returning to the already-mined Sikh / bhakti / Masnavi / Gelasius shelves.
- **Kept and added to `Parables.txt`: “The Blanket That Would Not Stay on His Bed.”** Source lane: the Life of John the Almsgiver. What cleared the bar was the simple transfer of comfort into shared warmth: one costly blanket becomes many rough blankets, and the story keeps its force without needing miracle, reward, or doctrinal scaffolding.
- Main near-misses this round: **Peter the Tax Collector** (powerful but too openly judgment-scale / conversion-legend shaped), **Serapion selling or giving away even his Gospel / clothes** (striking, but too ascetic-saying-like and not enough of a full parable movement), and John’s brief episodes of the **abusive beggar** and the **impostor whose loss he would not recover** (morally exact, but too short or too close to existing anti-shame / ask-not-again patterns).
- Judgment note: the blanket story felt admissible because it is not mainly about heroic deprivation; it is about letting the cold of the many interrupt the comfort of the one, which feels close to Jesus’ economic reversals without copying any one Gospel parable directly.
- No addition to `AI-Parables.txt` this round.

## 2026-03-07 — web round (Philaret / ashamed-poor / shepherd-hut lane)
- Referred to `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then searched fresh lanes around Philaret the Merciful, Basil of Moscow’s anti-shame mercy, and the poor-shepherd / hidden-host lane instead of returning to already-mined Sikh / bhakti / Hasidic / Gelasius shelves.
- **Kept and added to `Parables.txt`: “The Yoke He Would Not Divide.”** Source lane: Philaret the Merciful. What cleared the bar was the second movement: giving one ox is generous, but when the neighbor points out that a half-yoke is still a burden, Philaret gives the other too. The story survives without miracle or reward if retold that way.
- Strong near-misses / not kept: **Basil of Moscow and the foreign merchant too ashamed to beg in fine clothing** (morally exact, but too brief and skeletal to retell cleanly without inventing connective tissue), **Basil / the merchant rocking the empty cradle for his wronged mother** (piercing, but more prophetic rebuke than parable of kindness), and **“John the Blessed”** (beautiful poor-hut hospitality, but too visionary / feast-legend shaped for this shelf).
- Judgment note: Philaret’s oxen story felt admissible because it exposes the insufficiency of partial mercy. It is not “give what you can spare”; it is “if the gift still leaves your neighbor unable to stand, look again.”
- No addition to `AI-Parables.txt` this round.

## 2026-03-07 — web round (Macarius / The Thief's Story / silent-restoration lane)
- Referred to `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then searched fresh lanes around Desert Fathers theft-mercy stories and modern short fiction where mercy protects repentance without public confrontation.
- **Kept and added to `Parables.txt`: “The Wet Note in the Morning.”** Source lane: Ruskin Bond’s *The Thief’s Story*. What cleared the bar was the ending image: the stolen money returned in secret, the single note still wet from the rain, and the wronged man choosing not to expose the thief but to reopen his future by saying, in effect, “We begin lessons today.”
- Strong near-misses / not kept: **Macarius helping the thief load the donkey**, **the elder calling thieves back to take the forgotten bundle of money**, and **the elder urging the thieves to hurry before the brothers stop him from keeping Christ’s command**. All three are piercing and beautiful, but this round they felt too skeletal and too close to the already-kept Gelasius / anti-reclaiming / radical-nonpossession shelf to warrant a new main-shelf retelling.
- Secondary broad searches in teacher / shame / kindness / hospitality lanes produced mostly noise, school-note mirrors, or texts too explicitly lesson-built to survive the archive’s hardest filter.
- **No addition to `AI-Parables.txt` this round.**
- Judgment note: the Bond story worked because the mercy is neither denial nor indulgence. The theft is real, the knowledge is real, and the forgiveness takes the form of giving the thief a path forward before shame can harden him again.

## 2026-03-07 — web round (Camillus / saint-hagiography mercy-under-misuse lane)
- Referred to `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then pushed into a fresh saint-hagiography lane centered on mercy that survives waste, relapse, or misuse, rather than returning to the more exhausted thief / school-reader / Tolstoy shelves.
- **Kept and added to `Parables.txt`: “The Clothes He Gave Again.”** Source lane: St. Camillus de Lellis. What cleared the bar was the exact shape of the act: he does not deny that the gift was squandered, but he refuses to let failure cancel need. He reclothes the poor men as if nothing had happened, not because misuse is good, but because pity that cannot survive misuse is still too small.
- Comparative / near-miss checks in the same EWTN lane: **John of God** and **Francis of Girolamo** surfaced real mercy, but in this pass they felt either too miracle-marked, too biographical, or too thinly anecdotal to survive retelling as a rare main-shelf parable.

## 2026-03-07 — web round (anti-shame charity / Berditchev / school-reader lane)
- Referred to `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then pushed into a fresh anti-shame charity / institutional-dignity lane alongside one older school-reader anthology lane, instead of returning to the already-exhausted thief-mercy / battlefield / bhakti shelves.
- **No addition to `Parables.txt` this round.**
- Strongest near-miss: Chabad’s **“The Secret Benefactor.”** The second embedded story is the one that came closest: a merchant arrives in town, discovers his borrowed restocking money is gone, begins wailing in the street, and a wealthy man publicly claims he had “found” a matching sum and restores the amount from his own safe. The thief, seeing the victim’s shame covered and the loss borne by another, is pierced and tries to return the stolen money. Important for orientation, especially the emphasis on anonymity and on restoring self-sufficiency rather than merely relieving want, but it is ethical scaffolding rather than a portable story. ## 2026-03-07 — web round (Thai beggars / poor as givers)
- Referred to `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then worked a fresh lane around poor-as-giver reversals instead of the more exhausted thief-mercy and saint-charity shelves.
- **Kept and added to `Parables.txt`: “The Rice Bag in the Full Bowl.”** Source lane: Anchan’s “The Beggars.”
- What cleared the bar was the exact moral reversal: the religious man’s bowl is already full, the beggars’ bowl is empty, and yet they are the true givers in the scene. He mistakes blessing for benefaction; they know they have offered something from want. That feels close to Jesus’ way of exposing rank and honoring the poor without sentimentality.
- Main hesitation before keeping: the story stands near the widow’s-mite / false-benefactor shelf, so I checked carefully for duplication-by-structure. I kept it because the image is distinct: not a rich crowd overlooking a poor donor, but a pious receiver failing to see that he is receiving from those he pities.
- **No addition to `AI-Parables.txt` this round.** The found story was cleaner than anything original I considered.



## 2026-03-07 — web round (school-reader hidden-service / anthology boundary pass)
- Referred to `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then pushed into a fresh school-reader / hidden-service / anti-shame lane instead of returning to the more exhausted saint, Hasidic, Chekhov, and Russian-literary shelves.
- **No addition to `Parables.txt` this round.**
- Strongest near-miss: **“Jack’s Queer Ways”** from *Stories Worth Rereading*. A boy vanished every winter afternoon, and his family feared some hidden mischief; later his father found an abandoned old woman alive beside a bright stove and a neat pile of wood, and learned the boy had been chopping and carrying her fuel all season without telling anyone. Morally, it is very clean — secret service, no self-advertisement, care for someone who could not repay — but it still felt too much like a school-reader virtue piece and not enough like the rare, portable parable image this archive wants.
- Secondary near-miss: the **little sister who covered her brother’s public failure at graduation** by feeding him his forgotten first line and asking the president to let him try again. Humane, shame-covering, and memorable, but finally too occasion-specific and too sentimental to stand on the main shelf as a parable.
- Small control sweep: **The Ontario High School Reader** and **The Unique Story Book** were useful only as boundary checks; nothing there felt fresher or cleaner than the near-miss above.
- **No addition to `AI-Parables.txt` this round.** Nothing original I considered felt cleaner than an honest empty turn.

## 2026-03-07 — web round (hidden bread / quiet mercy)
Kept and added to Parables.txt:
- “The Bread Beneath Their Heads,” adapted from Anton Chekhov’s “A Day in the Country.”
Why it cleared the bar:
- The act is small and unspectacular.
- The giver is poor and socially negligible, not a hero by status.
- The kindness protects the children without making them perform gratitude.
- “Only the moon sees” gives it true hiddenness rather than moral showmanship.
Rejected / not added:
- Karl Josef Friedrich’s “Rachoff”: several radiant episodes, but too biographical / explicitly devotional for this shelf’s parable cadence.
- “The Flaming Heart of Danko”: potent sacrifice image, but too mythic-heroic and less neighbor-facing.
- “The Beggar and the Baker’s Daughter”: transformation is driven by magical punishment/reward logic; too fairy-tale punitive.
- Jewish folklore scan (“The Poor Man’s Miracle,” “Where Is Paradise?” etc.): interesting, but this pass found nothing cleaner than Chekhov’s hidden loaf.
Note to future passes:
- Quiet literary scenes may keep outperforming explicit moral tales.
- Hidden acts toward children, the ashamed, or the socially invisible remain a promising lane.

## 2026-03-07 — web round (Todd's Asylum / dignity-at-the-table lane)
- Referred to `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then pushed into a fresh lane of old anthologies and quieter literary pieces where abundance turns outward without humiliating the receiver.
- **Kept and added to `Parables.txt`: “The Guests We Were Waiting For.”** Source lane: Winthrop Packard’s **“Thanksgiving at Todd’s Asylum”** in *Good Cheer Stories Every Child Should Know*.
- What cleared the bar was not the holiday sentiment but the final movement: a hungry couple arrives trying not to be mistaken for beggars, and the host protects their dignity by receiving them not as applicants but as expected guests. That feels close to Jesus’ way of seating the poor without making them perform abasement.
- Secondary near-misses: **“Bread Cast Upon the Waters”** (beautiful village hospitality, but more social sketch than portable parable), **“King Alfred and the Beggar”** (still too reward-shaped / miracle-shaped), and the **Yashka / Andersen boundary checks** (useful only as lane control).
- **No addition to `AI-Parables.txt` this round.** The found story was cleaner than anything original I considered.


## 2026-03-07 — web round (quiet anthologies / White Lily near-miss)
- No new keeper added this round.
- Strongest near-miss: “The White Lily” (Plough / Jane Tyson Clement, adapted from Frances Jenkins Olcott). Beautiful tenderness, but the transforming flower felt too enchanted/programmatic; mercy arrives through a symbol that does too much of the moral work.
- Other lanes checked and declined: Frances Browne’s “Childe Charity” / “The Silver Tracks” in *The Laughing Prince* (hospitality stories, but too reward-punishment / fairy-tale mechanical); Vachel Lindsay’s *A Handy Guide for Beggars* (interesting hospitality prose, but not clean parable form); scattered Project Gutenberg anthologies and American Literature pages around poor-man / beggar / orphan / bread motifs.
- Heuristic reaffirmed: keep rejecting stories where goodness is cashed out by magical payout, humiliation, or heavy allegorical machinery. Keep looking for ordinary acts that preserve another person’s dignity without spectacle.

## 2026-03-07 — web round (carpenter / kingly-gift / anthology comparison lane)
- Referred to `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then pushed into a fresh literary / anthology / Plough comparison lane instead of returning to the more exhausted Tolstoy / Hasidic / thief-mercy / school-reader shelves.
- **Kept and added to `Parables.txt`: “The Prayer Made of Nails.”** Source lane: Peter Rosegger’s **“The Carpenter’s Christmas.”** What cleared the bar was the plain reversal: while others guard silence, mood, and ritual exactness, the carpenter goes out into the storm and makes shelter for a widow and her children. The priest’s line about hammering an *Our Father* into the roof is a little polished, but the core image itself feels strong enough to travel.

- Secondary boundary check: Lesskov’s **“The Guest.”** Powerful forgiveness shape, but too miracle-marked and too adjacent to the archive’s existing enemy-love / Christmas-visitor cluster.
