## 2026-03-16 — web round (bread-came-back-after-the-war / mercy-crossing-enmity lane)
Kept and added to Parables.txt:
- **The Bread That Came Back After the War** — retold from Brenda Arnold’s *My Unsung Hero* account. As a stranded American teenager in Germany, she was taken in and fed by a woman whose husband had once been a prisoner of war in the United States and had been treated kindly there.

Why it cleared the bar:
- The moral turn is unusually clean: mercy shown to an enemy does not end with that enemy, but ripens years later into hospitality for a stranger.
- It feels Jesus-grade because it answers history’s logic with kingdom logic; the couple does not repay nation to nation, but remembers received mercy and lets it govern the next vulnerable encounter.
- Distinctive lane for the archive: kindness crossing enmity and returning across generations.
- Strong concrete image set: the locked sister’s door, the suitcase on the sidewalk, the kitchen table, the sandwich and cake, and the old war hidden inside the present meal.

Considered seriously but did NOT keep:
- **Bill Price / invited into the group** — morally clean and formative, but too slight beside the archive’s strongest inclusion stories.
- **Trieste Belmont / “Don’t jump”** — powerful and life-saving, but the action stayed too minimal to bear a full parable retelling this round.
- **Mike Huddleston / stranger asked how to help** — excellent and exact, but too adjacent to the archive’s existing ask-before-you-heal lane.
- **Susan Greenstein Prescott / “I like listening to your voice”** — tender and true, but still closer to encouragement than to a sharper kingdom turn for this shelf.
- **Laura Holmes Haddad / X-ray tech saying “beautiful”** and **Sri Pisharody / rickshaw drivers and police helping** remained strong, but neither felt as fresh in contour as the war-ripple story.

## 2026-03-16 — web round (patient-the-student-could-not-bear / mercy-from-the-dismissed lane)
Kept and added to Parables.txt:
- **The Patient the Student Could Not Bear** — retold from Mark Metersky’s *My Unsung Hero* account. As a medical student, he disliked a manipulative patient with a heroin addiction; then at 3 a.m. he found that same patient cradling the head of a dying roommate and wiping his brow while the doctors had failed to comfort him well.

Why it cleared the bar:
- The moral turn is unusually sharp: the judged burden becomes the true comforter, while the competent and respectable are shown wanting.
- It feels Jesus-grade because it exposes the sin of moral sorting without speechifying; the revelation comes in an image, not an argument.
- Distinctive lane for the archive: compassion rising from the person already dismissed as difficult, manipulative, or unworthy of trust.
- Strong concrete image set: the night ward, the failing pain control, the delirious dying man, the lap, the towel, and the student’s shame.

Considered seriously but did NOT keep:
- **Virginia Squier / man in black leather on the train** — morally clean and memorable, but finally too adjacent to the archive’s existing appearance-judgment lane.
- **Kimberly Godsey / physician preparing a patient before hard truth** — strong and exact, but it felt more like wise pastoral practice than a fuller parable-turn this round.
- **Claire Burnside Och / customer drinking cork-speckled wine** — graceful and instructive, but too slight beside the stronger inversions.
- **Karleen / Hanukkah invitation after abuse** — deeply humane, but too near the archive’s stranger-fed-at-table and refuge-offered lanes.
- **Lorrie Paul / hand on the shoulder in the hallway** — powerful presence, but too close to the collection’s existing grief-attended / ministry-of-presence shapes.

## 2026-03-16 — web round (wrong-number / mercy-as-interruption lane)
Kept and added to Parables.txt:
- **The Man Who Answered the Wrong Number** — retold from Auburn Sandstrom’s *The Moth* story “A Phone Call.” In the middle of the night, deep in withdrawal and fear, she dialed what she thought was a Christian counselor; instead, a stranger who had merely answered a wrong number stayed awake with her until dawn, listening without judgment.

Why it cleared the bar:
- The moral turn is unusually clean: mercy arrives not through role, office, or preparation, but through interruption freely accepted.
- It feels Jesus-grade because the man does not first ask whether this suffering belongs to him; he makes himself neighbor simply by remaining present.
- Distinctive lane for the archive: compassion given by accident, yet received as providence; a misdial becoming a lifeline.
- Strong concrete image set: the filthy floor, the sleeping child, the radio clicking off, the unnamed man in bed becoming fully present, and the wrong number revealed only after dawn.

Considered seriously but did NOT keep:
- **Stacia / “Where do you want to go?”** — morally excellent, but already present in the archive as **The Woman Who Asked Only Where**.
- **Silvana Clark / professor paying tuition under the name of a scholarship** — already present in the archive as **The Teacher Who Called It a Scholarship**.
- **Kate Elsley / lone anti-Prop 8 sign-holder on the platform** — clean and moving, but too slender in action to displace stronger public-solidarity entries.
- **Mary Klein / strangers returning with Christmas dinner** — generous and memorable, but too adjacent to existing widened-table / feast-for-the-lonely entries.
- **Sue Norton / Clemency** — profoundly serious, but finally too near the archive’s already-strong killer-forgiven / enemy-made-kin lane.
- **Jolena Rothweil / pay it forward note** and **Eric Schwartz / silent bear hug** remained good, but did not open a contour rare enough for this shelf once overlap and distinctness were weighed.

## 2026-03-16 — web round (woman-met-his-eyes / small-mercy-at-the-edge lane)
Kept and added to Parables.txt:
- **The Woman Who Met His Eyes** — retold from Ken Wilcox’s *My Unsung Hero* account. On a bitter day in Washington, D.C., after pressure from work and the mounting deaths of friends from AIDS had driven him toward suicidal thinking, a stranger walking toward him deliberately met his eyes and gave him a beautiful smile just after a bus had brushed his coat on the curb.

Why it cleared the bar:
- The action is vanishingly small outwardly, yet morally exact: she does not solve his life, but she interrupts the lie that he is unseen and discardable.
- It feels Jesus-grade because the mercy is unassigned, unspectacular, and proportionally absurd — a smile against death, and still enough.
- Distinctive lane for the archive: not advice, not rescue logistics, not prolonged care, but the saving force of being seen at the brink.
- Strong concrete image set: the winter street, the tyrant boss, the bus brushing the coat, the stranger seeking his eyes, the smile, the step back from the curb.

Considered seriously but did NOT keep:
- **Shalom Auslander / forbidden gum from his grandmother** — vivid and memorable, but after re-checking the moral center it still felt too dependent on furtive anti-rule defiance rather than a cleaner act of mercy.
- **Nancy Pardo / Dr. Okere’s “Go live your life”** — wise and genuinely nourishing, but more counsel than parable scene.
- **Nick Epley / restaurant owner near the hospital** and several other Hidden Brain archive leads remained possible, but the public detail I could reach this round was too thin for a high-confidence retelling.
- **Lilah Clevey / the other driver after the crash** briefly resurfaced in search, but it had already been searched and previously set aside for overlap with stronger accident-forgiveness material in the archive.

## 2026-03-16 — web round (bearers-who-asked-what-music-she-loved / mercy-without-interrogation lane)
Kept and added to Parables.txt:
- **The Bearers Who Asked What Music She Loved** — retold from Clarice’s *My Unsung Hero* account on GPB/NPR. After many staff repeatedly made her relive a mental-health crisis, two EMTs carried her to the psychiatric ward, asked what music she liked, played it, and did not make her explain herself again.

Why it cleared the bar:
- The moral turn is unusually exact: mercy appears not as more analysis, advice, or cure, but as refusing to make the wounded unwrap themselves one more time.
- It feels Jesus-grade because the EMTs do not demand a performance of pain to justify tenderness; they make a little room where tears can happen without defense.
- Distinctive lane for the archive: compassion by restraint; relief given through not interrogating, not judging, and not turning a suffering person into a lesson.
- Strong concrete image set: the stretcher, the girl’s shame about her dirty feet, the small ambulance, the chosen music, and the silence in which she can finally cry.

Considered seriously but did NOT keep:
- **Jean Muenchrath / stranger and family at the trailhead** — real courage and tenderness, but it stayed in the rescue-from-death lane rather than opening a rarer moral contour.
- **Elaine Batchelor / two older men guarding her on the night train** — morally clean and memorable, but finally too close to the archive’s stranger-protection entries.
- **Ellen Wyoming DeLoy / train conductor ringing the bell** — alert, good, and concrete, but the action felt too slender beside stronger protection stories already kept.
- **Samantha Hodge-Williams / anesthesiologist singing before surgery** — beautiful bedside mercy, yet still adjacent to existing fear-soothed / presence-in-procedure material.
- **Barbara Alvarez / hall minister helping the newly widowed mother move in** — deserving and humane, but not distinct enough in contour for this shelf.
- **Lavonne Schaafsma / women recovering the purse** — admirable public courage, but more civic decency than parable-clean revelation.
- **Anna Schuleit Haber / The Magnificat** and **Michael Fischer / The Best Medicine** were searched as fresh lanes but not kept this round.

## 2026-03-17 — search notes (Saadi / substitution-mercy lane)
Kept and added to Parables.txt:
- “The Man Who Became the Bond” — retold from Saadi, *The Bustan*, “Story Illustrative of Practical Charity”: man cannot pay a prisoner’s debt, so he becomes the surety, releases the prisoner, and is jailed in his place. This one cleared the shelf because the turn is portable and clean: not mere generosity, but substitutional mercy.

- Saadi, “Abdul Aziz and the Pearl” — admirable, but the moral turn felt closer to kingly philanthropy than a mouth-carried parable.

- The shelf is strongest when the story keeps its force after biography is stripped away.
- Substitution, dignity, and neighbor-love under cost remain especially promising lanes.
- Better one severe keeper than three admirable near-misses.

Kept:

- It feels Jesus-grade without copying a Gospel parable: the wounded enemy becomes neighbor, and mercy is told to go farther than instinct allows.


## 2026-03-17 — web round (desert fathers / non-judgment / shared-exile lane)
Kept / added to Parables.txt:
- “The Elder Who Left with the Cast-Out” — retold from the Abba Bessarion saying. This cleared the shelf because it does more than warn against judgment: it shares the expelled person’s place. The moral turn survives shortening and feels Jesus-grade in a distinct way from the existing shelf; holiness is tested by whether it will cross the room and stand beside the shamed.

Considered but did NOT keep:

- Abba Isaac / angel at the cell door — morally serious, but more visionary admonition than mouth-carried parable.
- Various modern reflection pages — useful corroboration, but not primary enough to govern shelf judgment.

Why this one stayed:
- The image survives retelling with almost no biography required: a sinner, a door, an elder who stands up and leaves with him.
- It adds a fresh shelf lane: solidarity with the disgraced, not merely mercy to the poor or enemy-love in crisis.
- It sharpens the Jesus-grade standard rather than diluting it: do not guard your purity by abandoning the person already bowed under shame.

No new AI parable added this round.


## 2026-03-18 — web round (forgotten grave / dignity after disgrace lane)
- Referred to `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then pushed into a fresher lane around posthumous mercy, abandoned graves, and hidden dignity rather than the archive’s already-thick enemy-mercy / cloak / beggar shelves.
- Kept and added to Parables.txt: **“The Grave Behind the Poplars,”** retold from Lucy Maud Montgomery’s **“Freda’s Adopted Grave.”** What cleared the bar was the moral shape: a child with no grave of her own chooses the disgraced man’s neglected one, tends it in secret, and so restores both the forgotten dead and the living sister who feared shame would be the last word. It feels unusually clean: mercy shown where there is no advantage, no audience, and not even any possibility of repayment from the one receiving it.
- Considered but did **not** keep: George Gissing’s **“A Poor Gentleman”** (truth-telling and shame, but its force is more confession/exposure than parable image), and the Chekhov/Leskov gardener trace (still too secondhand to trust for a clean retelling this round).

## 2026-03-18 — Web search pass

- “The Bread-Woman and the Orphans” (Margaret Haughery / New Orleans tradition + public-history trail): strongest line was the refusal to sort children by creed or race — “They are all orphans alike.” Retold more tightly as bread-without-boundaries.

Considered but did NOT keep:
- L. M. Montgomery, “A Golden Wedding” — warm and beautiful gratitude, but finally more repayment than the freer, more unmerited mercy we want.
- Michael Wood, “The Willow Weaver” — interesting mystical imagery, but too visionary / diffuse for this shelf.

## 2026-03-18 — web round (late-funeral / living-mercy lane)
Kept / added to Parables.txt:
- “The Girl They Forgave at Her Funeral” — retold from the Cissy Gay / Valancy arc in L. M. Montgomery’s *The Blue Castle*. This cleared the shelf because it sharpens into one severe image: the one who crosses the threshold while shame is still contagious, contrasted with the respectable many who become generous only when the coffin has made generosity cheap. Distinct enough from the grave / cast-out / beggar lanes because the test is not whether the village will bury the disgraced, but whether anyone will sit with her before burial.

Considered but did NOT keep:
- Elizabeth Gaskell, *Lizzie Leigh* — morally serious and full of pity, but more redemption-novella / family-recovery than mouth-carried parable.
- *The Poor Clare* — strong atmosphere, wrong lane for the archive this round.
- Father Damien / leper-service materials — honorable witness, but the pages I reached were biography and exhortation rather than a compact parable-turn.

Why this one stayed:
- The image survives shortening: a threshold, a dying woman, the solitary watcher, then the late-arriving pallbearers.
- It carries a Jesus-grade rebuke without copying a Gospel scene: mercy is measured by whether it arrives while shame still costs something.
- It adds a fresh edge to the shelf’s dignity-with-the-disgraced material by opposing living companionship to posthumous respectability.

No new AI parable added this round.

## 2026-03-18 — web round (fresh public-domain mercy / dignity lane; no keeper)
- Referred to `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then stayed mostly in fresh public-domain literature rather than returning to the more exhausted saint / beggar / StoryCorps lanes.
- Strongest fresh near-miss: **Francois Coppée, "A Piece of Bread"** (from *International Short Stories*). Real moral force: a pampered soldier learns hunger by meeting a foundling who has been hungry all his life, shares his ration, and later discovers the man died taking his night watch. The closing image of the duke lifting bread from the mud in memory of him is memorable, but it still felt more like grief-debt / remembrance than the cleaner Jesus-grade mercy-image this archive tends to keep.

## 2026-03-18 — web round (children-reversing-shame / living-pardon lane)
Kept / added to Parables.txt:
- “The Children at Marie's Window” — retold from Prince Myshkin’s Marie story in Dostoevsky’s *The Idiot*. This cleared the shelf because the mercy does not stop at private pity: one person contradicts the village’s contempt, then teaches children to see the disgraced woman as still human, and the children carry that mercy farther than the respectable adults. The strongest image is the outcast hearing daily love at her window and going to her grave under flowers from the same children who once threw dirt.

Considered but did NOT keep:
- *Kabuliwala* — tender and memorable, but finally more father-loss / recognition than a clean parable of mercy.
- *The Peasant Marey* — morally beautiful, but more memoir-flash of tenderness than mouth-carried parable.

Why this one stayed:
- Distinct shelf lane: not merely helping the shamed person privately, but undoing communal contempt by teaching others—especially children—how to honor the one everyone has named unclean.
- It resists neat repayment; Marie does not recover or triumph socially, but she does die restored to human fellowship.
- It feels Jesus-grade without borrowing a Gospel frame directly: mercy touches the contaminated person, then multiplies through those least invested in respectability.

No new AI parable added this round.



## 2026-03-18 — web round (false oath / mercy-toward-the-wrongdoer lane)
Kept / added to Parables.txt:
- “The Woman Who Took Back the Bible” — retold from the courtroom opening of Selma Lagerlöf’s *The Girl from the Marsh Croft*. What cleared the shelf was the exact turn: a poor woman seeking child-support realizes that the married man who fathered her child is actually going to swear falsely to escape responsibility, and she would rather lose her case than let him damn himself with perjury. The image is compact, memorable, and morally severe in a way that feels unusually Jesus-grade.

Considered but did NOT keep:
- Selma Lagerlöf, *The Outcast* — opened as a nearby lane, but not enough of a compact mercy-image surfaced in this pass to justify keeping.
- T. S. Arthur, *The Power of Kindness and Other Stories* — likely too explicitly improving / didactic for this shelf; the title and framing already felt more tract-like than parable-clean.

Why this one stayed:
- Distinct shelf lane: not merely pity for the disgraced, but mercy toward the very wrongdoer who is about to preserve himself by another sin.
- The story survives radical shortening without losing force: accusation, oath, Bible, withdrawal.
- It resists sentimental repayment; the moral beauty is concentrated in the refusal to win at the price of another person’s corruption.

No new AI parable added this round.

  Why it cleared: not merely pity, but a public contradiction of inherited contempt; a child under shame is given a name and place before his history is cleaned up.

## 2026-03-18 — web round (substitute / prison-for-friend lane)
Kept / added to Parables.txt:
- François Coppée, *The Substitute* → retold as “The Mason Who Took the Handcuffs.”
  Why it cleared: the mercy is not generic pity, nor reward-shaped benevolence; it is a man who has only just begun to live honestly recognizing the exact point where another life will start to be ruined, and placing himself there instead. The strongest image is not sentiment but substitution: he hears the stolen coins discovered, understands the whole future at once, whispers for silence, and offers his own wrists.

Considered but did NOT keep:
- Maupassant, *Moonlight* — beautiful and unexpectedly generous, but more a revelation about eros and God's night than a compact parable of costly kindness.
- Coppée, *The Sabots of Little Wolff* — likely tender and clean, but the available framing marked it as a Christmas-legend / child-purity tale, which felt likelier to overlap older innocence lanes than to open a new shelf-space.

Why this one stayed:
- Distinct shelf lane: mercy by someone formerly ruined, protecting another person not merely from punishment but from beginning the long inward descent he knows firsthand.
- It is morally severe without being preachy; the friend is not noble, the rescuer is not respectable, and the act is both lucid and costly.
- It feels Jesus-grade in a fresh way because it is not just “taking blame”; it is a redeemed man using his knowledge of darkness to keep another from entering it.

No new AI parable added this round.


2026-03-18 — kept: Bret Harte, “Miggles.”
Why kept: not merely pity for the shamed, but a disgraced woman choosing long fidelity to a helpless man without taking the moral cover of duty or marriage; the key line is her refusal to bind mercy by contract.
Retelling angle: emphasize free mercy, hidden dignity, and the reversal where the judged woman stands above the respectable.
Near-misses this pass: T. S. Arthur, “An Angel in Disguise” (too reward-shaped / sentimental); Bret Harte, “The Idyl of Red Gulch” (strong pity and shame dynamics, but more custody/rescue than the cleaner free-mercy image); “The Luck of Roaring Camp” (moral regeneration through innocence, but more communal sentiment than direct mercy).
Other fresh lanes opened: Jewett/Lee/Sleight anthology; Molesworth anthology.

## 2026-03-18 — web round (cross-on-straw / umbrella-after-laughter lane; no keeper)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and deliberately stayed in fresh anthology ground: *Short Story Classics (Foreign), Vol. 3, German* and *Vol. 2, Italian and Scandinavian*.
- Strongest near-miss: **Antonio Fogazzaro, “The Silver Crucifix.”** The morally live image is a poor cholera-stricken woman, laid on straw so the bed will not have to burn, kissing the little cross once given her by the former countess and using her last strength not to curse the negligent rich but to bless them and pray for the husband she has long forgiven. I left it off the shelf because the force finally rises into pious commentary rather than becoming a clean, mouth-carried parable-image.
- Second near-miss: **Hermann Bang, “Irene Holm.”** After a worn old dancer is publicly laughed at during the one solo she has waited all her life to dance, the rector’s daughter walks her home, kisses her in the dark, and runs after the post-cart the next morning with food for the road. Real dignity-protection, but too slight and socially consoling to feel shelf-grade beside the sterner keepers.
- Briefly pressure-tested **Hermann Sudermann, “A New-Year’s Eve Confession,”** and the wider German volume lane around *The Stonebreakers* / *The Young Girl of Treppi*, but nothing else survived even to near-miss status.
- Decision: no addition to `Parables.txt`, and no new AI parable either. Better to keep the shelf sharp than to mistake humane literature for parable.


## 2026-03-18 — web round (old-soldier / hidden-duty / no keeper)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and tried to stay in fresher ground rather than the already-mined saint / shame / beggar lanes.
- Strongest fresh near-miss: **François Coppée, "The Captain’s Vices."** An old soldier sees a lame child working for soup and a bed under the stairs, takes her into his room as a servant, and then, by slow acts of renunciation, becomes her father instead. The image of him cutting away absinthe, cards, and small vanities so the child can eat and one day inherit a little shop is genuinely moving; still, it finally felt too redemptive-sentimental and too reward-shaped to clear the shelf beside the sterner keepers.
- Second near-miss: **L. De Bra, "A Life—A Bowl of Rice."** The opening hospitality is beautiful: a poor sugar-cane seller quietly gives his last bowl of rice to a proud starving old hatchetman without exposing his hunger. But the story turns into retaliatory killing in payment for the meal, which makes it unusable for this archive despite the strong opening image.
- Also glanced at / pressure-tested nearby lanes through Gutenberg result pages: **Louis Becke’s _Âmona; The Child; And The Beast; And Others_**, **William Dean Howells’s _The Quality of Mercy_**, **The Little Colonel’s Hero**, and **The Rich Little Poor Boy**. Nothing there ripened quickly enough into a compact, morally clean keeper for this pass.

## 2026-03-18 — web round (outlawed-for-another / creature-mercy lane; no keeper)
- Strongest near-miss: Selma Lagerlöf, “The Outlaws.” The image that mattered was the boy who accepted outlawry for his father's theft and had therefore inherited shame without earning it; but the ending turns violent enough that the story would not remain morally clean once radically shortened.
- Second near-miss: John Galsworthy, “Buttercup-Night.” The old man keeping night watch with the mare is unusually tender, and the line about some Christians lacking the soul of a horse has real bite, but the piece remains more atmosphere + reflection than portable parable.

## 2026-03-18 — web round (forgiveness-with-stain / pastoral-renunciation lane; no keeper)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and stayed in fresh anthology / index ground instead of revisiting already-mined shame-mercy keepers.
- Strongest near-miss: **Camille Lemonnier, “Saint Nicholas Eve”** (via *Stories by Foreign Authors: Polish, Greek, Belgian, Hungarian*). The living image is potent: Dolf prays over the coffin of the rich seducer, asks God to forgive him as he has forgiven him, and accepts the child as his own. But the story is morally stained at the root by Dolf’s own confession that, when he first recognized the drowning man, he thrust him back under the water before saving him. Too mixed, too inwardly violent, once radically shortened.
- Second near-miss: **Selma Lagerlöf, “The Silver Mine”** (via *Short Stories of Various Types*). A village minister learns that sudden wealth is ruining his people and chooses to bury the secret of the mine, remaining poor with them rather than enrich them into self-destruction. Noble and memorable, but finally it rises into idealized pastoral renunciation rather than becoming a sharp mouth-carried parable of mercy.
- Also checked **Thomas Burke, “The Chink and the Child,”** and rejected it outright for this archive despite its sacrificial end; the whole imaginative frame is too compromised by exploitation, voyeurism, and racialized sentiment to survive retelling cleanly.
- Decision: no change to `Parables.txt`, and no new AI parable this round. The shelf stayed sharper by refusing both the stained act and the noble abstraction.

## 2026-03-18 — imagined-correspondent / christmas-hovel lane
- **Strongest near-miss: Maxim Gorky, “Boless.”** A woman with no one to love her asks for letters to and from an imaginary man so she can hear them read aloud and briefly believe herself cherished. The student, once he understands, begins writing faithfully. There is real compassion here—entering another person's poverty of heart without mockery—but the mercy is inseparable from maintaining a fiction. That instability feels dangerous for this shelf: too easy to retell as sentiment or indulgent illusion, not clean enough as a Jesus-grade image.
- **Second near-miss: Pierre Loti, “The Wall Opposite.”** The living image is powerful: a crippled greengrocer and his adopted street-boy find a rich banker's lost child, feed him, lay both boys together in one bed, place Christmas sweets in both pairs of shoes, and refuse money. Beautiful, but the source leans hard on seasonal miracle / explicit conversion energy; once compressed, it glows too much and cuts too little.
- Briefly checked the broader *Short Story Classics (Foreign), Vol. 5, French II* lane—especially **“Putois”** and **“The Sign of the Key and the Cross”**—but nothing else carried a mercy image strong enough to warrant deeper extraction.


## 2026-03-18 — web round (blood-signal / confession-return lane)
Kept / added to `Parables.txt`:
- Vsevolod Garshin, **"The Signal"** → retold as **"The Man Who Raised a Flag from His Blood."**
  Why it cleared: the moral center is not bare heroism but a double mercy. Semyon spends his own blood to save the innocent strangers on the train, and that same wound becomes the thing that draws the saboteur back into confession. The line that mattered most in the source came before the blood: he calls after the fleeing man, in effect, to come back and save his soul from sin. That gave the whole ending a Jesus-grade turn instead of a merely brave one.

Considered but did NOT keep:
- **Alphonse Daudet, "At the Palais de Justice"** — vivid and tender, but finally more a poignant scene of lowly love in the ante-chamber of judgment than a clean, portable mercy-parable.
- **Erckmann-Chatrian, "The Dean's Watch"** — interesting village / accusation / crime lane, but too much apparatus and too little moral clarity for this shelf.
- Re-opened anthology lanes in **Stories by Foreign Authors: Russian / Scandinavian / Italian** and **Short Story Classics (Foreign), Vol. 4, French I**; useful for discovery, but no other title stayed alive after stripping away literary scaffolding.

No new AI parable added this round.

## 2026-03-18 — web round (feud-fire / neighbor-forgiven lane)
Kept / added to `Parables.txt`:
- **Leo Tolstoy, "The Candle"** → retold as **"The House That Burned Second."**
  Why it cleared: the living center is not merely arson or remorse but the dying father's command that the injured son keep silence about the guilty neighbor and forgive him. The source's image of a feud becoming a village fire, and then being answered not with exposure but with hidden mercy, feels unusually Jesus-grade: mercy does not deny the evil, but it refuses to let revenge be the last flame standing.

Strong near-misses / fresh lanes:
- **Fernán Caballero, "Bread Cast Upon the Waters"** — morally beautiful hospitality to a dying reaper family and later adoption, but too pious, extended, and reward-shaped for this shelf once radically shortened.
- **Ryūnosuke Akutagawa, "An Incident"** (via George Saunders's Story Club excerpt and secondary traces) — a rickshaw man stopping for a fallen old woman when the respectable passenger wants to move on. Very clean image, but I could not ground it in a sufficiently direct / stable primary-text lane during this pass, and in the available form it felt more like a wound of the narrator's shame than a fully bearable keeper.
- Briefly reopened Russian anthology / Spanish anthology discovery routes (*Short-story masterpieces, Vol. 3–4, Russian*; *Stories by Foreign Authors: Spanish*), but nothing else survived pressure-testing.

No new AI parable added this round.

## 2026-03-18 — web round (maildun / enemy-open-door)
Kept / added to `Parables.txt`:
- **P. W. Joyce, *Old Celtic Romances* ("The Voyage of Maildun")** → retold as **"The House at the End of the Sea."**
  Why it cleared: the revenge-quest is severe, but the keeper is not the quest; it is the final turn. A son arrives at the killer's door ready for vengeance, hears that the man would now welcome and clothe him, and chooses table over blood. The image feels clean, portable, and Jesus-grade in a rare way: the enemy's house becomes the place where revenge dies without being repaid in kind.

Near-misses / reopened lanes:
- **I. L. Peretz, "If Not Higher"** — reopened only to confirm it was already represented in the collection; not a new keeper this round.

## 2026-03-18 — web round (anthology grace / StoryCorps radical grace; no keeper)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and stayed out of the already-mined Tolstoy / Hasidic / saint / shame-mercy lanes.

Instead the teacher says, in effect, “Use the hand you have; I only need to be able to read it,” and turns the supposed defect into a gift. Clean, gracious, and memorable.

- **Anton Chekhov, "The Beggar"** → retold as **"The Woman at the Woodshed."**

She bears the sinner's burden with her own body, doing the work he cannot do, hiding the gift so he can still receive it, and weeping over him until he changes. ## 2026-03-18 — web round (rattrap / captain-named-mercy)
Kept / added to `Parables.txt`:
- **Selma Lagerlöf, "The Rattrap"** → retold as **"The Woman Who Called Him Captain."**
  Why it cleared: the center is not merely that a thief returns stolen money, but that undeserved dignity calls him up into truth. Edla does not excuse the theft or sentimentalize him; she simply refuses to deal with him as nothing but his worst act, and that trust becomes the very road by which he gives the money back. That feels unusually Jesus-grade: naming the buried person before the evidence has improved.

Considered but did NOT keep:
- **O. Henry, "A Retrieved Reformation."** Strong in self-exposure and costly action, but the deep moral energy finally runs through romance, suspense, and a detective's discretionary mercy; it feels more dramatic than hand-sized once radically shortened.
- **Selma Lagerlöf, "The Girl from the Marsh Croft."** There may be noble material there, but in the available pass it stayed too novelistic and socially entangled to become a clean, portable parable within ten sentences.

## 2026-03-18 — web round (mercy-vs-rigor / hidden-dignity / no keeper)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and then stayed in fresh ground: a small Mary Wilkins Freeman lane, scattered American Literature kindness/failure stories, and a StoryCorps lane looking for portable acts of dignity after shame.
- Strongest near-miss: **Mark Twain, "Was It Heaven? Or Hell?"** The live center is real — rigid truthfulness gives way to merciful speech beside the dying — but the kindness finally depends on repeated compassionate lying. That feels too jagged and morally unstable for this shelf once radically shortened.
- Second near-miss: **Mary E. Wilkins Freeman, "The Last Gift."** A ruined itinerant preacher, unable to watch a freezing family go on hungry, steals his host's coat to ransom them a little Christmas warmth and walks away ashamed but grateful. The pity is fierce, but the act itself stays too stained by theft to become a clean Jesus-grade parable here.
- Strong modern near-miss: **StoryCorps / Jane Vance and Lucinda Roy.** After the Virginia Tech shooting, a classroom of stunned students begins to speak, names the dead and the wounded, and rises to embrace one another before the lesson starts. Beautiful human tenderness, but still more witness-memory than hand-sized parable.
- Also checked a few other fresh kindness lanes (Edith Wharton, T. S. Arthur, Edward Payson Roe, and broader StoryCorps dignity / re-entry pages). Nothing else survived even to keeper-level pressure.
- Decision: no addition to `Parables.txt`, and no new original AI parable this round. Better to leave the shelf hungry than to feed it something morally blurred.

# 2026-03-18 — web round (non-possessive rescue / restored footing)
- Kept: **“The Shoe on the Hill”** — retold from Sui Sin Far’s *The Story of One White Woman Who Married a Chinese* / *Her Chinese Husband*. What cleared the bar was not mere rescue, but the rarer turn that the rescuer gave the woman and child shelter, work fitted to her skill, and a future that did not leave them owned by his kindness. The phrase that finally convinced me was that he “made them both independent, not only of others but of himself.”
- Strong near-miss: **O. Henry, “A Service of Love.”** Warm and mutually sacrificial, but the hidden center depends on mutual deception, and the shape felt too close to *The Gift of the Magi* already living on this shelf.
- Near-miss: **O. Henry, “The Green Door.”** Beautiful chance-mercy to a starving stranger, but too coincidence-driven / romance-shaped after shortening.
- Near-miss: **O. Henry, “Witches’ Loaves.”** Sympathetic impulse, but the kindness does not listen and ends by ruining another person’s work.
- Also checked **“Two Thanksgiving Day Gentlemen.”** Tender ritual dignity, but too comic and ceremonial to stand as a parable here.
- No new AI parable this round.

## 2026-03-18 — web round (Rosicky apron / no keeper)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and stayed off the already-mined Hughes / Cather keepers once I noticed `Thank You, Ma'am` and the Rosicky goose lane were already represented in `Parables.txt`.

- Strong near-miss: **Willa Cather, “Neighbour Rosicky”** — specifically the kitchen-apron scene where Rosicky sees his daughter-in-law turning lonesome, takes the family car from his own sons, washes her dishes himself, slips his proud son money for ice cream, and protects the young couple's dignity without sermonizing. Beautiful, but it remains part of a larger life-portrait; when radically shortened, it feels more like a facet of a saintly character than a self-standing parable-image.
- Also checked **A. E. Coppard, “Tribute.”** Powerful as a social criticism of wealth fed by other people's losses, but too satirical / systemic for this shelf; it denounces false stewardship more than it hands the listener a clean, portable act of mercy.

Rich in benevolence, but many of Hopper's strongest episodes depend on tactical deception in anti-slavery rescue work, which makes them hard to compress into a clean Jesus-grade parable without moral remainder.

## 2026-03-18 — web round (Tagore father-recognition / Kabuliwala handprint)
Kept / added to `Parables.txt`:
- **Rabindranath Tagore, "The Cabuliwallah"** → retold as **"The Handprint in the Folded Paper."**
  Why it cleared: the deepest turn is not mere sentiment between a child and a peddler, but one father recognizing another father across fear, class, crime, and ceremony. The handprint on the paper makes the whole story small enough to carry; the wedding grows dimmer so another household can be restored. That feels clean, concrete, and Jesus-grade.

- **Premchand, "Idgah."** Still excellent, but already on the shelf in our tongs-from-the-fair retelling, so I left it untouched.

- **T. S. Arthur, "The Portrait."** Interesting filial / moral image, but too essay-like and admonitory for this shelf.

No new AI parable this round.

## 2026-03-18 — web round (poverty-peace / saint-vigil / no keeper)

## 2026-03-18 — web round (threshold belonging / full share)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and then stayed in fresh ground: the American Literature feel-good list as a discovery map, Laura E. Richards’s **"Little Benjamin"**, Louisa May Alcott’s **"Cousin Tribulation’s Story,"** Matsuo Basho / folktale **"The Aged Mother,"** Chekhov’s **"A Story Without a Title,"** and a modern comparison lane around Raymond Carver’s **"A Small, Good Thing."**
- Kept: **"The Boy Who Could Not Be Kept Halfway"** — retold from Laura E. Richards’s **"Little Benjamin."** What finally cleared the bar was the father’s refusal to let the household go on enjoying the warmth of the foundling while withholding true belonging: either send him away, or let him be brother with a full share. That image of mercy refusing permanent probation feels rare, sharp, and clean enough for this shelf.
- Strong near-miss: **Louisa May Alcott, "Cousin Tribulation’s Story."** The image is beautiful — poor children giving away their own New Year breakfast and carrying warmth to a freezing family — but the archive already has several fierce scarcity-giving lanes, and this one finally felt more memoir-blessing than distinct new parable.
- Strong near-miss: **Raymond Carver, "A Small, Good Thing."** The ending held surprising weight: a baker who had wounded grieving parents with ignorant phone calls repents, seats them, feeds them hot rolls and bread through the night, and makes human fellowship out of shared eating. I left it off the shelf because the story remains bound to a child’s death and to a longer arc of alienation/reconciliation that does not condense cleanly enough into a ten-sentence mouth-carried parable.
- Boundary checks declined: **"The Aged Mother"** (wise and loving, but too folktale-wisdom / filial piety centered rather than neighbor-mercy centered), **"A Story Without a Title"** (interesting, but more anti-ascetic satire than keeper), **"Caline"** and **"Hot Potatoes"** (not in the right moral field), and **"Deacon Pitkin’s Farm"** (too baggy / domestic for this shelf).
- No new AI parable this round.

## 2026-03-18 — web round (shared shame / church steps / one keeper)

Wilkins Freeman, "A Conflict Ended"** → kept as **"The Bride Who Would Have Sat Beside Him."** What cleared the bar was the exact turn on the church steps: the bride does not lecture, threaten, or walk ahead; she becomes willing to share the public shame and deadlock with him. ## 2026-03-19 — web round (teacher / doll / deep cuts / no keeper)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and deliberately stayed off the more exhausted saint / Hasidic / Tolstoy / O. Henry shelves. This pass pushed into Kind World, StoryCorps archive material, picture-book kindness lanes, public-domain “feel-good” lists, and a few Marginalian / Plough / Kirkus / Guideposts side roads.

But the archive already effectively contains that same dignity-image more cleanly as **“The Table Set at Noon.”** I left it closed as a duplicate rather than re-adding the lane under a different title.
- Secondary near-misses: the **courthouse-restroom hug** in StoryCorps **“An Act of Kindness,”** the older woman gifting a car in **Donna Macomber and Linda Macomber,** and **“A Different Window.”** All real kindness; none survived hard shortening into a mouth-carried parable without turning into testimony, atmosphere, or long-arc biography.
- `AI-Parables.txt` left unchanged.

## 2026-03-19 — web round (kept: man who covered his face)
- Referred to `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and then pushed into fresh lanes: Parabola index leads, picture-book / peace-education leads, and Bahá’í enemy-love anecdotes.
- Kept: **The Man Who Covered His Face**, retold from the ‘Abdu’l-Bahá / hostile Afghan story. What cleared the bar was the unforgettable image-field: one hand stretched out for healing, the other still hiding the face from the healer. That is clean enough to carry “love your enemies” without sermonizing.
- Why it felt shelf-worthy despite overlap risk: the mercy here is not mainly hospitality to a stranger or patience with a difficult guest, but sustained material care for an active enemy who remains unsoftened for years. The story does not wait for repentance before beginning mercy.
- Considered and did **not** keep: **The Forgiveness Garden** (Lauren Thompson). Morally bright and surprisingly strong for a children’s parable, but the allegorical naming (`Us/Them`, `Forgiveness`, `Kindness`) made it feel slightly over-engineered beside the cleaner enemy-love image in the Bahá’í story.
- Considered and did **not** keep: Parabola index probes (`The Good Serpent`, `Parable of the Trees and the Stars`) as discovery leads only; in the reachable results this round they did not yield a text I could responsibly assess for the main shelf.
- AI-Parables left unchanged this round.

## 2026-03-19 — web round (kept: enemy's son / one keeper)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and stayed on fresh ground. This pass moved through several contrast lanes: school-reconciliation picture books (`Enemy Pie`, `The Other Side`, `The Hundred Dresses`), a Lebanon-after-war lane (`The Olive Tree`), a hidden-mercy-under-occupation lane (`Gifts from the Enemy`), and a refugee-chair lane (`The Day War Came`).
- Kept: **the ‘Abdu’l-Bahá / worst-enemy’s son story** → kept as **“The Son He Called His Own.”** What finally cleared the bar was the exact turn of naming: the child is not presented as a project, a cause, or even merely an orphan, but as “my son.” That renaming forces mercy to move out of abstraction and into long obedience — feeding, teaching, washing, raising. It feels Jesus-grade because it refuses to let love of enemy remain sentimental or theoretical; it places the wound directly in the house and asks whether mercy will keep custody.
- Strong near-miss: **Gifts from the Enemy.** The German woman hiding sandwiches in a waste can for a starving Jewish boy is morally serious and memorable. But after pressure-testing it, too much of the force remained tied to the vast Holocaust setting and to testimonial witness rather than to a stand-alone parable image.
- Strong near-miss: **The Day War Came.** The empty chair brought for the refugee child is a clean and honorable image of welcome, but the archive already has several stronger place-made / room-left / chair-kept forms, and this one felt slightly more campaign-symbol than fresh parable.
- Secondary near-miss: **The Olive Tree.** Beautiful postwar atmosphere and a good fence/tree image, but the lightning-strike reconciliation felt a little too externally arranged compared with the harder inner turn in the keeper.
- Duplicate controls only: **Enemy Pie**, **The Other Side**, and **The Hundred Dresses** all remained useful calibration pieces but were either too engineered, too issue-explanatory, or too close to mercy-patterns the archive already holds.
- `AI-Parables.txt` left unchanged this round.

## 2026-03-19 — web round (no keep: enemy/wing/shoes/date-grove lanes)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and stayed off the most harvested shelves. This pass tested several fresher lanes: a contemporary enemy-recognition picture-book lane (`The Enemy` by Davide Calì), a wartime-dilemma fiction lane (Pearl S. Buck’s `The Enemy`), an Arab/Jewish shared-folktale lane (`Yaffa and Fatima`), a scarcity-and-giving lane (`Those Shoes`), and a small-creature compassion lane (`How to Heal a Broken Wing`).
- **No keeper added.** A number of these stories were good, but none felt rare or clean enough to justify moving onto the main shelf against the standard the archive is now enforcing.
- Strong near-miss: **The Enemy** (Davide Calì / Serge Bloch). The foxhole swap is elegant and memorable, and the discovery that the enemy carries the same manual is genuinely clarifying. But after pressure-testing it, the piece felt more like a lucid anti-war allegory than a Jesus-grade parable of mercy; it exposes false enmity well, yet stops short of the deeper positive action the collection’s strongest pieces carry.
- Strong near-miss: **Pearl S. Buck’s “The Enemy.”** The cleanest moment was the surgeon calling the wounded prisoner “my friend” in the middle of the operation, forgetting for an instant that he was meant to hate him. But the story as a whole remained a moral-dilemma narrative rather than a sharply transferable parable image, and the doctor’s action felt governed as much by professional duty and conflicted conscience as by the free, surplus mercy that usually marks a keeper here.
- Near-miss / duplicate-control: **Yaffa and Fatima: Shalom, Salaam.** Warm, honorable, and interfaith-beautiful, but too close in shape to already-kept mutual-secret-giving forms; it felt more like a culturally relocated cousin of archive material than a new shelf-worthy core.
- Near-miss / duplicate-control: **Those Shoes.** Morally clean and genuinely tender: a boy gives away the very shoes he longed for when he sees another child needs them more. But the archive already contains stronger want-vs-mercy and dignity-under-poverty forms, including shoe-adjacent ones, so this felt illuminating as calibration rather than necessary as addition.
- Near-miss: **How to Heal a Broken Wing.** Lovely in its simplicity: many step over the hurt creature, one child stoops. But it ultimately read as a beautiful compassion vignette more than as a rare, surprising parable-turn.
- Useful duplicate-control observation: this whole round confirmed again that children’s literature produces many honorable kindness stories, but the archive’s bar is now mostly rejecting those that explain themselves too gently or whose force depends mainly on classroom usefulness rather than on inexhaustible moral compression.
- `Parables.txt` unchanged. `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged.

## 2026-03-19 — web round (kept: eleven flights / blouse / school-clerk / amish-forgiveness lanes)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and stayed off the heavily harvested saint / Tolstoy / picture-book-review shelves. This pass moved through a vandal-compassion lane, a service-worker-dignity lane, a school-shooter-averted-by-empathy lane, and an Amish-forgiveness lane.
- Kept / added to `Parables.txt`: **“The Woman Who Climbed Eleven Flights.”** Source was a brief anecdote embedded in a longer essay, but the anecdote itself finally cleared the bar. What made it keep-worthy was not mere generosity but the exact form of it: while others kept feasting, one woman interrupted her own celebration, climbed all the way back to her room, and covered a worker’s visible shame with the garment she had saved for herself. The image is concrete, portable, and merciful without needing any reward or explanatory machinery.
- Strong near-miss: **Harshida / the oranges thrown at the house** (`DailyGood`). The turn — meeting vandals with “Can I have the oranges so they don’t go to waste?” — is alive and disarming. But the story remained more like a luminous anecdotal fragment than a fully landed parable; the act is memorable, yet the image did not quite ripen into a shelf-stable whole.
- Strong near-miss: **Antoinette Tuff talking down the gunman.** Morally serious and astonishingly brave. Several lines are unforgettable, especially the refusal to hate and the maternal voice offered to a man with nothing to live for. I did not keep it because it still reads more as public heroism inside a modern crisis than as a compact parable-image one would naturally carry in the mouth.
- Strong near-miss: **Nickel Mines / Amish forgiveness.** The acts are severe enough for the shelf: concern for the killer’s widow, attendance at the funeral, even later material aid. But the whole event remains communal, historical, and tragedy-scaled; it felt more like witness than parable, and I was reluctant to force such grief into the collection when the blouse story carried the moral weight more quietly.
- `AI-Parables.txt` left unchanged this round. The discovered human story was cleaner than the originals I considered.

## 2026-03-19 — web round (kept: enemy love song / off-list / amerasian shirt lanes)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and deliberately stayed away from the most exhausted saint / Tolstoy / picture-book-review shelves. This pass pushed through a noisy spirituality-review lane, a small Plough-forgiveness lane, WBUR Kind World, a StoryCorps hospitality / hunger lane, and then a music-and-enemy lane that finally produced a keeper.
- Kept / added to `Parables.txt`: **“The Man Who Played the Enemy's Love Song.”** What made it shelf-worthy was the exact form of the act: in darkness, with one sniper still hunting them, a soldier chose not a patriotic tune or a taunt but the enemy’s own love song. The image is concrete, portable, and morally severe in the right way: it does not deny danger, yet it reaches across it to the homesickness and belovedness still buried inside the other man.
- Strong near-miss: **“Off The List”** (WBUR Kind World). The extra thirty-two people taken from the tent are morally serious, and the title-image of the ones “not on the list” is strong. But after pressure-testing, the piece remained more humanitarian dilemma than parable; its force depends on logistics, risk, and scale rather than on one clean transferable act.
- Secondary near-miss: **Jimmy Miller giving his shirt to another Amerasian child.** A beautiful, morally clean fragment: a boy mocked as the child of the enemy sees a poorer child and gives away the shirt on his own back. I did not keep it because the anecdote is almost too brief; it shines, but it does not quite ripen into a full shelf-stable parable without leaning on the larger biography around it.
- Useful duplicate-control observation: several story and essay lanes this round kept circling back to arguments about enemy-love rather than to stories that enact it. The trumpet story finally cleared the bar because it carries the teaching inside one unforgettable gesture rather than inside explanation.
- `AI-Parables.txt` left unchanged this round.


## 2026-03-19 — web round (no keep: hidden-bread / borrowed-men / great-joy lanes)

## 2026-03-19 — web round (kept: night-caller / peace-ring / jailer-cover / enemy-seller lanes)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and stayed off the archive’s more exhausted saint / Tolstoy / sermon-illustration shelves. This pass moved through a Bruderhof/Nazi-history lane, a reconciliation-story lane around Michael Weisser and Larry Trapp, a Bassam Aramin prison-memory lane, and an old devotional enemy-love anecdote about a man caring for the one who sold him.
- **Kept / added to `Parables.txt`: “The Ring for the Night Caller.”** What finally cleared the bar was the sequence of mercies being concrete rather than merely admirable: learning the hater’s name, offering him a ride for groceries instead of an answering curse, then crossing his threshold with supper and a peace-ring. The image of hatred-rings being removed so another ring can be put in their place feels portable, morally severe, and clean enough to live in the mouth without extra explanation.
- Strong near-miss: **Bassam Aramin’s jailer covering him during a prison beating.** The image is astonishing — an enemy who had begun with argument and coffee suddenly throwing his own body over the prisoner. I held it back because the surrounding historical and political freight is so heavy that the anecdote still feels more embedded witness than free-standing parable, though it is one of the strongest acts I saw this round.
- Strong near-miss: **Elijah caring for the slave trader who had sold him.** The turn itself is severe enough for the shelf: he uses his authority not for revenge but to bring the old man into his own hut and cool his face when he is dying. I did not keep it because the surviving text comes through devotional children’s retellings with heavy dialect and explicit moralizing, which made it harder to trust as a clean source-piece for this archive.
- Useful non-keep: **An Embassy Besieged** added atmosphere and ethical backbone but no compact keeper. Plenty of principled enemy-love language, little in the way of one self-sufficient story-image.

Heuristic reinforced:
- At this stage the danger is not failing to find stories, but mistaking *adjacent goodness* for *new necessity*.

## 2026-03-20 — web round (Marey keeper / Wedding Shoes control / Hedgehog near-miss)

The later turn is also strong enough to travel: that remembered mercy breaks the narrator’s disgust and lets him look at violent prisoners as human again. That feels rare, clean, and Jesus-grade.

## 2026-03-20 — web round (Teacher Jensen keeper / Christmas anthology / Parabola index scan)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and avoided the more exhausted Tolstoy / Premchand / Chekhov / saint-legend / Basheer lanes. This pass worked a fresh Project Gutenberg anthology lane (**_Christmas in Modern Story_**) plus a light Parabola-index scan for untouched mercy titles.
- **Added one keeper to `Parables.txt`: “The Trees for Every Cell.”** Source pressure-test: Karin Michaelis’s **“Teacher Jensen.”** What cleared the bar was the exact movement from imagination to mercy: children are made to feel, in their own bodies, one hour of prison-silence under separate trees; then they spend themselves so that every prisoner has a tree, a light, a note, and for one night the doors stand open. This feels unusually clean and Jesus-compatible because it does not flatter the children or excuse the prisoners; it simply restores remembered humanity to the forgotten.
- Strong near-miss, not a keeper: **Temple Bailey, “The Candle in the Forest.”** Sweet and morally decent — a lonely rich boy is welcomed into a poor family’s imaginative joy and ends by giving away a coveted doll — but it stayed too Christmas-card / wistful-domestic once shortened.

## 2026-03-20 — web round (Rothschild keeper / Jerome controls / Piglia control)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and then worked a fresh but stern lane through **Chekhov via American Literature**, **Jerome K. Jerome** moral-fictions, and a **Words Without Borders** microfiction control rather than circling the more exhausted testimony / saint-legend shelves.
- **Added one keeper to `Parables.txt`: “The Fiddle for the Man He Wronged.”** Source pressure-test: Chekhov’s **“Rothschild’s Fiddle.”** What finally cleared the bar was not the atmosphere alone but the moral exactness of the turn: a man who has spent his life counting losses comes at last to see that hatred itself has been his great waste, and that the person he despised is also the person he most needs to bless. The final act — giving his cherished instrument to the man he had frightened and insulted — feels severe, memorable, and close to Jesus’ kind of repentance, where wrong is not merely regretted but placed back into the hands of the wronged as gift.
- Useful negative control: **Jerome K. Jerome, “The Cost of Kindness.”** Interesting social irony, but too satirical and village-comic for the main shelf.
- Useful near-miss, not a keeper: **Jerome K. Jerome, “The Passing of the Third Floor Back.”** Morally serious, but too openly benefactor-novel / Christ-figure allegory to live comfortably beside the archive’s smaller, mouth-carried parables.
- Useful negative control: **Ricardo Piglia, “Brief Stories.”** Sharp and suggestive, but too fragmentary, essay-adjacent, and dark to ripen into a clean keeper.
- Left `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged on purpose. No original I conceived this round felt cleaner than the Chekhov keeper.

## 2026-03-20 — web round (Red Gulch near-miss / Twain lie-misery / no-keeper pass)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and deliberately searched around unswept territory rather than returning to the Tolstoy / prison / feud-reconciliation shelves.
- Worked four fresh lanes: (1) older moral-story collections and anthologies (**T. S. Arthur**, **Victorian / foreign short-story anthologies**); (2) a few directly readable public-domain literary texts on **American Literature** (**Bret Harte**, **Mark Twain**, **Chekhov**); (3) one German/Jewish family-forgiveness lane through **Wikisource / Kompert**; and (4) a quick translated-fiction scan at **Words Without Borders**.

- Strongest near-miss: **Bret Harte, _The Idyl of Red Gulch_**. What kept it alive was the one brief act that almost survives compression: the schoolteacher first agrees to take the child of the shamed woman, then, at the door, catches that woman to her own pure breast for one moment before shutting the door. Real mercy is there. I still left it off the shelf because the story leans too much on melodrama and on severing the child from the father; it does not feel clean enough, or plain enough, beside the archive’s stronger pieces.

- Useful refusal: **Kompert, _Ghetto Violet_**. There is forgiveness pressure around the father returning from prison, but the story remains too extended and family-novelistic for this shelf.
- General lesson from this pass: some stories contain a single Jesus-grade gesture inside them, yet still fail the shelf because the surrounding machinery (melodrama, thesis, family plotting) is doing too much of the work.

## 2026-03-20 — web round (Head-Gardener / Three Gifts / no-keeper pass)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and then deliberately searched around less-mined, directly readable anthology paths instead of circling the now-heavy Tolstoy / prison / feud / testimony shelves.

- Clean **no-keeper** round. Left `Parables.txt` unchanged. Left `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged.
- Strongest near-miss: **Chekhov, _The Head-Gardener’s Story_.** What kept it alive is the severe claim that it is “better to be deceived than to suspect,” and that merciful faith in human dignity may be higher than correct suspicion. I still left it off the shelf because the acquittal of a clearly guilty murderer feels too indiscriminate / civically unclean to sit beside the archive’s sterner keepers; Jesus-grade mercy tells the truth without needing to pretend evil is unreal.
- Useful refusal: **I. L. Peretz, _Three Gifts_.** The gifts are unforgettable — a blood-stained grain of Holy Land sand, a pin used to preserve modesty under execution, a skullcap retrieved under the lash — but the piece is built on visionary martyrdom and sacred beauty more than neighborly kindness that can be carried in the mouth as parable.
- Useful refusal: **A. L. O. E., _The Convict’s Child_.** Worth mapping as a Victorian redemption lane, but what I could verify this round looked too juvenile / novelistic and too extended to justify a shelf slot.
- Useful refusal: **Agnes McClelland Daulton, _A Christmas Confession_.** Tender and humane in its wish that no one be left out, but too tidy / domestic / seasonal to clear the collection’s present bar.
- Useful unresolved lane: the **Leskov gardener** in _The Cathedral Folk_ still surfaced only indirectly this round (through Chekhov and bibliographic references), not with enough direct narrative footing to trust a retelling.
- General lesson from this pass: some stories preach generous trust beautifully, but the archive still seems to prefer acts of mercy that remain truthful, concrete, and portable after the commentary is stripped away.

## 2026-03-20 — web round (duplicate controls / signal near-miss / tanukhi insufficiency / no-keeper pass)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and deliberately worked around the existing trail instead of revisiting the already-mined thief-mercy shelf blindly.
- Clean no-keeper round. Two morally strong things surfaced immediately but proved to be duplicates already living on the shelf: **Langston Hughes, “Thank You, Ma'am”** (already represented in the purse / blue-shoes lane) and the StoryCorps bank-forgiveness conversation, already kept as **“The Man Who Thought of His Own Son.”**
- Strongest genuine near-miss: **Vsevolod Garshin, “The Signal.”** A railway worker discovers that an embittered coworker has torn up a rail, cuts his own arm to make a blood-red warning flag, and by that act stops a passenger train; the saboteur, seeing the self-wounding mercy, steps forward and confesses. Powerful, but I left it off the main shelf because it finally feels more like duty-and-sacrifice under catastrophe than the archive’s cleaner neighbor-mercy or dignity-protecting turns.
- Useful under-grounded lead: the **al-Tanukhi / Julia Bray** note about a very short parable in *Deliverance Follows Adversity*—a man goes mad after killing a calf before its mother and is healed when he lifts a fledgling back into its nest. The image is striking and morally alive, but from this pass I could ground it only through secondary discussion, not through a directly readable primary text solid enough for a trustworthy retelling.
- Left `Parables.txt` unchanged. Left `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged; nothing I conceived this round felt rarer or cleaner than the best things already on the shelf.


## 2026-03-20 — web round (substitute duplicate / forgiveness near-miss / no-keeper pass)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and deliberately searched around unswept anthology / translated-story lanes rather than revisiting the same prison-mercy pages blindly.
- **No new keeper.** `Parables.txt` unchanged. `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged.
- The strongest-seeming candidate this round was **François Coppée, _The Substitute_** — an ex-convict turned mason takes the theft on himself so that his younger friend will not begin the same descent through prison and hardening. But this turned out not to be a fresh keeper at all: the archive already carries it in retold form as **“The Mason Who Took the Handcuffs.”** Useful duplicate-control result, not a new shelf item.
- Strongest fresh near-miss: **Guy de Maupassant, _Forgiveness_.** The ending is morally serious — a wife finally agreeing to reconcile only after kneeling first at the grave of the woman who wronged her with her husband — but it stayed too bound to adulterous-reconciliation drama and too novelistic in its emotional machinery to feel like a hand-sized parable for this shelf.
- Useful refusal: **Maria Edgeworth, _Forgive and Forget_.** Plainly virtuous, but too didactic / juvenile and too engineered around lesson delivery rather than a surprising kingdom-image.
- Useful refusal: **Fernán Caballero, _Bread Cast Upon the Waters_.** Real hospitality to sick strangers, but the story broadens into patriotic war material and loses the compact portable clarity the archive prefers.
- General lesson from this pass: duplicate control matters almost as much as discovery now; some of the strongest moral stories still surface only to reveal that the shelf has already absorbed their cleanest image.

## 2026-03-20 — web round (fur coat / Little Me / no-keeper pass)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and then worked around fresher but still directly readable primary-text lanes instead of circling the same prison / feud / thief-mercy shelves again.
- Main fresh lanes this pass: **Hjalmar Söderberg / _Modern Swedish Masterpieces_**, **Sui Sin Far / _Mrs. Spring Fragrance_**, a small **StoryCorps archive** kindness-grace probe, and the contents / side-indexes of **_The Best Short Stories of 1920_** plus one archive-text detour for Wilbur Daniel Steele.
- **No new keeper.** Left `Parables.txt` unchanged. Left `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged.
- Strongest near-miss: **Hjalmar Söderberg, “The Fur Coat.”** A dying doctor borrows his wealthy friend’s fur coat, is mistaken in the dark by his own wife for the man she now secretly longs for, and receives one last moment of warmth before returning the coat and thanking the friend whose garment carried that happiness. Real pity and tragic tenderness are there, but I left it off the shelf because the moral weight depends too much on erotic misrecognition, status-envy, and the pathos of a marriage already bent out of shape. It does not travel as a clean kingdom-image.
- Useful near-miss: **Sui Sin Far, “The Gift of Little Me.”** The teacher insists that the poor child’s handmade gift is as precious as the rich children’s costly presents, and then goes into the alley when his baby brother disappears. Humane, memorable, and worth mapping — but it stayed more in the lane of social tenderness / classroom ethic than the archive’s rarer hand-sized parable salience.
- Useful refusal: **Achmed Abdullah, “A Simple Act of Piety.”** Reopened because the title still tugged; rejected quickly because its engine is murder rationalized by clan duty and exotic fatalism, not mercy.
- Useful under-grounded lead: **Wilbur Daniel Steele, “God’s Mercy.”** It surfaced in indexes and secondary traces, but I still did not get a directly readable primary-text lane clean enough to trust for retelling this round.
- General lesson from this pass: not every story with pity, sacrifice, or a wound-hidden gift belongs here. The shelf still seems to prefer acts of mercy that remain morally plain even after romance, atmosphere, and social explanation are stripped away.

## 2026-03-20 — web round (Hasidic anti-shame / French accusation lane / no-keeper pass)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and then worked mostly in directly readable primary-text lanes that had not yet been logged: a small **Chabad / anti-humiliation** cluster and a **Wikisource French-story** accusation lane.
- **No new keeper.** Left `Parables.txt` unchanged. Left `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged.
- Useful duplicate-control reminder: I reopened **Chabad's "The Loan"** while testing the Hasidic lane, then recognized it as an already-mapped boundary case in the scratch history: morally serious, but the marriage-as-repair ending still feels too socially specific / status-mediated for this shelf.
- Strongest fresh near-miss: **"A Tailor in a Stitch"** (Chabad). A child exposes a tailor who has kept the leftover cloth from a dress; afterward, when asked what happened, the child refuses to identify the man because shaming him once was already sin enough. Very clean and severe, and close to the archive's center. I still left it off the main shelf because it functions more as a holy anecdote or ethical maxim than as a hand-sized parable with a full turn.
- Useful refusal: **"The Black Pearl"** (Stories by Foreign Authors / French I). Its live center is painful and memorable: a Bohemian girl is accused because she once stole as a child, and the household kneels to ask forgiveness when they think they have wronged her. But the plot finally resolves by lightning rather than by mercy or repentance, so the moral weight gets stolen by mechanism.
- Useful refusal: **"The Gift"** (Chabad / Safed challah story). The sincere man who thinks God is eating his loaves is moving, and the rabbi's rebuke is judged from heaven; but the whole force depends on miracle-validation and mystical correction, not on a plain neighborly act that can travel in the mouth as parable.
- Useful side-lane refusal: **"The Story of a Lie"** (Littell's Living Age). There is reconciliation and apology in it, but it stays too novelistic and family-machinery-heavy once compressed.
- General lesson from this pass: the archive still resists stories whose goodness depends on miracle, status repair, or literary complication. The cleanest anti-shame pieces remain either too slight to keep, or else close cousins of things already stronger on the shelf.

## 2026-03-21 — web round (Selma dignity lane / Hatim keeper pass)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and deliberately tried a fresh **Selma Lagerlöf / Invisible Links** lane before moving into a tighter **anti-shame anecdote** lane.
- **Added one new keeper to `Parables.txt`: “The Deafness He Chose.”** Source lane: the Hatim al-Asamm tradition, where a woman is suddenly embarrassed in conversation and he protects her by acting hard of hearing, then keeps up the pretense for years.
- Why I kept it: the moral motion is unusually clean, costly, and non-spectacular. The act does not solve a social problem, win admiration, or depend on miracle. It simply accepts a small lifelong inconvenience so another person does not have to carry one public wound.
- Strong near-miss lane: **Selma Lagerlöf, _Invisible Links_** (`Mamsell Fredrika`, `The Romance of a Fisherman's Wife`, `His Mother's Portrait`, `A Christmas Guest`, `A Fallen King`). Rich atmosphere and moral weather, but on this pass they still read as beautiful story-worlds rather than hand-sized kingdom images.
- Useful near-miss: **George Washington Cable / Madame Delphine** surfaced as a possible dignity-and-hidden-sorrow lane, but I did not press it this round because Hatim proved cleaner and more immediate once the anti-shame theme sharpened.
- Left `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged.
- General lesson from this pass: the shelf still seems to prefer mercy that is plain, durable, and privately costly. A very small act becomes keeper-grade when it keeps another person's dignity alive long after the moment itself has passed.

## 2026-03-21 — web round (Aryeh Levin pudding keeper pass)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and then probed a fresh **Rabbi Aryeh Levin / educator-mercy** lane rather than another generic anti-shame lane.
- **Added one new keeper to `Parables.txt`: “The Teacher and the Two Puddings.”** Source lane: the Reb Aryeh Levin anecdote about a boy who, after being publicly shamed for trying to get more pudding, kicked over the pudding cart and expected expulsion the next morning.
- Why I kept it: the turn is clean, surprising, and portable. The adult does not excuse the wrong, but neither does he make the child wear the worst moment of his hunger forever. He asks for truth, asks for repentance, and then restores the boy by sharing the very thing around which the shame gathered.
- What makes it feel shelf-worthy: the mercy is not abstract forgiveness but embodied companionship. The teacher does not lecture the child out of anger; he sits beside him and quietly changes the meaning of the scene.
- Strong supporting lane: multiple retellings of the incident surfaced (grandson recollection, summary biography, and secondary profiles), enough to make it feel like a stable anecdotal tradition rather than a single floating meme.
- Near-miss reconsidered but still rejected: the Masnavi story of the Prophet cleaning an infidel guest’s bedding after the guest fell ill. Still luminous, but still reads more like devotional hagiography than a hand-sized parable for this shelf.
- Left `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged.

- Reopened the archive and checked `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first; most broad “protecting dignity” lanes were either already covered or morally thinner than they first appeared.
- Strong near-miss: the Sdai Chemed anecdote (bearing false shame rather than reopening scandal). Admirable, but it felt more like a case of heroic restraint than a parable with a single clean turn.
- Rechecked the Eknath holy-water-for-a-donkey lane only to confirm we already had its moral territory in the collection.
- Kept: the Namdev story of the dog stealing his bread and Namdev chasing after it with ghee so the thief would not have to eat dry bread. The turn is simple, unforced, and severe in the best way: it blesses even the thing that has just taken from you.

## 2026-03-21 — web round (hidden-dignity / anti-shame charity / no-keeper pass)

## 2026-03-21 — web round (Vitalis / scandal-mercy keeper pass)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and then pushed into a narrower **mercy-under-scandal** lane, trying to avoid the now-crowded thief-mercy and classroom-dignity shelves.
- Briefly re-surfaced Chabad’s **“The Stolen Ring and the Surprised Thief”** through a broad thief/saint search result, but kept the previous rejection in place; still too close to existing archive shapes.
- **Added one new keeper to `Parables.txt`: “The Wages at Dusk.”** Source lane: the St. Vitalis / Vitalius of Gaza tradition, corroborated through OCA and Notre Dame saint pages, with secondary support from other hagiographic retellings.
- Why I kept it despite the hazardous subject matter: the heart of the story is not titillation, scandal, or even rescue-success. The heart is that he spends his strength and reputation to buy exploited women one night in which no one may use them, and he protects their names even when doing so costs him his own.
- What clears the bar: it joins two Jesus-grade notes at once—mercy toward the publicly despised, and rebuke of the righteous crowd that prefers gossip to costly love. The reveal after death also lands cleanly: the city has to learn that it has mistaken contamination for compassion.
- Risk acknowledged: this is hagiographic material, not a minimalist folktale, and the received versions contain miraculous / punitive elements I intentionally left out of the retelling. The shelf version works only if stripped down to the moral spine: labor, hidden mercy, lost reputation, restored humanity, exposed judgment.
- Left `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged this round. The discovered story felt stronger and stranger than anything new I was prepared to write.

## 2026-03-21 — web round (John the Persian keeper; basin-footwashing pass)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and aimed for fresh lanes that were not just more variants of “saint lets thief take things”: **Abba John the Persian / foot-washing intruders**, **Bhai Nihal Singh / leave the hole for the thief**, **Nonnus and Pelagia**, plus a few sanity-check thief-mercy pages in Sikh, Sufi, Zen, and Chabad retellings.
- **Added one keeper to `Parables.txt`: `## The Basin at the Door`.**
- Why it survived: the moral turn is immediate, portable, and sharply Jesus-like without copying a Gospel parable. The old man does not merely refrain from violence; he takes the servant’s place in front of would-be attackers, and their repentance arises from being treated as guests before they have earned it.
- Source stability was good enough for shelf use: the core wording appears in multiple Desert Fathers collections / quotations (OrthodoxWiki, EWTN’s *Paradise of the Desert Fathers*, Patheos quoting the Sayings, and a recent Comboni roundup citing the same apophthegm).
- Strong near-miss: **Bhai Nihal Singh and the hole in the wall**. Beautiful line—“don’t plug that hole; he may be too shy to ask by day”—but I left it off the shelf because the archive already has several stronger thief-mercy stories, and this one risks reading more like luminous policy than an unforgettable kingdom turn.
- Strong near-miss: **Nonnus / Pelagia**. Morally serious and anti-objectifying, but in the forms I found it depends too much on later conversion frame and commentary about the gaze; it didn’t compress as cleanly into a hand-sized parable.
- Rejected again: **Macarius helps the thief load the camel**, not for weakness but because the archive already contains that body (`## The Robbery Accomplice`).
- Left `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged. The discovered saying was cleaner than anything new I was prepared to invent this round.

  - **Abba Zosimas / the forgotten purse** (robbers empty the cell; the elder runs after them with the pouch they missed).

  - **Brian Doyle’s ruined tire confession** (brief, odd little mercy fragment).
