## 2026-03-21 — web round (Shmuel HaKatan anti-shame keeper pass)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and then pushed into a narrower **anti-shame / voluntary blame** lane after too many thief-mercy and scandal-mercy stories began collapsing into archive-duplicates.
- **Added one keeper to `Parables.txt`: `## The Eighth Seat`.** Source spine: Sanhedrin 11a, with support from Sefaria, Torah in Motion, and DafYomi outline material.
- Why it cleared the shelf: the act is instant, portable, and clean. A man in a room of status sees humiliation about to fall on an unnamed person and steps into the blast himself before the room can turn its gaze into a knife.
- Distinctive moral shape: unlike Hatim’s chosen deafness, this is not the quiet carrying of another’s bodily embarrassment over time; it is the immediate surrender of one’s own standing in a public room. Unlike the pudding / classroom mercy stories, it does not rehabilitate after shame has landed; it intercepts shame before it can name its victim.
- Strong near-miss discovered inside the same sugya-chain: **Rabbi Chiyya and the garlic**, and deeper still **Rabbi Meir writing a get** so the guilty man in the study hall would not be singled out. I left both untouched this round because the Shmuel body was cleaner and more quickly intelligible in English retelling.
- What makes the Shmuel story feel Jesus-grade: it costs almost nothing materially and almost everything socially in that moment. The mercy is not spectacle, sentiment, miracle, or punishment; it is the willing consent to be misunderstood so another person can keep his face.
- Left `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged. The discovered keeper was stronger than any original I was prepared to add tonight.


## 2026-03-21 — web round (Bankei keeper; Shichiri and Euprepius near-miss)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and pushed into a fresh **Zen / Desert Fathers / keep-the-wrongdoer-inside-the-circle** lane, trying to find mercy that does more than merely forgive after the fact.
- **Added one keeper to `Parables.txt`: `## The One He Would Not Send Away`.** Source spine: the Bankei “Right and Wrong” story, confirmed in both *The World of Zen* and *Zen Flesh, Zen Bones*, plus a stable web retelling.
- Why it cleared the shelf: the moral turn is clean and portable. The teacher does not deny the theft, excuse it, or dazzle anyone with miracle; he simply refuses to throw away the one person who most needs teaching, even when the respectable majority threatens to leave.
- Distinctive shape relative to the archive: this is not thief-mercy by gift alone (`The Thief at the Cell`, Ryokan/Shichiri-type bodies), nor enemy-mercy, nor voluntary public shame-bearing. Its kingdom weight comes from **refusing exclusion** and letting the “good” students feel the poverty of goodness without compassion.
- Strong near-miss: **Shichiri Kojun / the thief who became a disciple** (“Don’t take it all; I need some for taxes,” then “He is no thief as far as I am concerned”). Beautiful and sharp, but too close in silhouette to existing thief-transformation and candlestick-adjacent material already on the shelf.

## 2026-03-21 — web round (John the Apostle / robber-chief keeper; anti-shame controls)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and then pushed into a fresh **pursuit-of-the-lost / anti-despair / public-shame controls** lane, trying not to thicken the already-crowded thief-mercy shelf unless the new story carried a genuinely different burden.
- **Added one keeper to `Parables.txt`: `## The Horse Up the Mountain`.** Source spine: Eusebius *Church History* III.23 preserving Clement of Alexandria’s “not a tale but a narrative” about John and the young robber, with stable secondary retellings from Christian-History, Aleteia, ChurchPOP, and Taylor Marshall.
- Why it cleared the shelf: the decisive mercy is not merely forgiving a thief, feeding robbers, or refusing to expel the guilty. It is an old man going uphill after someone who has already become violent, already despaired, and already accepted a ruined identity — and offering to stand answerable for him before Christ.
- Distinctive shape relative to the archive: unlike `The One He Would Not Send Away`, this is not about keeping a sinner inside the school; unlike `The Robber Who Was Called Brother`, it is not kindness at the point of assault; unlike `The Eighth Seat`, it is not taking blame in a room. It is **costly pursuit after self-condemned wrongdoing**.
- Why it still felt shelf-worthy despite Gospel resonance: yes, it naturally brushes the lost-sheep field, but it does so by embodiment rather than imitation. The old apostle does not narrate the parable; he becomes the man inside it.
- Strong near-miss / control: **Rabbi Chiya and the garlic** — morally beautiful, but still too near `The Eighth Seat` in anti-shame silhouette.
- Strong near-miss / control: **Joseph sending the Egyptians out before revealing himself to his brothers** — luminous and likely true to the collection’s standard, but I left it untouched because it is scriptural rather than a post-biblical human parable-body.
- Strong near-miss / control: **Imam Ali and the old Christian beggar** — noble and already represented here in the old-worker / pension-mercy branch; good negative control, not a new keep.
- Left `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged. The discovered keeper was stronger than any original I was prepared to add this round.


## 2026-03-21 — web round (Ibrahim ibn Adham keeper; Honest Thief / Abu Hanifa controls)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and tried not to thicken existing lanes that were already crowded with stronger cousins.
- **Added one keeper to `Parables.txt`: `## The Mouth Made for Praise`.** Source spine: the Ibrahim ibn Adham anecdote preserved in later Sufi / devotional transmission, with stable wording in the Islam&Ihsan Bayazid page and the older Nicholson/Arberry-line archive text of *Muslim Saints and Mystics*.
- Why it cleared the shelf: the moral turn is immediate, bodily, and anti-disgust. The saint does not merely preach repentance to a ruined man; he stoops to touch the part of him everyone else would count defiled beyond tenderness, and he interprets that ruined mouth by its original purpose rather than its present filth.
- Distinctive shape relative to the archive: this is not battlefield enemy-love, not anti-shame in a courtroom or classroom, not pursuing a robber up the mountain, and not generic care for the sick. It is **reverence for a degraded person expressed through physical tenderness at the point of revulsion**.
- Strong near-miss / control: **Dostoevsky’s “An Honest Thief.”** Deeply moving, but too psychologically extended and novelistic for this shelf; the mercy diffuses across too many pages to become a hand-sized parable.
- Strong near-miss / control: **Abu Hanifa and the drunk neighbor** reappeared during the search, but the archive already has the stronger body (`The Silence in the Night`), so it functioned only as duplicate control.
- Left `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged. The discovered keeper was cleaner than any original I was prepared to add this round.

## 2026-03-21 — web round (Ramanuja keeper; universal-mercy / secrecy-cost lane)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and then pushed into a fresh **esoteric-knowledge / universal-mercy / cost-to-self** lane, looking for a story whose compassion widened access rather than merely softening judgment.
- **Added one keeper to `Parables.txt`: `## The Words from the Tower`.** Source spine: modern Hinduism Today retelling plus older Ramanuja biography witnesses from archive.org and Koyil / Srivaishnava transmission, all agreeing on the decisive act — a mantra or secret saving teaching entrusted under oath, then publicly shared out of compassion.
- Why it cleared the shelf: the moral center is not doctrinal cleverness and not rebellion for its own sake. It is a man who is told that a saving gift must remain with the worthy few, and who cannot bear to keep release hoarded while common people remain outside the circle.
- Distinctive shape relative to the archive: unlike anti-shame stories (`The Eighth Seat`, Hatim, Reb Aryeh), this does not chiefly protect embarrassment; unlike enemy-mercy stories, it is not about retaliation; unlike thief-mercy or pursuit stories, it is not about the morally fallen. It is **the refusal to keep grace scarce once you know others need it**.
- Why it still felt admissible despite religious specificity: the image is hand-sized and portable — a tower, a guarded secret, a crowd, a threatened cost, a heart unable to keep a gate narrow. The doctrinal particulars can be thinned without losing the parable-body.
- Control / near-miss lane: **Mary of Egypt / prostitute-saint hagiography sweep** — useful as a boundary check, but too penitential / legend-shaped and not a fresh keeper for this shelf.
- Control / near-miss lane: **“An Honest Thief”** remained moving but too novelistic and psychologically extended.
- Left `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged. The discovered keeper was stronger than any original I was prepared to add this round.

## 2026-03-21 — web round (Bayazid ant-home keeper; small-exile / tiny-creature lanes)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and deliberately avoided thickening the already-crowded insult-mercy, thief-mercy, and battlefield-mercy branches.
- Fresh lanes tested this round:
  - Bayazid / Abu Yazid al-Bistami and the ant carried away in black cumin or on a cloth.
  - Buddhist “young monk who saved the ants” / life-prolongation lane.
  - Creature-tenderness controls (Francis / worms, generic saint-animal material), mostly rejected as too diffuse or already shadowed by stronger cousins in the archive.
- **Added one keeper to `Parables.txt`: `## The Ant in the Bundle`.** Source spine: Bayazid al-Bistami notices that he has carried an ant away from its home and goes back to restore it. The core turn appeared in multiple witnesses, including Topbaş citing Attar and an older secondary book-page preserving the same moral body.
- Why it cleared the shelf: the mercy is almost absurdly small, and therefore searching. No one is watching, no human reputation is at stake, and the injured party cannot speak. Yet the saint treats unchosen displacement as real harm and spends miles correcting what most people would not even count as an event.
- Distinctive shape relative to the archive: there are already strong bodies here about feeding animals, relieving thirst, and seeing the holy in the despised; this story is different because it is about **restoring the unnoticed small one to its proper home**. It is less about pity than about refusing to let power make exile trivial.
- Strong near-miss / control: the Buddhist novice who saves drowning ants and thereby lengthens his own life. Beautiful compassion, but the karmic-reward frame made it feel less clean for this shelf than Bayazid’s unrewarded return.
- Strong near-miss / control: general Francis creature-tenderness pages (worms, rabbits, etc.). Honorably bright, but too scattered and not a single hand-sized moral turn.
- Left `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged. The discovered keeper felt cleaner than any original I was prepared to add this round.

## 2026-03-21 — web round (Eknath keeper; mercy-before-ritual lane)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and deliberately looked for a body that would not just thicken existing thief-mercy / anti-shame / pursued-sinner branches.
- Fresh lane tested this round: **Eknath and the holy water for Rameshwaram given to a dying donkey**.
- **Added one keeper to `Parables.txt`: `## The Water Before the Temple`.** Source spine: the older `Saints of Maharashtra` archive text preserves the full scene and line “This is my Rameshwara,” while the official Eknath mission page and Sri Sathya Sai Balvikas retelling confirm the same core act and moral.
- Why it cleared the shelf: the story is startlingly close to the “mercy before sacrifice” nerve without copying a Gospel parable. The point is not generic animal-kindness, but a vowed act of worship being judged and fulfilled by compassion on the road.
- Distinctive shape relative to the archive: unlike `The Dog and the Cup of Ghee`, this is not blessing what has been taken; unlike `The Ant in the Bundle`, it is not tiny unnoticed repair; unlike the sick-monk story, it is not care within a community. It is **choosing a thirsty life over a costly religious offering, and recognizing that as the truer offering**.
- Strong near-miss / control: Eknath’s **108 spittings** story is morally serious, but the archive already has stronger insult-endurance and anti-shame bodies.
- Strong near-miss / control: the **untouchable child** anecdote on the Eknath mission page is beautiful, but in this round it read more like equality-witness than a nearly perfect hand-sized parable.
- Left `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged. The discovered keeper was stronger than any original I was ready to add this round.


## Mar 21, 2026 (wudu / battlefield-water pass)
- Fresh duplicate-control check confirmed that the Bhai Kanhaiya battlefield-water story is already on the shelf in multiple bodies (“The Water That Chose No Side,” “The Water Skin Among the Wounded,” “The Water-Bearer Between the Lines”), so I did not add it again.
- Kept: Hasan and Husayn teaching the old man by asking him to judge between them. The moral turn is very clean: truth is preserved, but the mistaken person is not publicly diminished in order to receive it.
- Why it cleared the bar: this is not only anti-shame; it is tact in service of truth, which feels unusually Jesus-grade and portable.
- Sources were later / devotional rather than hard-early, but the anecdote’s body was stable across Sunni and Shi‘i retellings I checked.

## Mar 21, 2026 (continued — Tukaram / Metarya round)

  - Tukaram sugarcane episode from Bhaktavijaya / Abbott translation.
  - Jain Metarya / goldsmith / bird episode (reconsidered carefully; still left out this round).

- Left out: Monk Metarya. Morally serious and memorable, but the story leans harder on martyrdom / ascetic extremity than on a hand-sized turn Jesus would readily tell in the open road register.

## Mar 21, 2026 (continued — Rabbi Meir reconsidered)
- Reopened a previously logged near-miss from Sanhedrin 11a after duplicate pressure got severe.
- Kept: Rabbi Meir’s study-hall story — not just anti-shame in the abstract, but communal burden-bearing that protects a wronged woman’s future while letting the guilty man repair the harm without a spectacle.
- Reason it cleared the bar this time: unlike “The Eighth Seat,” the moral weight is not only “I will take blame so another is not exposed,” but “we will carry enough apparent blame together that repentance and release can still happen.”
- Fresh near-miss: Guru Har Rai’s broken-flower remorse. Beautiful and true, but too slight and too close in silhouette to Bayazid’s ant once the shelf is this dense.
- Also logged but did not pursue further: related Sanhedrin chain (garlic / Rabbi Chiyya) because the body felt too cousin-like to existing anti-shame entries.


## Mar 21, 2026 (continued — porters / wages round)
- Fresh lane: rabbinic “beyond the law” stories rather than saint-biographies.
- Kept: Rabba bar bar Hanan and the porters (Bava Metzia 83a) — the workers truly caused loss, but the story still refuses to leave them stripped, hungry, and unpaid; that felt like a rare clean union of justice and mercy.
- Title added to Parables.txt: “The Cloaks at Sundown.”
- Strong near-miss: Rabbi Yishmael b. Rabbi Yosei and the wood-bundle (Bava Metzia 30b). Beautiful, but more legal/ethical vignette than piercing parable-shape.
- Reason this round’s keep survives duplicate-check: not thief-mercy, not anti-shame alone, not general charity; it is mercy toward the actually at-fault laborer without denying the damage.

## Mar 21, 2026 (continued — hidden-charity / Nicholas round)
- Fresh search lane after checking the existing URL log: stories where mercy not only gives, but also refuses to turn the poor into a spectacle.
- Most results this pass were either derivative sermon material, thin “moral story” reposts, or spiritually noisy enough that I did not trust them for the main shelf.
- Kept: Saint Nicholas and the three secret gifts (retold as “The Gold Through the Window”).
- Why it cleared the bar: the act does more than solve material need; it preserves the family’s dignity, hides the giver, and lets hope arrive without humiliation. That feels unusually clean and portable in a Jesus-like register.
- Strong near-miss: St. Vitalis of Gaza. Deep mercy is there, but the method and scandal-frame felt too noisy for this collection when the standard is “would Jesus tell this?”
- Left `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged this round.

## 2026-03-21 — web round (Buddha sick monk; keeper)
- Re-opened the ledger first and deliberately avoided the already-crowded hidden-giving / debt-mercy / enemy-mercy lanes.
- **Kept:** Buddhist Vinaya story of the abandoned monk with dysentery, found fouled in his own waste because the others said he was “of no use” to them.
- Why it cleared the bar:
  - The image is unforgettable and clean: the teacher himself kneels to wash the one everyone else avoided.
  - It carries a strong Jesus-grade reversal without merely copying a Gospel parable: the forsaken, filthy, and seemingly useless person becomes the place where true nearness to the holy is proved.
  - It does not feel like a duplicate of the shelf’s existing leper / hospitality / hidden-giving stories; its edge is specifically against usefulness-as-worth.
- Added to `Parables.txt` as **“The Monk in the Corner.”**
- Left `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged.

## 2026-03-21 — web round (parents' tears keeper; prayer-shortening near-miss)
- Re-opened the URL ledger first and pushed this pass toward stories where **religious zeal is corrected by immediate mercy**, rather than toward the already-crowded hidden-giving / enemy-mercy / public-shame lanes.
- **Kept:** the hadith about the man who came to pledge himself for hijrah after leaving his parents weeping, and was told to go back and make them laugh as he had made them cry.
- Why it cleared the bar:
  - The turn is sharp and portable: a noble religious ambition is interrupted, not by laziness or fear, but by the tears of the people nearest him.
  - It feels Jesus-grade without copying a Gospel parable: the teacher refuses distant heroism when obedience first requires repairing the sorrow one has personally caused.
  - The shelf has stories about pilgrimage turning toward need, but not this exact silhouette of **zeal sent home to become filial mercy**.

## 2026-03-21 — web round (Yom Kippur cholera keeper; public scandal for life)
- Re-opened the URL ledger first and deliberately pushed this pass toward stories where **public holiness is lowered or interrupted to keep vulnerable people alive**, rather than toward the already-crowded hidden-charity / enemy-mercy / embarrassment-sharing lanes.
- **Kept:** the Rabbi Israel Salanter cholera story, in the strong traditional form where he publicly ate on Yom Kippur so frightened and scrupulous people would stop treating dangerous fasting as obedience.
- Why it cleared the bar:
  - The image is sharp and portable: bread lifted in front of the ark on the holiest fast, not out of contempt for the day, but out of reverence for life.
  - It opens a distinct silhouette on the shelf: not private exemption, but a **publicly scandalous act of mercy** that releases the weak from false piety.
  - It feels Jesus-grade because it says, in action rather than slogan, that sacred forms are for human flourishing, not human destruction.
- Added to `Parables.txt` as **“The Mouthful by the Ark.”**
- Important caution: some retellings differ on details, and at least one later source presses the story toward legend-status or softens the most dramatic version. Even so, the traditional shape is old, morally clean, and strong enough for this archive.
- Strong near-miss: the St. Francis story in which he breaks the brothers’ fast so a hungry friar can eat without embarrassment. Beautiful and close to the archive’s heart, but in the end it felt too near the shelf’s existing shared-shame / fasting-for-love cluster once the Yom Kippur story appeared.
- Left `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged.

## 2026-03-21 — web round (Korczak keeper; accompaniment unto the last road)
- Re-opened the shelf and URL ledger first, then pushed this pass toward a rarer silhouette: **remaining visibly with the vulnerable when escape is possible but rescue is not**.
- **Kept:** Janusz Korczak and the orphanage children on the road to deportation.
- Why it cleared the bar:
  - The central gesture is clean and unforgettable: he is offered ways out, but refuses to step out of line because the children are still in it.
  - This is not just courage in general and not merely martyrdom; its specific mercy is accompaniment. He cannot save them from the train, so he refuses to make them bear abandonment in addition to terror.
  - It feels Jesus-grade without copying a Gospel parable: when the little ones are led into darkness, the shepherd does not preserve himself first.
  - Distinct silhouette check: not hidden charity, not enemy-mercy, not public-shame shielding, not ritual-bent-for-the-weak, not tending the sick body; this is **presence where deliverance fails**.
- Added to `Parables.txt` as **“The Doctor Who Would Not Step Out of Line.”**
- Important caution:
  - Strong sources support that Korczak refused outside offers to escape and went with the children to the Umschlagplatz / deportation.
  - Some dramatic embellishments around banners, songs, or a German officer personally sparing him are inconsistent or contested. I deliberately avoided those in the retelling.
- Strong near-misses:
  - **Maria Skobtsova** remains morally luminous, especially the later tradition that she took another prisoner's place, but I left her out this round because the historical shape is more contested in its sharpest form and the shelf gained a cleaner image in Korczak.
  - **The poor woman whose funeral the Prophet later sought out** is beautiful dignity-for-the-hidden-poor, but too close to the archive's existing hidden-dead / neglected-grave cluster.
  - **Rantideva** is powerful, but the archive already has stronger hospitality-to-the-lowly / last-water-for-the-needy silhouettes.
- Left `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged. No original parable felt more inevitable than the keeper.

## 2026-03-21 — web round (former dignity / child-in-worship / Damien solidarity; no keeper)
- Re-opened the ledger first and deliberately avoided the already-crowded lanes of hidden charity, enemy-mercy, and burial-poverty customs.
- **No new keeper added to `Parables.txt`.**
- Strongest live candidate: **Hillel running before a poor man of good family** when no servant could be found (Ketubot 67b). What makes it interesting is the claim that mercy sometimes restores *lost social dignity*, not just calories or shelter. I left it out because, while the compassion is real, the image itself remains morally arguable in a way the shelf’s clearest pieces usually are not; it risks reading as the preservation of status rather than the rescue of a person.
- Also re-tested the **child-in-worship** lane: the Prophet carrying a child during prayer / lengthening prostration for the child on his back. Beautiful and very close to the archive’s heart, but still too near the already-logged cluster where worship bends itself around the weak and little ones.
- Also checked **Father Damien / “we lepers.”** Powerful solidarity, but after pressure-testing it against Francis and the leper, the sick monk in the corner, and Sunita, it felt too close to the archive’s existing leper/outcast identification lane.
- Left `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged. No original parable felt more inevitable than the best traditional near-misses, and none of those cleared the bar.

## 2026-03-21 — web round (starved servants / keeper)
- Re-opened the shelf and URL ledger first, then pushed this pass toward a sharper judicial silhouette: **mercy that refuses to punish downward when the powerful created the desperation**.
- **Kept:** the Umar / Hatib slaves she-camel case from *al-Muwatta*.
- Why it cleared the bar:
  - The visual turn is strong and portable: the case begins at the servants' hands but ends at the master's table.
  - This is not merely leniency toward the hungry; it is blame being traced upward to the person who made hunger do the stealing.
  - Distinct silhouette check: not hidden charity, not enemy-mercy, not embarrassment-sharing, not reforming ritual, not burial-equality. It is **judgment refusing to stop at the weakest visible offender**.
  - It feels Jesus-grade because it exposes how cruelty hides behind legal simplicity when the poor are made to carry the blame for deprivation arranged by others.
- Added to `Parables.txt` as **“The Camel and the Thin Wrists.”**
- Supporting context checked this round:
  - The Hatib-slaves report in *al-Muwatta* gives the case details: stolen she-camel, Umar's suspicion that the slaves had been starved, and his order that the owner repay heavily.
  - Additional legal context from later Sunni explanation confirms the broader famine principle: Umar did not treat such cases as ordinary theft because necessity and hardship altered the judgment.
  - A later preaching source carried a sharper aphoristic paraphrase about cutting the ruler's hand if his people came hungry or unemployed. I used it only as background pressure on the moral silhouette, not as the text-base for the keeper.
- Left `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged. No original parable felt more inevitable than the keeper.

  - Mother Teresa / baker spits in hand, then bread for the children: compact and piercing, but provenance felt folk-legend-thin for the main collection; may revisit only if a cleaner source trail appears.

  - Teacher and the stolen watch: beautiful shame-preserving mercy, but internet-fable energy was too high, and the archive already has several stronger public-shame / concealed-mercy pieces.
- Search directions used this pass: anonymous charity / dignity in Jewish sources, Sikh story collections, Mother Teresa mercy anecdotes, teacher-shame/mercy stories.

## 2026-03-21 — Web round (Turgenev / Dostoevsky / Boccaccio)
Kept (added to Parables.txt):
- “The Peasant and the Frightened Boy” (Dostoevsky, *The Peasant Marey*): one muddy hand on a terrified child’s face becomes a lifelong antidote to contempt.

Considered but did NOT keep:
- Turgenev’s “Two Rich Men” → already effectively present in archive as “The Salt They Went Without.”
- Turgenev’s “The Beggar” → already effectively present in archive as “The Beggar Who Received a Name.”
- Turgenev’s “Cabbage Soup” → piercing, but more about grief and poverty than active neighbor-love.
- Dostoevsky’s “The Heavenly Christmas Tree” → powerful, but too weighted toward heavenly compensation rather than earthly enacted mercy.
- Boccaccio’s “Federigo’s Falcon” → hospitality is real, but the courtly / reward structure blurs the kingdom sharpness.
- Tolstoy’s “Alyosha the Pot” → beautiful innocence, but more life-portrait than parable-shape.

Heuristic note:
- A recurring sign of ‘keep’ quality: a small physical gesture that carries undeserved tenderness without rhetoric. Marey’s earth-stained finger on the boy’s lips did more than several cleaner stories managed.



## 2026-03-21 — web round (Lawson / Chopin / Glaspell / StoryCorps controls; no keeper)

I still left it out because the story leans on brother-drama, mining accident, and deathbed reconciliation rather than on the cleaner, more portable mercy-gesture the main shelf now requires.
- **Kate Chopin’s “Doctor Chevalier’s Lie”** stayed morally alive for a moment: a doctor shields a dead young woman’s family from public shame by writing home a gentler account and arranging her burial. I did **not** keep it because the lie carries too much of the weight, and the story feels more like compassionate concealment in a tragic world than a hand-sized Jesus-grade parable.
- **Susan Glaspell’s “His Smile”** has a true kindness-turn — grief finally opens outward when a widow supports a sleeping child beside her — but the cinema / memory / bereavement machinery is too elaborate once radically shortened.
- The **StoryCorps / kindness-of-strangers** controls were useful mainly as boundary markers: humane, often moving, but too diffuse, biographical, or contemporary-essay-shaped to survive compression into the rare shelf-clean parable form.

## 2026-03-21 — web round (Lagerlöf bird's nest keeper)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then deliberately pushed this pass away from the archive's more exhausted thief-mercy / hidden-charity / burial-equality / simple stranger-kindness shelves and into **older literary saint-legend territory**.
- **Kept:** Selma Lagerlöf's **“The Legend of the Bird's Nest.”**
- Retold in `Parables.txt` as **“The Nest in the Raised Hand.”**
- Why it cleared the bar:
  - The central image is rare and portable: a hand raised for judgment becomes a shelter for helpless life.
  - The turn is morally deep without being clever: the hermit is not argued out of wrath; he is entrusted out of it.
  - It opens a distinct silhouette on the shelf: not hidden charity, not forgiveness of an enemy, not dignity for the shamed poor, not ritual bent around the weak, but **apocalyptic certainty undone by tenderness**.
  - It feels Jesus-grade because mercy does not merely soften the man; it changes how he imagines God.
- Near-misses / controls this round: the broader George MacDonald / Leskov / Lagerlöf literary sweeps produced humane atmosphere and isolated vivid lines, but no other hand-sized keeper with this much moral compression.
- Left `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged. No original parable I conceived felt more inevitable than the keeper.

## Mar 21, 2026 (continued — Master and Man keeper)
- New web sweep stayed away from sermon-circulation anecdotes and pushed into literary scenes with one clean moral gesture.

## 2026-03-21 — web round (Young King keeper)
Kept / added to Parables.txt:
- Oscar Wilde, *The Young King* → retold as “The Crown That Cost Too Much.” The keeper-image is unusually clean: a ruler refuses beauty once he sees the children, slaves, and miners hidden inside it. Feels Jesus-grade because it makes mercy and brotherhood outweigh ceremony, and because it refuses to let splendor be purchased with another person’s wound.

Considered but did NOT keep:
- Turgenev, “The Sparrow” — beautiful and nearly perfect in miniature, but I left it out because it centers instinctive creature-love rather than the human/neighborly mercy lane this shelf most needs.
- Oscar Wilde, “The Star-Child” — powerful repentance and repeated mercy to the leper, but too close to existing hidden-king / hidden-Christ-in-the-poor patterns already in the archive.
- O. Henry, “The Green Door” — gentle and memorable, but more romance-of-chance than kingdom-weight.

Heuristic sharpened this round:
- A story rises when its moral demand can be carried in one concrete object: a robe, a coin, a bowl, a gate, a burden, a hand.
- Social mercy can clear the bar when it becomes personal enough to stain the image itself: not “injustice exists,” but “this robe is woven out of someone else’s hunger.”

## 2026-03-21 — web round (Purun Bhagat keeper)
Kept / added to Parables.txt:
- Rudyard Kipling, *The Miracle of Purun Bhagat* → retold as **“The Holy Man and the Falling Hill.”** What cleared the bar was the shape of gratitude becoming rescue: the villagers' daily bowl becomes the holy man's storm-night repayment, and the old statesman's commanding voice returns only to wake the poor.

Why it felt shelf-distinct:
- Not merely renunciation, not merely animal-brotherhood, and not merely self-sacrifice, but **a hidden elder spending his last strength because he remembers being quietly fed**.
- The animal warning is morally alive rather than decorative: creation itself rouses the man toward neighbor-love.
- The central image is portable: a bowl left at a shrine comes back as a torch in the rain.

Considered but did NOT keep:
- Frank Stockton, **“The Griffin and the Minor Canon”** — funny, memorable, and morally bright in places, but too whimsical and structurally elaborate once compressed.
- Kipling, **“The Story of Muhammad Din”** — tender, but too entangled with colonial regret and pathos rather than enacted mercy.
- Re-check controls around **Kabuliwala** and **Idgah** only as boundary markers; both were already represented in the archive under stronger retellings.

Left `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged. No original parable I conceived this round felt more inevitable than the keeper.

## 2026-03-21 — web round (Coppée / shelter / Irish saint-legends controls; no keeper)
- Referred to `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and searched fresh lanes rather than reopening the recent legend / prose-poem controls.
- Closest near-miss: François Coppée’s **“A Piece of Bread.”** It has a real image that survives retelling — a rich officer throwing bread away, a lifelong-hungry soldier lifting it from the mud, then later dying in the officer’s place on night watch. I left it out because its force feels more memorial than parable: the story moves by wartime sentiment and remembered sacrifice rather than by one freely transferable act of mercy.
- Also checked **The Motherless Bairns, and Who Sheltered Them**. There is some life in the woman giving away the clothes of her own dead child to unknown strays, but the telling is too piously managed and diffuse to sit cleanly on this shelf.
- Opened an Irish Christian folklore lane through **Legends of Saints & Sinners**, especially contents around **Christmas Alms** / **The Lady of the Alms**, plus **A Child’s Book of Saints** as a saint-legend index. Nothing cleared the bar: most of it either leans on miracle-punishment mechanics, Christ-legend expansion, or hagiographic atmosphere rather than one hard human gesture.
- Shelf heuristic sharpened again: a story can be morally bright and still fail if its center is remembrance, wonder, or edification rather than an act another person could carry in the hand and do.

## 2026-03-21 — web round (Lu Xun keeper; Scandinavian controls)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and pushed this pass into a fresh lane of **compact literary prose with one risk-bearing act**, plus a Scandinavian control cluster to keep the standard honest.
- **Kept / added to `Parables.txt`:** Lu Xun’s **“An Incident”** → retold as **“The Rickshaw Man Who Stopped.”**
- Why it cleared the bar:
  - The act is morally exact and portable: a poor driver stops for a ragged old woman **even when leaving her would be easy, socially safer, and materially smarter**.
  - The mercy is not sentiment but exposure: he walks her toward the police gate where the blame is likely to land on him.
  - The story has a rare silhouette on this shelf: **not almsgiving, not forgiveness after injury, not hospitality, but self-endangering honesty toward a weak person who may even be troublesome**.
  - It feels Jesus-grade because it refuses the clever question “Does she deserve this?” and answers instead with costly nearness.
- Strong control / near-miss: **Bjørnson’s “The Father.”** It is concise and morally serious, and the ending—selling the farm to make a legacy for the poor in the dead son’s name—has real weight. I still left it as a control because its energy remains grief-transformation more than a hand-sized enacted mercy another person could simply go and do.
- I also checked the Scandinavian anthology path around **“When Father Brought Home the Lamp.”** Useful boundary marker, but it belongs more to technology / status / household change than to the mercy shelf.
- Left `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged. No original parable I conceived this round felt more inevitable than the keeper.

## Mar 21, 2026 (Carver / late-bread round)

## 2026-03-21 — web round (Robin Hood / pious thief / invisible child / ancient exempla controls; no keeper)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and deliberately pushed into a few lanes that did **not** simply recycle the archive’s strongest mercy clusters.
- **No new keeper added to `Parables.txt`.**
- Closest live near-miss: **The Pious Thief** (Chabad). There is real pressure in the thief begging not to be publicly ruined, and real moral interest in mercy that refuses exposure. I still left it out because the story is built around the mayor’s coercive trap and sits too close to existing candlesticks / protected-thief / dignity-mercy shapes already on the shelf.
- **Robin Hood and the poor knight** was worth checking as a control because the anti-usury / anti-predatory-religion energy is alive, but the story’s working machinery stays too rough — binding, beating, outlaw cleverness, and recovery-by-counterviolence — for a Jesus-grade retelling here.
- **Roman Charity** remained striking as an ancient exemplum, yet the moral center is filial extremity rather than a portable neighbor-mercy one could carry cleanly into this collection.
- **The Grateful Dead** motif sharpened another useful boundary: burying the abandoned dead is morally bright, but most versions lean hard on supernatural repayment, so reciprocity ends up louder than mercy.
- **Tove Jansson’s “The Invisible Child”** nearly cleared in spirit. Its deepest truth is that ordinary hospitality can restore a person who has been erased by coldness. I still held it back because the force is spread across a gradual atmosphere of care rather than one clean, hand-sized act that can survive severe compression into a shelf-parable.
- Left `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged. No original parable I conceived this round felt more inevitable than the strongest near-misses, and the near-misses still did not clear the bar.

## 2026-03-21 — web round (honest thief / dignity-service / mixed tradition controls; one keeper)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and pushed this pass into several genuinely fresh lanes rather than recircling the shelf: one **Dostoevsky** control that had somehow not yet been tested directly, one modern **dignity-through-service** article as a trust / shape control, a **Masnavi** re-opened only to confirm duplication, and a few mixed tradition / anthology leads that mostly proved too diffuse.
- **Added one new keeper to `Parables.txt`: `The Lodger Who Could Not Live Under Mercy`.** Source pressure came from Dostoevsky's **“An Honest Thief.”**
- Why this one cleared the bar: it does not depend on marvel, reward, or clever reversal; its force lives in one severe moral fact—**that a man may be reached by mercy more deeply than by accusation**. The image of the thief crawling under the bed to help search for what he himself stole is unforgettable, but the true center is later: bread and shelter become heavier than punishment, and confession ripens under undeserved kindness.
- Why it feels distinct enough from the current shelf: unlike the bishop / candle or captain / thief lanes already present, this is not conversion by grand pardon, social honor, or mistaken identity. It is smaller, sadder, and more inward: the guilty man cannot bear to keep eating under a roof he has betrayed.
- I did **not** add anything to `AI-Parables.txt` this round. Once this web keeper surfaced, the task felt cleaner if the AI shelf stayed still.

## 2026-03-21 — web round (Guardian kindness-of-strangers / train rescue keeper)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and deliberately routed away from the archive’s exhausted saint-legend / public-domain duplicate lanes into a fresher, higher-trust modern dignity series: **The Guardian’s “Kindness of strangers.”**
- **Added one new keeper to `Parables.txt`: `The Stranger Who Spoke as Kin`.** Source pressure came chiefly from the Guardian story about the young man on the near-empty train who pretended to know the trapped woman and, by invented familiarity, gave her a path out.
- Why this one cleared the bar: the central act is hand-sized, morally clean, and memorable. It is not generosity as display, nor rescue by force, nor reward machinery. It is **protective nearness plus borrowed kinship**: seeing danger, entering it calmly, and creating an exit without further exposing the vulnerable person.
- Why it feels shelf-distinct enough: the archive has many mercy stories about food, forgiveness, hidden charity, enemy-love, and restoring dignity after shame; this one is different in texture. Its wisdom is tactical without ceasing to be gentle. The helper does not conquer, accuse, or sermonize — he simply acts as though the stranger belongs to him, and that belonging itself becomes shelter.
- Useful control from the same lane: the Guardian **opera usher** story was bright and humane, especially in the way the usher offered a private seat so the wounded woman would not have to return to public humiliation. I left that one off the main shelf because it remained more pastoral-comfort than parable-pressure.
- Left `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged this round. Once the train-rescue image proved strong enough, it felt cleaner to let the AI shelf stay still.

## 2026-03-21 — web round (Guardian shame-covering lane / lifeguard towel keeper)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and deliberately routed around the archive’s exhausted public-domain duplicate lanes into a fresher modern dignity question: **which stories turn not on rescue or reward, but on the quick covering of another person’s shame?**
- Strongest candidate, and final keeper, came from the Guardian’s **Kindness of strangers** series: **“when my period arrived, a lifeguard quietly put a towel around me.”** The act is small, but morally severe: the helper sees first, moves first, says little, and refuses to let another person’s body become public entertainment.
- **Added one new keeper to `Parables.txt`: `The Boy Who Came with a Towel`.** I retold it in the collection’s register rather than as adolescent nostalgia, so the center of gravity would remain on the act itself: shame covered before laughter can form.
- Why it cleared the bar: this is not generic niceness, not romance, not reward, and not sentimentality. It is protective discretion. The helper does not expose, instruct, or linger over gratitude; he becomes a garment and then disappears. That feels very close to the collection’s best Jesus-grade logic.
- Useful controls from the same lane: the Guardian **op-shop suit** story and the **nurse hug** story were both morally alive, but less parable-tight. The first turns chiefly on affirmation by words; the second on comfort in extremity. Both were bright, neither as portable as the towel.
- Left `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged this round. Once the shame-covering image proved sturdy enough, an AI addition would only have diluted the signal.

## 2026-03-21 — web round (car-park mercy / overpayment control / one keeper)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and deliberately stayed off the most recent rescue / shame-covering grooves. This pass used fresh Guardian series paging (pp. 2–4) mainly to test whether there was a **distinct mercy-toward-fault** shape still missing from the shelf.
- **Added one new keeper to `Parables.txt`: `The Other Mother in the Car Park`.** Source pressure came from the Guardian story about the overwhelmed mother who reversed into another parent’s car and braced for deserved anger, only to be met with immediate pardon.
- Why this one cleared the bar: the act is small, morally exact, and portable. The recipient is not merely unlucky; she is actually at fault. The mercy is therefore not just help but **release** — a deliberate refusal to collect, shame, or enlarge a stranger’s burden when one has every ordinary right to do so.
- Why it feels shelf-distinct enough: the archive holds many stories of rescue, covering, enemy-love, and hospitality. This one turns on **foregoing rightful payment** in order to spare a crumpling person one more blow. It is not spectacle, not sentiment, and not transaction.
- Strong control from the same lane: the Guardian **overpaid teller / returned $20** story was honest and humane, especially because the customer spent extra time to protect a low-wage worker from bearing the loss. I left it off the main shelf because it felt more like beautiful honesty plus empathy than the severer grace of relinquished claim.
- Left `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged this round. Once the car-park pardon proved sturdy enough, an AI addition would have diluted the signal.


## 2026-03-21 — web round (judgment-relief / spoken blessing / one keeper)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and deliberately stayed off the freshest rescue / covering / debt-release shapes so I would not merely stack another adjacent Guardian anecdote beside them.
- Strongest live candidate came from the Guardian’s **Kindness of strangers** series: the crowded-train story where a young mother, already used to feeling judged in public, was nearing tears with her overtired toddler when an older woman touched her arm and said, in effect, **you are doing well**.
- **Added one new keeper to `Parables.txt`: `The Woman Who Blessed the Young Mother`.** I retold it as a story of spoken mercy rather than parenting advice. The point is not that the child became easy, but that the mother was released from silent accusation.
- Why this one cleared the bar: the act is tiny, but it is exact. No money changes hands. No spectacle is made. No problem is outwardly solved. Yet the stranger sees a person bent beneath scrutiny and puts blessing where blame was gathering. That feels close to the Jesus-like logic of *neither do I condemn you* and *come to me, all who labor* without simply copying either shape.
- Why it feels shelf-distinct enough: the archive has many strong entries on food, shelter, covering shame, rescuing the endangered, and releasing a claim. This one turns on **removing imagined judgment by a few true words**. It is mercy by recognition and benediction.
- Useful controls from the same pass: the Guardian **doorstep soup** story was generous and lovely, but too close to existing food-at-the-threshold / hidden provision lines already stronger on the shelf. The **petrol-station worker paying for fuel** story was similarly clean-hearted, but it stayed closer to small debt-help than to a newly distinct moral form.

## 2026-03-22 — Web round (comment threads / table-mercy / one keeper)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` and tried to avoid repeating the Guardian-style shame-covering lane; sampled a few Reddit small-kindness threads mainly as controls, and they mostly yielded either generic help or sentiment too thin to carry alone.
- The actual keeper came from **re-reading an already-logged Cup of Jo page** (`https://cupofjo.com/2022/04/06/kindest-things/`) and noticing an overlooked comment about a boyfriend’s stepmother at an upscale Chinese restaurant.
- **Keeper admitted:** **“The Woman Who Named the Dish.”** What cleared the bar was the exact shape of the mercy: the older woman sees class-shame rising *before* the poor girl is exposed, names a safe dish aloud, and then quietly keeps serving from that plate. It is not advice after humiliation, but rescue before humiliation ripens.
- Distinctive image: **someone at the table giving another person a safe word to say in public.**
- Why it feels shelf-worthy: it is not food-as-gift in the usual sense, nor spoken blessing, nor covering a mess. It is social mercy at the table — discreet guidance that lets the vulnerable belong without being taught in front of everyone.
- Borderline but declined this round: the ICU mother and the late sandwich, the bike-crash mothering story, and several Reddit petrol-station / milk / tissue anecdotes. Real kindness, but either too close to existing hospitality lanes or too slight to survive compression.

## 2026-03-22 — Web round (shoelace humility / one keeper)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and worked a narrow lane: very small bodily helps that remove an “impossible little burden” without fanfare.
- Fresh pages checked this round included Cup of Jo’s **11 Reader Comments on Kindness** and **What’s One Nice Thing Someone Has Done For You Recently?**; several stories were tender, but most either overlapped existing table-mercy / public-shame / roadside-comfort lanes or felt more like lovely memories than portable parables.
- **Keeper admitted:** **“The Stranger Who Knelt for the Lace.”** Source anecdote: a woman on crutches in a train station, loaded with bags, realizes her shoelace is undone; a stranger simply kneels and ties it.
- Why it cleared the bar: the act is almost comically small, yet for the sufferer it had become impossible. The stranger does not advise, pity from afar, or ask questions; he lowers himself. This gives the story a clean Jesus-like contour of lowliness meeting need at ground level.
- Distinctive image: **someone kneeling in a public thoroughfare to lift a burden that is only an inch off the floor.**
- Declined but noted: the airport mother whose shoe was tied while she wore a baby and dragged luggage (too close to the same lane once the stronger crutches version was found); the origami-crane thank-you story (beautiful reversal of the helper’s own hard mood, but more charm than kingdom-weight); and the Guardian train-intervention page resurfaced again, but that moral shape is already kept in the collection.
- No AI parable added this pass.

  - **airport / sorrow / ticket / pooled-resource threads** — several bright overlaps, none sharper than what is already on the shelf.
- **Keeper admitted:** **“The Technician Who Called Her Daughter.”**
- Why it cleared the bar: the woman is alone in a sorrow tangled with blood, fear, and private moral complication. The man who sees her does not pry, correct, or retreat; he reaches for paternal kinship and remains present when the worst news lands. That feels close to Jesus-grade moral imagination because it refuses to sort the sufferer before sheltering her.
- Distinctive image: **a medical worker bringing warm blankets and tear-cloths after first making sure an alone woman knows she is somebody's child.**
- Left `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged this round.

## 2026-03-22 (late — one-keeper round: hidden credit / mother honored)

## Mar 22, 2026 (continued — fatherhood / anti-shame pass)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and then pushed into a somewhat different lane: **StoryCorps iconic/animation pages**, with light comparison branches through **Bitter Southerner** and **Longreads** for memoir-grade moral shape.
- Strongest fresh keeper: **Patrick Haggerty / StoryCorps — “The Saint of Dry Creek.”** The contour that made it admit-worthy was not mere acceptance in the abstract, but a father refusing to weaponize shame. He names what he sees, refuses contempt, and warns his son not to live bent over as though his own life were a secret crime. That felt unusually Jesus-grade: truthful, clean, fatherly, and liberating without softness or spectacle.
- Added to `Parables.txt` as **The Father in Work Clothes**.
- Strongest near-misses:
  - **StoryCorps / “Lessons Learned”** — powerful teacher intervention shaping a Black student’s future, but too near the archive’s existing teacher-rescue / unseen-hand lane.
  - **StoryCorps / “Miss Betty’s Calling”** — morally serious vocational mercy, but too tied to institutional/political context for this shelf’s current contour work.

## 2026-03-22 — web round (White Robin keeper / mockery answered by water)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then pushed into fresh **StoryCorps / This American Life / Moth / Plough / Guideposts** lanes, concentrating on dignity, embarrassment, restitution, and stories where mercy protects or restores equal standing.
- Keeper admitted: **Jane Tyson Clement, “The White Robin.”** What finally cleared the bar was not the white-bird symbolism by itself, nor the drought/fire frame, but the morally exact turn at the end: boys who had helped turn wonder into cruelty discover that the boy they had mocked has stood with them in danger, and their repentance takes bodily form as carrying water uphill for the house that their contempt had burdened. That action survives retelling. It is not only remorse, but restitution.
- Added to `Parables.txt` as **The Water They Carried Uphill**. In the retelling I deliberately tightened away from the providential-drought contour and centered the cleaner Jesus-grade line: contempt prepares the wound, and repentance carries the weight.
- Strong near-misses / controls:
  - **StoryCorps / Herman Travis grocery deliveries** — still morally good, but remains more steady witness than one decisive parable-turn.
  - **This American Life / Nick and Laraine in “Sinatra’s 100th Birthday”** — generous and bright, but too quirky / occasion-bound for this shelf.

## 2026-03-22 — web round (Paint it Black keeper / private shame-covering)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then pushed into a fresh **Moth radio-hour / story-transcript** lane, with a light **StoryCorps archive** control pass to avoid simply re-mining the same dignity stories.
- Keeper admitted: **Rose Saia, “Paint it Black.”** What finally cleared the bar was not only that the teacher fed a hungry child, but that she refused to expose the child in order to help her. First she let the girl repaint the black so that “no one would ever know,” and then, in a second move of the same mercy, she turned her back before handing over the lunch bag. That feels rare and Jesus-grade: shame-covered mercy rather than compassion performed in public.
- Added to `Parables.txt` as **The Teacher Who Turned Her Back**. In the retelling I tightened the two linked images — the rainbow scratched from beneath black paint, and the teacher physically standing between the child and the gaze of the room.
- Strong near-misses / controls:
  - **Charlotte Mooney / “A Good Car”** — patient, tender care for a confused wandering elder, especially the tea-at-the-station ending, but it still reads more as prolonged witness than one decisive parable-turn.

- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then pushed into a fresh **Moth / This American Life / archive StoryCorps / classic-literature control** lane, looking mainly for stories where mercy creates repentance, restores equal standing, or protects a person from becoming fixed under their worst moment.

An old dispatcher with no real authority over prisoners talks with a young man in jail, likes him, buys him hamburgers, then turns him loose on trust so he can go borrow the money for his fine and return before the shift ends. When the man comes back late, he has paid the fine and even gotten the haircut the old man told him to get. ## 2026-03-23 — web round (keeper / shoes left at the door)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then worked a fresh lane through **children’s literature about want, poverty, and relinquishment**, with controls from folk material (`Sweet and Sour`), classic-short-story redemption (`A Retrieved Reformation`, already represented), and adjacent Maribeth Boelts material (`A Bike Like Sergio’s`) to keep from admitting something merely tidy.
- Added one keeper: **“The Shoes Left at the Door,”** retold from Maribeth Boelts’s **Those Shoes** (2009). What cleared the bar was the hidden, unadvertised relinquishment. The boy does not become generous because abundance has softened him; he is still poor, still aching to belong, and still feels the sting of not having what others have. Yet when he sees another child whose need runs deeper than his want, he loosens his grip and gives the coveted thing away without waiting around to be thanked. That feels rare and Jesus-grade: secret giving, no reward, no sentimentality, and a clean reversal in which the object of envy becomes a covering for a neighbor.
- Strong nonkeepers / controls this round:
  - **WBUR / “Sweet and Sour”** — lively and memorable, but too reward-shaped and folktale-mechanical for this shelf.
  - **A Bike Like Sergio’s** — ethically bright, but its center is honesty under temptation rather than mercy that actively bears another’s burden.
  - **Last Stop on Market Street** — beautiful, but finally more contemplative / communal than hand-sized parable-turn.
- Left `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged. Nothing newly conceived this round felt truer than the admitted keeper.

## 2026-03-23 — web round (no keeper / overlapmercy lane)

I took it seriously because the central mercy is not mere charity but dignifying intervention: a child keeps a friend's hunger secret as long as she can, then risks the friendship by telling an adult so the house can be fed. That is morally intelligent and genuinely tender. I still abstained because the archive already holds several stronger hidden-hunger / dignity-protecting pieces (`The Teacher Who Turned Her Back`, `The Mistake Every Day`, `The Mother’s Flowers`), and this one finally feels more like a wise social-emotional lesson than a rare hand-carried parable.

## 2026-03-23 — web round (keeper / men who did not go home)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then worked a fresh lane through **StoryCorps / lived mercy among the elderly and forgotten**, with a few controls in adjacent children’s anti-bullying / solidarity material to keep from admitting something merely sweet.
- Added one keeper: **“The Men Who Did Not Go Home,”** retold from StoryCorps’ **Maurice Rowland and Miguel Alvarez** account. What cleared the bar was the utter lack of spectacle. Ownership failed, wages failed, the institution failed, and the two lowest-status workers became the actual neighbors. They crossed job boundaries without self-display, stayed awake for people with dementia, brought movies from home for the frightened, and one of them did it consciously so the abandoned would not feel what he had once felt himself. That feels very close to Jesus-grade mercy: lowly people preserving the dignity of the forgotten at real personal cost, with no reward-shape and no sentimental varnish.
- Strong nonkeepers / controls this round:
  - **I Walk with Vanessa** — morally good and useful, but finally too solidarity-symbolic and too easy in contour for this shelf.
  - **Ronald Ruiz / the bus passenger who felt like Cinderella** — beautiful act of dignity, but too miniature and one-beat to carry as a parable beside stronger pieces.
  - **Ceceley and William Chambers / visiting seniors with memory loss** — full of wisdom (especially the child entering another’s world without correcting it), yet more contemplative lesson than hand-sized parable-turn.
- Left `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged.

## 2026-03-23 — web round (keeper / basket next door)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then worked a fresh lane through **elder-memory / child-attention / restoring personhood without sentimentality**, with a second control pass through adjacent elder-care picture books so I would not admit something merely tender.
- Added one keeper: **“The Basket He Carried Next Door,”** retold from Mem Fox’s **Wilfrid Gordon McDonald Partridge** (1984). What cleared the bar was the moral shape: the child does not conquer forgetfulness, explain it, or make himself heroic. He asks what memory is, receives small ordinary definitions, and carries ordinary objects back across the threshold until the woman is addressed again from within her own life. It feels Jesus-grade because the act is lowly, attentive, concrete, and dignity-restoring: he treats the one others speak of as already disappearing as someone still reachable through love.
- Strong nonkeepers / controls this round:
  - **A Sick Day for Amos McGee** — gentle and beautiful reciprocity, but more friendship-fable than revelatory mercy-turn.

## 2026-03-23 — web round (keeper / hole meant for light)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then worked a fresh lane through **lived-mercy audio stories and literary/picture-book stories of dignity-restoring sacrifice**, avoiding previously burned URLs where possible.
- Added one keeper: **“The Hole Meant for Light,”** retold from Jean Bell Mosley’s **“Lights for Thanksgiving.”** What cleared the bar was the story’s conversion of intent. The father begins in vanity and uplift rhetoric — brighter lamps, impressed neighbors, a visible improvement to his own house. But on the road home he discovers three darker needs: a family that cannot bring a son home, children whose sickness calls for fruit, and neighbors about to lose their land for unpaid taxes. He turns around, gives up the improvement meant for display, and spends the same money in secret on other people’s night. That feels unusually Jesus-grade: the hole remains, the room stays plain, but real light appears where burdens are lifted.
- Serious contenders this round that I left out:
  - **Hector Black / StoryCorps** — profoundly real forgiveness after his daughter’s murder, but too close in contour to existing radical-forgiveness material already on the shelf.
  - **Azim Khamisa / StoryCorps, “Carrying the Weight”** — spiritually serious and strong, especially “victims at both ends of the gun,” but again too near existing prison/forgiveness pieces for this collection.
  - **Jeff Wilson and Tammie Baird / StoryCorps** — lovely humane symmetry, but more healing anecdote than parable-shaped mercy.
  - **Brittany Bouck and Charlie Sharp / StoryCorps** — admirable accompaniment and life-changing help, yet finally more friendship/testimony than hand-carried parable.
  - **Boxes for Katje** — good and generous, but less morally sharp than the admitted keeper.
- Left `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged. I did not conceive anything this pass truer than the keeper.

## 2026-03-23 — web round (no keeper / heartbox reciprocity lane)

The line that kept it from the shelf was not the poverty or the gift but the return arc: the family’s generosity comes back around in a love box left on their own porch. ## 2026-03-23 — web round (keeper / paper feet from across the sea)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then worked a fresh lane through **children’s literature and true-story picture books of enemy-love, postwar mercy, and quiet sacrifice**, with side checks through dignity-and-homelessness books so I would not admit something merely warm or reward-shaped.
- Added one keeper: **“The Paper Feet from Across the Sea,”** retold from Lita Judge’s **_One Thousand Tracings: Healing the Wounds of World War II_** (2007). What cleared the bar was the hand-sized image and the moral cost. After the war, an American family receives tracings of children’s feet from hungry, freezing people in Germany and responds by gathering shoes and help for those who had recently been called enemy. The image is unusually clean and Jesus-grade: paper feet laid on a table until mercy becomes impossible to keep abstract. It is enemy-love without sentimentality, restitution without self-congratulation, and care directed first to the most vulnerable body-part of the defeated—the child’s cold feet.
- Strong nonkeepers / controls this round:
  - **_Boxes for Katje_** — real generosity and historical beauty, but the return gifts and pen-pal reciprocity make it feel more mutual than the rarer one-way mercy this shelf tends to keep.
  - **_The Can Man_** — morally serious and better than most classroom empathy books, yet the skateboard restoration at the end still bends the story toward reward.
  - **_A Chair for My Mother_** — durable and lovely, but finally more family resilience / communal rebuilding than revelatory mercy-turn.
  - **_The Gardener_** — beautiful hidden labor that restores a smile, though more healing-beauty than neighbor-mercy with a sharp parable edge.
  - **_Christmas Day in the Morning_** and **_Last Stop on Market Street_** remain excellent, but neither crossed the bar for this archive on this pass.
- Left `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged. I did not conceive anything this pass stronger than the admitted keeper.

## 2026-03-23 — Web round (Lu Xun / quiet mercy under class shame)
Kept and added to Parables.txt:
- “The Man Who Grew Larger in the Wind” — shaped from Lu Xun’s “An Incident” / “A Little Incident.”

Why it cleared the bar:
- The helper is low-status and gains nothing; in fact he probably invites trouble.
- The story does not flatter the hearer by saying “be nice”; it exposes the respectable bystander who wants convenience over care.
- The unforgettable image is bodily and moral at once: the rickshaw man seems to grow larger as the narrator grows ashamed.
- It shadows Samaritan territory, but not as a copy; the center of gravity is the bystander’s conversion-by-shame.

Strong near-misses this round:
- Boxes for Katje — beautiful, but too close in contour to existing postwar-shoe-relief / enemy-child mercy already preserved in “The Paper Feet from Across the Sea.”
- Sam and the Lucky Money — sincere and clean, but a bit too direct/instructional for this shelf.
- Sidewalk Story — morally serious advocacy for an evicted friend, but it reads more like social-realist chapter-book action than a compact parable.
- The Gardener / A Chair for My Mother lanes — warm and worthy, but not sharp enough in neighbor-mercy for this particular collection.

## 2026-03-23 — web round (keeper / seed in the thief’s hand)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then worked a fresh lane through **forgiveness stories in children’s literature, especially where the wronged person gives future-oriented mercy rather than merely withholding punishment**, with a comparison lane through thief-transformation stories so I would not admit something too close to material already on the shelf.
- Added one keeper: **“The Seed in the Thief’s Hand,”** retold from Edith Hope Fine’s **_Under the Lemon Moon_** (1999). What cleared the bar was the ending image. A girl’s tree is stripped and wounded; instead of exposing or shaming the thief when the tree is healed, she places a lemon in his hand and tells him to plant the seeds. That feels unusually Jesus-grade because the mercy comes from the one who was wronged, it is concrete rather than abstract, and it does not stop at pardon—it imagines fruit growing where theft had been.
- Strong near-miss this round: **Alma Flor Ada’s _The Gold Coin_**. I took it seriously because Juan the thief is changed by following Doña Josefa and finally returns the coin for a newborn in need. I left it out because the archive already carries several thief-undone-by-mercy stories, and this pass wanted the rarer contour in which the injured party herself places a future in the wrongdoer’s hand.
- Control / abstention: **How to Heal a Broken Wing** remains beautiful and morally clean, but for this shelf it reads more like compassion vignette than a hand-sized neighbor-parable.
- Left `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged.


2026-03-23 — Round 0436
Kept: Uncle Jed’s Barbershop. Why it cleared the bar: the core act is costly mercy before fulfillment, not success after perseverance. Jed empties his own shop-fund so Sarah Jean can be healed, then continues serving people who cannot pay. Family-loyalty is present, but the later free/credit labor widens it beyond kin and prevents the story from collapsing into mere private devotion.

## 2026-03-23 — web round (keeper / bear he could have taken back)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then worked a fresh lane through **poverty / homelessness picture books, child-sized relinquishment stories, and a small Willa Cather comparison lane** so I would not mistake classroom empathy for the rarer thing.
- Added one keeper: **“The Bear He Could Have Taken Back,”** retold from David McPhail’s **_The Teddy Bear_**. What finally cleared the bar was not mere generosity but the renunciation of a rightful claim. The boy has truly lost something precious, truly finds it again, and could keep it without guilt. But when he hears the homeless man’s grief, he yields the bear back because mercy has revealed a second ownership deeper than possession: comfort has already migrated. That feels unusually Jesus-grade to me—less about charity than about refusing to insist on one’s rights when love can see the poorer hand.

## 2026-03-23 — web round (keeper / calf across the sea)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then worked a fresh lane through **neighbor-care StoryCorps pieces and generosity picture books that had not yet been burned**, with a special eye toward whether any story carried *costly* mercy rather than merely tenderness.
- Added one keeper: **“The Calf He Sent Across the Sea,”** retold from Carmen Agra Deedy and Wilson Kimeli Naiyomah’s **_14 Cows for America_**. What finally cleared the bar was not abstract international goodwill but sacrifice with risk. Kimeli asked leave to give his only cow to distant sufferers after 9/11; the teacher’s guide / backmatter make clear how serious that request was in Maasai life, and that refusal could have made him an outcast. The parable-body that remains is very clean: a poor man lets a far people’s grief enter his heart so deeply that tomorrow itself must be offered.
- Why it felt distinct enough from **“The Paper Feet from Across the Sea”** to keep: that earlier keeper is enemy-child mercy through practical relief. This one is not aid to the cold but sacrificial identification with the wounded powerful from the wounded poor, and the gift is not shoes but a living source of milk, wealth, and future. The moral grain is different.
- Strong near-misses this round:
  - **_A Hat for Mrs. Goldman_** — lovely neighborly devotion and a true mitzvah story, but more affectionate perseverance than parable-grade costly mercy.
  - **“Dear Mrs. Doyle”** (StoryCorps) — unforgettable kindness to a grieving boy, but the act is too brief and low-cost for this shelf.
  - **“A Gem of a Guy”** (StoryCorps) — beautiful repetitive checking-in on elderly neighbors, but too close in contour to Herman Travis / the uphill cart keeper already preserved.
  - **_Harold Loves His Woolly Hat_** — clean relinquishment, but too slight / animal-book gentle for the weight this archive is asking.

## 2026-03-23 — web round (keeper / trees through the snow)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then worked a fresh lane through **holiday/neighbor-mercy stories and cross-difference acts of care** while actively excluding already-burned titles and any story whose goodness depended mainly on sentiment.
- Added one keeper: **“The Trees They Carried Through the Snow,”** retold from Patricia Polacco’s **_The Trees of the Dancing Goats_**. What cleared the bar was the clean structure of the act: a Jewish family in the middle of Hanukkah preparations discovers Christian neighbors bedridden with scarlet fever, cannot enjoy their own feast while the others sit in darkness, and gives away the carved wooden gifts meant for their own children in order to decorate trees and bring holiday dinners next door. The moral grain felt unusually right: mercy crossing difference without fanfare, sacrifice that is concrete but not melodramatic, and joy becoming truest precisely when it stops being private.
- Why it felt distinct enough for this archive: this is not merely generosity to the poor, nor thief-mercy, nor one more child relinquishing a coveted object to a peer. It is feast interrupted by neighboring grief, and a family discovering that celebration is false if it refuses proximity to the sick.
- Left `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged.

## 2026-03-23 — web round (keeper / family table in rough place)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and deliberately stayed off the more exhausted Tolstoy / O. Henry / saint-legend / thief-mercy shelves. This pass moved through a fresher lane of **quiet picture books about hidden restoration** (`The Gardener`, `A Sick Day for Amos McGee`, `One Green Apple`, `The Quiltmaker's Gift`) and a **precarious-neighbor StoryCorps lane**.

What cleared the bar was the distinct moral contour: an undocumented mother with children and uncertain footing does not merely hand out aid but repeatedly sets a table in a hard place until men who have been reduced to “the homeless” feel addressed by name and welcomed as family again. The strongest line in the source material is Willie’s testimony that she “didn’t make us feel homeless,” which turns the story from service into restored dignity.
- Why it felt distinct enough from Herman Travis / the uphill cart keeper to admit: Herman’s strength is weekly provision joined to respect. Yelitza’s strength is table fellowship from social precarity outward; the meal is home-cooked, holiday-shaped, family-making. It feels closer to feast-parable than service-logistics.
- Strong near-misses this round:
  - **_The Gardener_** — lovely hidden labor and beauty carried into a grim city, but in the end it still leans more toward hope and soft transformation than costly mercy.
  - **_The Quiltmaker’s Gift_** — morally bright, but too rule-based / fable-explicit; the king learns the lesson too cleanly for this shelf.
  - **_A Sick Day for Amos McGee_** — warm reciprocity, but the reciprocity is the point, which makes it gentler than the severe one-way mercy preserved here.
  - **_One Green Apple_** — humane and dignifying, though more about belonging and confidence than about one person carrying mercy to another.

What cleared the bar was not generic wartime heroism but the moral shape: repeated, hand-written, personally risky mercy carried out under explicit refusal from above. The story’s force is that he does not perform one dramatic gesture and walk away; he keeps writing until departure itself interrupts him. That feels Jesus-grade in the severe sense this shelf wants—one man spending his standing for strangers whose names he is still learning.

It is conscience at a desk, mercy against procedure, and compassion that continues even as the train is leaving.

- **Israel Goy** (Chabad): very strong hidden-charity / anti-judgment story. I still left it off the shelf this pass because the reveal is almost too cleanly exemplary; it teaches beautifully, but for this archive it felt more like an honored moral anecdote than one of the rare, inexhaustible parables.
- **The Loan** (Chabad): the rabbi who ruins a poor man by demanding repayment he had already received, then restores the man’s name by binding his own house to the wounded family. Morally alive, but the restorative marriage resolution makes the silhouette feel culturally specific and a little over-resolved for this shelf.
- **The Gift** (Portuguese converso bringing challah for God): powerful sincerity and a devastating warning against trampling childlike devotion. I did not keep it because its center is so close to already-kept “simple prayer / sincerity over polish” pieces, and because the heavenly punishment frame can eclipse the tenderness.
- **Where Love Is, There God Is Also** (Tolstoy): still great, but too directly adjacent to Matthew 25 / disguised-Christ visitation to justify adding it as a fresh non-copying parable.

Looked at and declined quickly:
- T.S. Arthur **Good-Hearted People** — too Victorian-instructional / diffuse.
- Louisa May Alcott **The Cross on the Old Church Tower** — moving, but more sentimental sketch than clean parable.
- Various publishers’ weekly / picture-book lanes — nothing rare or morally exact enough to crowd the shelf.

Search heuristic reinforced:
- Hidden mercy that protects dignity still feels like one of the best live seams.
- But many stories in that seam either end with a tidy reveal or lean too hard on explanation.
- Better next lane may be quieter acts of restitution, neighborly concealment, or mercy that costs status without staging sainthood.

## 2026-03-24 — web round (keeper / twenty rubles and the red string)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and pushed into a fresher seam: dignity-protecting mercy, hidden charity that refuses honor, and acts willing to spend status so another person can keep her joy intact.
- Added one keeper: **“The Twenty Rubles for the Trouble,”** retold from the Reb Zusha of Anipoli story (checked across DailyZohar, Ascent of Safed, and *L’Chaim Weekly*). What cleared the bar was not mere generosity but the full moral contour: he gives away his own daughter’s dowry, then willingly accepts suspicion and public disgrace so the widow does not have to stand in the unbearable light of knowing exactly what has been sacrificed for her.
- Why it feels distinct enough for the shelf: the center is not only hidden charity, but **charity that actively conceals its own cost by bearing shame**. That feels unusually Jesus-grade — not just “do good secretly,” but “let misunderstanding fall on you if that is what keeps the gift from bruising the receiver.”
- Strong near-misses / controls this round:
  - **The Samovar** (*Stories We Tell* / Reform Judaism) — warm and memorable, but too reward-shaped and wonder-shaped for this shelf.
  - **The Giving Season** (DailyGood) — useful reflection on anonymous giving and dignity, but essay-shaped rather than parable-bodied.
  - **SikhNet langar / pilgrimage lanes** (`Journey Transformed at Sri Harmandir Sahib`, `Carried down the Mountain in a Basket`, `Harmony in Diversity`) — morally alive and generous, but testimony/memoir-shaped rather than one clean hand-sized story-turn.
  - **StoryCorps dignity / service lane** (`April Garcia and Elia Moreno`; `Mafe Chase & Melissa Tiberio`) — good witness material, but no new keeper stronger than what is already on the shelf.
- Left `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged. I did not conceive anything this pass truer than the admitted keeper.


## 2026-03-24 — web round (no keeper; hidden-service / old-home / clever-rabbi lane)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and then stayed in fresh ground: a **StoryCorps hidden-service lane**, a **public-domain literary mercy lane**, and one **rabbinic quick-story / justice-vs-cleverness** check.
- No keeper added.

The image is morally clean and attractive: bringing a box of art supplies into a park, sitting with homeless people, and refusing to turn the act into self-advertisement. I still left it out because the interview never condenses into one inexhaustible hand-sized story-turn; it remains witness and ethos more than parable.

Anna March receives a sick former rival who has wandered “home,” lets her remain in the house, and says the old spite died with the old self she once resented. There is genuine mercy there, but the piece is more domestic sketch and character-portrait than portable parable; the power diffuses before the closing.

- Heuristic reinforced: I still want stories where mercy costs something inwardly — status, explanation, retaliation, or the right to be seen as right — but which do not rely on either a tidy reveal or a merely clever reversal.
