## 2026-03-24 — web round (keeper / teacher's eye; honor spent for peace)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and stayed out of the heavily mined thief-mercy / hidden-charity corridors.
- Fresh lanes this pass: (1) **rabbinic humiliation / peace-making stories** around Rabbi Meir, (2) **public-domain / Christmas duty stories** (`The Carpenter's Christmas`, `Three Young Kings`) as controls, and (3) **saintly reputation-spending mercy** via St. Vitalis of Gaza.
- Added one keeper: **“The Teacher's Eye,”** retold from the Rabbi Meir / Jerusalem Talmud Sotah story. What cleared the bar was the exact moral contour: a teacher spends his own public honor, bodily and visibly, so a woman does not have to remain shut out, publicly trapped, or broken under an ugly domestic demand. The center is not cleverness for its own sake but **honor willingly surrendered to make a path home for someone with less power**.
- Why it feels distinct enough for the shelf: it is not merely hidden charity or forgiveness of an offender. It is a rarer shape — **absorbing humiliation in order to protect another person from humiliation**. That feels sharply Jesus-grade.
- Strong near-miss: **Somebody Else** (Rabbi Dovid of Lelov beaten by mistake, then consoling the woman). Beautiful and compact, but too aphoristic / one-turn to outrank the Meir story this round.
- Strong near-miss: **St. Vitalis of Gaza**. Enormous courage and compassion, and a real willingness to lose reputation for the sake of the despised. I left it off because the legend is more exposed to hagiographic inflation, and because the prostitution frame can dominate the spiritual contour rather than concentrate it.
- Control rejected: **The Carpenter's Christmas**. Strong anti-hypocrisy energy, but more social-religious contrast story than hand-carried parable.
- Control / near-miss: **Three Young Kings**. Generous and attractive, but still more episode than inexhaustible parable.
- `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged.

## 2026-03-24 — Web search pass (no keeper; gate-lamps / bride-dignity lane)
Strongest near-miss:
- **Bava ben Buta / the lamps at the gate (Nedarim 66b).** A husband, angry and coarse, sends his wife on an errand meant to dismiss or humiliate her; through dialect confusion she breaks two lamps over the head of the sage Bava ben Buta, who responds not with wounded dignity but with blessing. What almost cleared the bar was the beautiful reversal: a public embarrassment becomes public praise because the wise man refuses to defend his own honor at her expense. I still left it off the shelf. It stands too close to the collection’s existing “honor spent to spare another’s shame” lane—especially **The Teacher’s Eye**—and the marital-obedience frame muddies the image just enough that it no longer feels rare.

Other tested near-misses:
- **Rabbi Ishmael beautifies the poor bride / gold tooth stories (Nedarim 66a–b).** Tender and morally serious: he sees beauty obscured by poverty and spends his own money to restore dignity. I did not keep it because the moral force is too entangled with marriageability, adornment, and appearance-economy to travel cleanly as a hand-sized parable.
- **Mar Ukba secret charity / oven story** was re-checked from fresh angles while sweeping this lane and confirmed as already represented here by **The Coins Under the Door**; no need to duplicate.
- Broad kindness / dignity sweeps across Sefaria sheets, Chabad shame pages, DailyGood, Plough, and Parabola yielded mostly essays, commentary, or stories requiring too much explanation after the moment itself.

Heuristic reinforced this round:
- The shelf is not merely collecting admirable stories about non-humiliation; it needs a moment that can be carried in the hand without footnotes.
- Near-miss warning sign: when the explanation is cleaner than the image, do not keep it.

## 2026-03-24 — web round (no keeper; school-silence / another-chance lane)
- Re-opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and pushed into a narrower contour: **accepting blame, or at least refusing self-vindication, so another person is not finished off by one more public blow**.
- No keeper added.

A classmate with a worse record frames him; he accepts the public scolding; later he explains that the other boy was already in trouble for many things and might have been expelled, while he himself, being blameless until then, could bear one unjust punishment. The contour is beautiful: **yielding the right to clear oneself in order to buy another person one more chance**. I still left it off the shelf because it leans just enough on the later explanation — and on explicit Christ-imitation language — that it feels slightly more exemplary biography than inexhaustible parable.
- Near-miss: **St. Gerard Majella’s silence under false accusation** (`factuk.org`; `catholictradition.org`). Morally serious, especially the refusal to damage the accuser’s reputation further, but it stands too near the archive’s existing false-accusation / honor-spent lane (Hakuin, Teacher’s Eye, etc.) to earn fresh shelf space.
- Control / resonance check: **Rabbi Sacks on Sara Kestenbaum welcoming Stephen Carter’s family** (`rabbisacks.org`). Clean and memorable, but too testimonial and essay-framed for this shelf.
- Existing archive check that mattered this round: there is already strong representation here for silent innocence, for honor surrendered to protect another from shame, and for giving a wrongdoer another future. That made the bar especially high.
- `Parables.txt` unchanged. `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged.

## 2026-03-24 — web round (keeper; lenient creditor / duplicate-control)
- Opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and deliberately stayed off the archive’s crowded anti-shame chamber.
- Kept / added: **“The Master Who Told His Servants to Wait.”** Source lane: *Sahih Muslim 1561* via `sunnah.com` and corroborating translation at `abuaminaelias.com`. What made it shelf-worthy is the moral center: mercy is exercised precisely where a person has legal leverage and social permission to press harder. The image is clean, portable, and Jesus-grade without feeling like a reheated version of the unforgiving-servant contour already known from the Gospels.
- The decisive sentence in the source tradition is that nothing notable was found in the man except that, being wealthy, he instructed his servants to show leniency to those in hardship. That is hand-sized and inexhaustible: **the hour of collection becomes the test of whether another person is to you an account or a neighbor.**
- Strong near-miss: **Abba Macarius and the pagan priest** (`patheos.com` retelling of the apophthegm). Alive and memorable, but it resolves more as a proverb-with-example about gentle speech than as a shelf-fit parable-body.
- Important duplicate-control check: **Bhai Kanhaiya serving water to both sides** reappeared while probing enemy-love lanes, but it is already in `Parables.txt` as **“The Water That Chose No Side.”** No re-admission.
- Left `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged.


## 2026-03-24 — web round (keeper; brick / duplicate-control)
- Opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and avoided the more mined lanes.
- Important duplicate-control check: **Rabba bar bar Hanan and the porters** resurfaced immediately as a strong labor-mercy story, but the archive already contains that lane as **“The Cloaks at Sundown.”** I therefore treated it as a control rather than a live candidate.
- Kept / added: **“The Brick They Could Not Fight Over.”** Source lane: Desert Fathers / *The Paradise of the Desert Fathers*, corroborated through Christ in the Desert and EWTN.
- What cleared the bar was its severe portability. Two old men try to manufacture a quarrel and fail because one of them would rather lose the object than lose the brother. That is not merely calm temperament; it is a radical refusal to let possession become identity.
- Why it feels distinct on this shelf: it is not an anti-shame story, not a forgiveness-after-injury story, and not a hidden-charity story. It opens a smaller, cleaner chamber: **yield the thing before it can ripen into enmity**.
- Jesus-grade test: the image stands near “leave your gift and be reconciled,” “turn the other cheek,” and “what does it profit to gain the world,” without copying any one Gospel parable directly. It is hand-sized, memorable, and morally exact.
- Left `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged.


## 2026-03-24 — web round (keeper; Bessarion / walked out with him)
- Opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and deliberately searched for fresh lanes where mercy is shown not by explanation but by bodily solidarity.
- Kept / added: **“The Elder Who Walked Out With Him.”** Source lane: Desert Fathers / Abba Bessarion. Core text confirmed in *The Paradise of the Desert Fathers* and echoed by secondary reflections.
- Why it cleared the bar: the action is not advice about judging, and not only inward humility. It is a man refusing to let the condemned leave alone. The story spends belonging, not money.
- Distinct chamber on the shelf: this is not mainly anti-shame, not merely forgiveness after injury, and not hidden charity. It opens a sharper room: **stand beside the expelled before you explain anything**.
- Strong near-miss / control: Abba Poemen letting a sleeping brother rest with his head on his knees. Tender and memorable, but too slight and dependent on saying-form rather than full parable-body.
- Also re-glanced at Abba Agathon’s leper/service lane as a control; morally excellent, but the archive already carries neighboring burden-bearing contours strongly enough.
- `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged.

## 2026-03-24 — web round (keeper; shortened prayer / duplicate-control)
- Opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and deliberately stayed off several crowded chambers.
- Kept / added: **“The Prayer He Shortened for the Child.”** Source lane: *Sahih al-Bukhari* 709/710 and *Sahih Muslim* 470b, with a secondary explanatory retelling at Abu Amina Elias.
- Why it cleared the bar: it is not a generic “be compassionate” saying, and not merely mercy-over-ritual in the abstract. The action happens **inside** worship. The leader hears a child cry, imagines the mother’s distress, and makes the holy act lighter rather than demanding that the vulnerable carry the cost of beautiful religion.
- Distinct chamber on the shelf: this is close to mercy-over-ritual, but it is cleaner and smaller than many of those stories. It does not abolish prayer; it perfects it by making room for the burdened within it.
- Important duplicate-control check: the Desert Fathers **“head on the knees”** lane resurfaced through a PDF discussion of Abba Poemen, but it is already present in `Parables.txt` as **“The Head on the Knees.”**
- Secondary near-miss: **Rabbi Preida and the 400 repetitions** (*Eruvin* 54b). Morally real patience, but it remained more pedagogical than parabolic for this shelf.
- Another near-miss: **carrying the granddaughter during prayer** (Bukhari 5996 / Muslim 543a). Beautiful and embodied, but too adjacent to the newly admitted chamber to keep both.
- `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged.


## 2026-03-24 — web round (no keeper; angels-voices / breakfast-for-thief / status-spent lane)
- Opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and deliberately tried a few fresh seams rather than widening the same net: **children as interruption to prayerful life**, **non-shaming correction of sexual failure**, **feeding or humanizing the wrongdoer before punishment**, and **spending status by sitting beside the disregarded**.
- No keeper added.
- Strongest near-miss: **Abba Poemen and the “angels’ voices” of children**. A few brothers wanted to move away because nearby monasteries and children’s crying disturbed their stillness; Poemen answered, “You want to get away from here because of the angels’ voices?” It is beautiful and morally exact, but I left it out because it opens a chamber already crowded by the archive’s child-in-worship / burden-within-prayer contour, especially now that `The Prayer He Shortened for the Child` is on the shelf.
- Another near-miss: **Poemen’s bottle of wine for the brother with a child**. The motion is severe and lovely — not rebuke but a celebratory gift that lets conscience awaken without public humiliation — yet the source trail I found this round was too secondary/noisy, and the chamber itself still leans close to the shelf’s existing anti-shame family.
- Another near-miss: **Mahatma Gandhi asking the caught thief whether he had eaten breakfast before speaking of punishment** (`hindupedia.com`). Strong, but too cousin-like to existing thief-mercy and prison-consolation pieces.
- Another near-miss: **Pandit Madan Mohan Malaviya sitting beside a neglected beggar woman until the crowd filled her bowl** (`hindupedia.com`). Real moral force, but it felt more like an illustration of status-spent charity than one of the shelf’s cleanest mouth-carried parables.
- Rejected as too scaffolded / supernatural for this shelf: **the Sufi moneylender’s “one good hour”** (`sufi-tavern.com`).
- Left `Parables.txt` and `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged.


## 2026-03-28 — web round (no keeper; trembling-ox / insult-gift lane)
- Opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and deliberately used it as a duplicate-control gate before deciding what lanes to reopen.

The moral motion is real and high-grade — compassion must be extended from the animal one can see to the people one has trained oneself not to see. I still declined it because the source unit functions more as philosophical persuasion than as a clean parable-body, and the exchange-of-ox-for-sheep leaves a moral aftertaste that is not quite pure enough for this shelf.

- Working conclusion: the freshest chamber probed this round was not thief-mercy or anti-shame, but **whether compassion can be extended from the seen creature/person to the unseen many**. Important morally, but nothing I found landed with the shelf’s clean, Jesus-grade portability.

## 2026-03-28 — web round (keeper; pudding child / duplicate-control Bhai Kanhaiya)
- Opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and used it as a real duplicate-control gate before widening the search.
- Kept / added: **“The Two Cups of Pudding.”** Source lane: Rabbi Aryeh Levin, preserved through Rabbi Berel Wein’s retelling and a fuller family-memory route through Rabbi Benji Levene / Jewish Action / derivative mirrors.
- Why it cleared the bar: the child is not excused, but neither is he reduced to the thing he overturned. The teacher gets the confession first, then restores him by sharing the very food around which the shame gathered. It is not permissiveness; it is table-mercy after truth.
- Distinct chamber on the shelf: **non-shaming correction of the hungry and humiliated child**. Close to anti-shame, but smaller and cleaner: authority using fellowship, not spectacle, to turn a child back toward the good.
- Strong duplicate-control / near-miss: **Bhai Kanhaiya** looked excellent in this round’s search results, but `Parables.txt` already contains that chamber (`The Water That Chose No Side` / `The Water-Bearer Between the Lines`), so I treated it as previously admitted ground rather than a live candidate.

## 2026-03-28 — web round (keeper; Ramanuja tower / Anandamayi control lane)
- Opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and deliberately worked fresh lanes rather than widening the archive’s crowded thief-mercy chambers.
- Kept / added: **“The Secret Given from the Tower.”** Source lane: Ramanuja receiving the liberating mantra under restriction, then giving it publicly from the temple tower even at the cost of his own salvation (`dlshq.org`, `encyclopedia.com`, and a Ramanuja-list retelling).

It is a man accepting personal loss so that hidden grace will not stay hidden from the poor, the lowly, and the excluded. That burden-bearing generosity feels unusually close to the shelf’s Jesus-grade chamber.

## 2026-03-28 — web round (keeper; butcher returns bride / doctor-shame lane)
- Opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and used it as an actual duplicate-control gate before widening the search.
- Kept / added: **“The Bride He Gave Back.”** Source lane: *The Butcher in Heaven* (`chabad.org`), itself attributed there to **Otzar Midrashim**.
- Why it cleared the bar: the moral center is not only generosity, but the refusal to convert rescue into claim, gratitude into pressure, or public preparation into entitlement. He lets truth interrupt his own household’s joy and pays the social cost of that interruption without bitterness.
- Distinct chamber on the shelf: **mercy that refuses to own what it has saved**. This is not merely charity, ransom, or anti-shame; it is the surrender of a morally “deserved” future when love is seen to belong elsewhere.
- Strong near-miss: **Rabbi Meir Chodosh sending visitors out so a young intern could miss the vein without humiliation** (`midnightrabbi1eligoldsmith.wordpress.com`). Beautiful and exact, but too adjacent to the shelf’s already rich anti-shame / burden-on-self-to-spare-the-other family.
- Secondary near-miss: **Two Orphans and Their Wedding Presents** (`chabad.org/kabbalah/...`). The moral movement is real, but the miracle-and-reward scaffolding sits too visibly on top of the act for this shelf.
- Another near-miss: **A Rich Man for a Day** (`chabad.org`). Humane, but it is more witness and reflection than parable-body.
- Left `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged.

## 2026-03-28 — web round (keeper; borrowed-white-garments / bikkurim-control lane)
- Opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and deliberately stayed off several already-worked thief-mercy / anti-shame chambers.
- Kept / added: **“The White Dresses No One Owned.”** Source lane: *Mishnah Ta’anit 4:8* through Sefaria’s English explanation, with Rabbi Sacks used only as a framing aid for why the custom matters morally.
- Why it cleared the bar: the mercy here is **structural, joyful, and status-lowering**. The strong do not merely give to the weak after exposure; they voluntarily dim their own display in advance so that the poor can enter the place of choosing and being chosen without being named by lack.
- Distinct chamber on the shelf: **communal joy deliberately redesigned so poverty cannot be seen on the body**. This feels different from private almsgiving, anti-shame correction, or funeral simplicity; it is a social custom made merciful at the point where desire, status, and future family life meet.
- Strong near-miss / control lane: **Bikkurim first-fruits customs** (`voices.sefaria.org/sheets/35154`; Yerushalmi / Sifrei material). Beautiful: the poor bring wicker baskets and those baskets are kept to favor them; later, one reader recites for everyone so the unlearned will not stop coming in shame. I still left it out because it felt more procedural and less hand-sized than the vineyard / borrowed-garment chamber.
- Duplicate-control thought: I compared this keeper against the already-admitted **“The Linen at the Last Door.”** Both are communal anti-shame customs, but this one opens a sufficiently different room: not mourning, but joy; not death, but courtship; not the end of a house, but the possible beginning of one.
- `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged.

- Distinct chamber on the shelf: **refusing to speak to anyone as though they were spiritually finished**. Close to dignity and mercy, but not the same as secret charity, anti-shame design, or forgiveness after repentance. This one honors the hidden possibility *before* change is visible.

I still left it out because, this round, it felt more procedural and less portable than the Never Disparaging chamber.

## 2026-03-28 — web round (keeper; weary-man / ungrateful-monkey-control lane)

- Distinct chamber on the shelf: **honoring the hostile outsider as tired and human enough to greet, and thereby turning hostility into shared burden-bearing**. The pagan does not merely soften; he helps carry the wounded disciple whom he had just beaten.

## 2026-03-28 — web round (keeper; Yudhishthira-dog / Marina-Neminath control lanes)
- Opened `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and tried to stay off the archive’s crowded thief-mercy, anti-shame, and child-shame chambers.

- Why it cleared the bar: the moral turn happens **before** the reveal. He does not know the dog is anything more than a lowly creature that trusted him. He is willing to stand outside reward rather than enter a joy purchased by abandonment.
- Distinct chamber on the shelf: **solidarity with the least at the very threshold of reward**. Not kindness to an animal in passing, not rescue, not anti-shame, not forgiveness after repentance: this is refusing a heaven that would train the heart to step over the faithful weak.

High compassion, but the bride-cost / renunciation frame left a cleaner but less portable residue than Yudhishthira’s refusal-at-the-gate.

## 2026-05-13 — web round (keeper; blind-honor / reedcutter-secret-tzedakah controls)
- Re-opened the current archive first and checked for existing blind-man / anti-shame / Rabbi Eliezer ben Yaakov material before admitting anything.

## 2026-05-13 — web round (keeper; hidden-clinic payment box / Asanga-Poemen controls)
- Re-opened the current archive first and checked for existing Abba Umna / Abba the Bloodletter / doctor-payment-box material; I did not find this chamber already admitted.
- **Kept / added:** “The Box He Would Not Open,” from the Abba Umna / Abba the Bloodletter passage in Taanit 21b, checked through the Sefaria sheet / Soncino-derived translation and a newer Ezra Brand collation of the passage.
- Why it cleared the bar: this is not merely giving free care, but arranging the room so poverty does not have to identify itself even to the healer. The bell rings for payer and non-payer alike; the mercy protects bodily modesty, economic dignity, and recovery after treatment.
- Distinct chamber on the shelf: **self-blinding mercy in the healing room** — choosing not to know the measure of another person’s need, so help can be received without a hidden debt of shame. This is adjacent to hidden charity, but different: the giver protects the receiver from the giver’s own knowledge.
- Strong near-miss / control lane: **Asanga and the wounded dog / maggots / Maitreya reveal**. It carries fierce compassion for repulsive suffering, but I left it out because the graphic self-injury, extreme ascetic shape, and miracle-reveal make it less clean and potentially less safe as a hand-sized kindness parable.
- Strong near-miss / control lane: **Abba Poemen: do twice as much good for the brother called a sinner, “for he is sick.”** Morally true, but too close to already-kept mercy-to-the-fallen / keep-the-wrongdoer-inside chambers (`The One He Would Not Send Away`, Bessarion/cast-out variants, `The Head on the Knees`).

## 2026-05-13 — web round (keeper; garland-around-neck / Acacius-Salanter-Bayazid controls)
- Re-opened the current archive and checked for Puran Singh / Piara / Pingalwara / garland language before admitting anything; I did not find this chamber already represented.
- **Kept / added:** “The Garland Around His Neck,” from the Bhagat Puran Singh and Piara Singh tradition, checked through the official Pingalwara biography, SikhChic’s “Puran and Piara” account, and Discover Sikhism’s Pingalwara page.
- Why it cleared the bar: the sharp moral hinge is not ordinary almsgiving. The source tradition explicitly says many were ready to feed the abandoned disabled child, but no one came forward to take him under care. Puran Singh answers the harder question: not “Will I give something?” but “Will I let this person belong to my life?”
- Distinct chamber on the shelf: **mercy as lifelong carrying / making family when society offers food without belonging**. Close to abandoned-child and sick-room entries, but the “garland around my neck” image and the refusal to stop at feeding opened a fresh enough room.
- Strong near-miss / control lane: **Acacius of Amida selling church vessels to ransom Persian captives** (`newadvent.org`, `catholiclibrary.org`, `wisdomlib.org`). Very Jesus-compatible — God does not need cups while prisoners need bread and freedom — but it remained close to the archive’s sacred-object-becomes-bread and institutional enemy-mercy clusters.
- Strong near-miss / duplicate-control lane: **Rabbi Yisrael Salanter missing Kol Nidre for the crying child**. Beautiful, but too close to `The Prayer He Shortened for the Child`, and the archive already has the Salanter cholera/Yom Kippur chamber.
- Secondary near-miss: **Bayazid Bastami standing with water for his sleeping mother**. Tender and memorable, but more filial-devotion than neighbor-mercy, and less rare than the Puran/Piara image.
- `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged.

## 2026-05-13 — web round (keeper; Woolman hidden-wages / Elizabeth Fry-Sufi-Plough controls)
- Re-opened the current archive and search log first, then tried a fresh Quaker / prison-reform / Sufi-thief / Plough-broad-mercy pass rather than returning to the newly crowded anti-shame bread and carried-child lanes.
- **Kept / added:** “The Guest Who Would Not Enter Free,” based on John Woolman’s practice of refusing to receive slaveholding hospitality as a free gift: he insisted on paying the enslaved person or the host for the service, and one London Grove story has him asking whether the man at the door was enslaved, then declining the hospitality and going on foot.
- Why it cleared the bar: the act makes hidden cost visible without spectacle. The moral center is not generalized abolitionist argument, but the tiny door-threshold refusal: friendship, politeness, and comfort must not be allowed to rename stolen labor as hospitality.
- Distinct chamber on the shelf: **refusing a gift whose sweetness depends on an unseen neighbor’s bondage**. Close to exploitation-revealing stories like `The Crown That Cost Too Much`, but more intimate and actionable: one guest, one threshold, one servant whose wages must not disappear.
- Strong near-miss / control lane: **Elizabeth Fry at Newgate**. The prisoners voting rules / choosing a schoolmistress / learning paid sewing are deeply humane, but the material stayed biography-and-reform shaped rather than a single hand-sized parable this pass.
- Other controls not kept: Sufi thief/saint lanes were too close to already-rich thief-mercy clusters; broad Plough searches were mostly essays, PDFs, or already-canonical Gospel discussion; Dhu'l-Nun / Rumi hospital lanes were more mystical teaching than plain enacted kindness.
- `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged.


## 2026-05-13 — web round (keeper; shared-name POW formation / Emmaus-Sousa controls)
- Re-opened the current archive and URL ledger first, then moved away from the recent dignity/hidden-giving lane into rescue-solidarity under state violence: Roddie Edmonds, Abbé Pierre / Emmaus origin, and Sousa Mendes / Rabbi Kruger controls.
- **Kept / added:** “The Name They All Took,” based on Master Sgt. Roddie Edmonds at Stalag IXA, corroborated through the National WWII Museum, Jewish Foundation for the Righteous, Yad Vashem-linked testimony pages, and Army University Press. The living image is the formation: when the persecutor asked the endangered group to separate itself for harm, the whole body stood together and the leader answered with the hunted name.
- Why it cleared the bar: this is not only bravery, and not only rescue paperwork. It is neighbor-love by shared identity at the exact point where hatred demands classification. The non-Jewish soldiers protect the Jewish soldiers not by hiding them as objects, but by belonging to them in public under threat.
- Distinct chamber on the shelf: **taking the name of the hunted so the hunted cannot be isolated**. It is adjacent to Sugihara / Korczak / Yudhishthira-type wartime and threshold solidarity, but the moral mechanics are different: the endangered are saved because everyone refuses the divider’s categories.
- Control not kept: **Abbé Pierre / Georges Legay / Emmaus origin**. The phrase “help me help others” remains morally serious because it gives a despairing man usefulness rather than only pity, but current allegations around Abbé Pierre make this a poor candidate for a Jesus-grade shelf unless treated with unusually careful distancing; the archive also already has several despair / restore-purpose lanes.
- Control not kept: **Sousa Mendes / Rabbi Chaim Kruger refusing a private visa unless the crowd received help too**. The refusal-to-be-saved-alone is beautiful, but it overlaps strongly with both the existing Sugihara papers chamber and the newly added / recently added solidarity-at-the-threshold stories.
- `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged.

## 2026-05-13 — web round (keeper; Eglantyne Jebb enemy-children fine / Edhi-Geulen controls)
- Re-opened the current archive and URL ledger first, then deliberately moved across several less-recently used lanes: Edhi / Bilquis Edhi baby cradles, Irena Sendler jars and identity records, Moshe Leib of Sassov love-and-need teachings, Desmond Tutu / Trevor Huddleston dignity stories, Andrée Geulen's teacher-rescue lane, and Eglantyne Jebb / Save the Children.
- **Kept / added:** “The Fine That Became Bread,” based on Eglantyne Jebb's 1919 arrest and trial for distributing famine leaflets about starving children in former enemy countries after World War I. The source spine is especially strong in Save the Children's own history and Clare Mulley's account: Jebb was found guilty, but the crown prosecutor paid or offered to pay the £5 fine; the fine then becomes part of the founding-money image for Save the Children.
- Why it cleared the bar: the moral turn is not generic humanitarian activism. A child is still being called an enemy after the war, the law punishes the person who shows the child's hunger, and then one officer of that same law quietly places the fine back into mercy's hand. The story has a compact parable engine: **the penalty for telling the truth about hungry enemy-children becomes bread for hungry enemy-children**.
- Distinct chamber on the shelf: adjacent to enemy-love / war-rescue keepers, but different from Roddie Edmonds' shared-name formation, Bhai Kanhaiya's battlefield water, and Sugihara's papers. Here the mercy is public truth and postwar provision for the children of the defeated, not immediate protection at a checkpoint or battlefield.
- Strong near-miss: **Edhi / Bilquis Edhi jhoolas**. The cradle with a no-questions path for unwanted infants is morally luminous and may deserve a future focused pass; I left it out here because this round's Eglantyne image had the cleaner single reversal, and Edhi's institutional mercy may need a more careful standalone pressure-test against existing anonymity / child-protection / anti-shame chambers.
- Other controls not kept: Irena Sendler's jars are moving but overlap the archive's wartime children-rescue and preserved-identity lanes, with public retellings also prone to inflated-number simplification; Moshe Leib of Sassov's “if you love me, know what hurts me” is a profound teaching but more aphoristic than enacted kindness; Tutu / Huddleston's hat story is beautiful but near the newly added public-honor / seat-below lane and carries some later biography-complexity; Andrée Geulen's aprons / “war on Jewish children” retort is strong but too close to the just-added shared-name / marked-children solidarity chamber.
- `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged.

## 2026-05-13 — web round (keeper; Akiva office / guide-dog-AbuHanifa-Vincent controls)
- Re-opened the current archive and URL ledger first, then pushed away from the recent war-mercy / hidden-wages / secret-dignity lanes into **care that preserves agency**: guide-dog access to synagogue as a control, Rav Shlomo Zalman Auerbach child-dignity stories, Abu Hanifa duplicate-control, and a quick Vincent de Paul galley-slave reliability check.
- **Kept / added:** “The Office He Gave the Child,” from the Rav Shlomo Zalman Auerbach / Akiva tradition preserved in Jewish Action, Imprimis, and related Teller-retelling routes. The parents were choosing a residential institution for their developmentally disabled son without having asked him; the teacher first insists that the child has a soul and voice, then gives the child a role as his representative in the new home.
- Why it cleared the bar: the mercy is not simply kindness to a vulnerable child. It refuses to let necessary care become a quiet exile. The teacher spends his own public honor to send the child away with office, dignity, and purpose rather than as a problem transferred between buildings.
- Distinct chamber on the shelf: **care that asks the person’s heart and gives a vocation where others would only make arrangements**. It is adjacent to public-honor and disability-dignity entries, but the shape is fresh enough: not a seat, a gift, or a rescue, but an entrusted office.
- Near-miss / control: **guide dogs in synagogue / Western Wall access**. Morally important and potentially future-useful, but this pass produced mostly halachic access discussion rather than one enacted, mouth-carried mercy story.
- Duplicate-control: **Abu Hanifa and the drunk neighbor** reappeared, but the archive already has it as “The Silence in the Night.”
- Reliability-control: **Vincent de Paul substituting himself for a galley slave** was not kept; at least one parish summary notes that thorough biographers treat the famous substitution story as legend, and the stronger sourced material is broader ministry rather than a compact parable-turn.
- Other Auerbach near-miss: the widow’s milk crates are beautiful hidden labor, but too near the archive’s hidden-service / unseen-burden chambers.
- `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged.
## 2026-05-13 — web round (keeper; Dorothy Day diamond-ring beauty / John XXIII prison-visit control)
- Re-opened the current archive and URL ledger first, then moved into a **beauty / agency for the poor** lane rather than another anti-shame bread, hidden-wages, war-solidarity, or child-voice lane. Also checked a Pope John XXIII prison-visit control lane.
- **Kept / added:** “The Ring She Did Not Sell,” based on the Dorothy Day / Catholic Worker diamond-ring story reported through Jim Forest and Tom Cornell channels: a donor gave Day a diamond ring, and Day gave it directly to a difficult poor woman rather than converting it into efficiently managed rent money.
- Why it cleared the bar: this is not mere extravagance or bad stewardship. The parable-turn is that mercy refuses to reduce a poor person to a schedule of necessities; the woman may sell, wear, enjoy, or choose. The poor are not just mouths to be fed but souls allowed beauty and agency.
- Distinct chamber on the shelf: **unmanaged beauty placed into a poor person's hand without demanding utility, gratitude, or permission from the respectable.** It is adjacent to dignity and anti-shame entries, but the moral shape is different: charity does not seize control of the recipient's joy in the name of efficiency.
- Control not kept: **Pope John XXIII at Regina Coeli prison** (“you could not come to me, so I came to you”). Beautiful and Gospel-shaped, but in this archive it felt closer to existing prisoner-visitation / authority-descends / mercy-goes-to-the-confined chambers, and it lives mainly as one pastoral line rather than a fully fresh parable body.
- `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged.
## 2026-05-13 — web round (keeper; Parihaka bread-for-soldiers / Ruth-Burks-Haining controls)
- Re-opened `rev0489` and searched three lanes: modern AIDS bedside/burial mercy (Ruth Coker Burks), Holocaust school-presence fidelity (Jane Haining), and indigenous nonviolent enemy-welcome at Parihaka.
- **Kept / added:** “The Bread for the Soldiers,” based on the 5 November 1881 invasion of Parihaka, where an armed colonial force came expecting resistance and was met by singing children and women with warm bread.
- Why it cleared the bar: the moral image is unusually severe and clean: not private charity, not heroic rescue, but a community refusing to let violence dictate its likeness. The bread does not stop the plunder; it witnesses that the power to wound is not the power to define the wounded.
- Distinct chamber on the shelf: **enemy-welcome under armed invasion, where hospitality is maintained without pretending injustice is harmless.** It complements but does not repeat “enemy children fine bread” or “shared-name formation.”
- Control not kept: Ruth Coker Burks has an intensely moving “mistaken mother / bedside abandonment” core, but several current sources document significant contestation around the public legacy and cemetery narrative; not clean enough for this archive today.
- Control not kept: Jane Haining’s “days of sunshine / days of darkness” is very strong, but after the recent Holocaust solidarity keeper it felt less parable-shaped than Parihaka’s warm-bread-before-rifles image.
- `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged.

## 2026-05-13 — web round (keeper; Edhi/Bilquis cradle-before-blame / Kolbe-Tetsugen controls)

- **Kept / added:** “The Cradle Before Blame,” based on the Edhi / Bilquis Edhi jhoola project. The official Edhi page confirms cradles outside Edhi centers for abandoned newborns and shelter/medical care before adoption; Dawn and ThePrint preserve the sharper sign / image: do not kill the child; leave the child in the cradle.
- Why it cleared the bar: the act does not wait until a frightened person is honest, respectable, named, or safe to meet in daylight. It builds a place where shame can hand a child to mercy without first surviving accusation. That is a distinct and very clean chamber: **rescue made available before blame, so the smallest life is not lost while adults argue about guilt**.
- Control not kept: **Maximilian Kolbe**. The substitution itself is morally luminous and strongly sourced through Auschwitz and Vatican accounts, but it is already famous at saint-exemplum scale and the broader public biography has enough contested anti-Jewish-material discussion that I chose not to add it in this pass.
- Control not kept: **Tetsugen / the invisible sutras**. Beautiful and highly parable-like, but it sits close to the shelf’s existing mercy-over-ritual / holy-object-becomes-life chambers (`The Water Before the Temple`, `The Mouthful by the Ark`, and the Acacius control lane). It remains a good future control, not a keeper today.

## 2026-05-14 — web round (keeper; Keshia Thomas body-roof / Abd el-Kader control)
- Re-opened `rev0491`, checked the current keeper shelf and URL ledger, then searched two lanes that were not simply hidden-giving or dignity-anonymity: Keshia Thomas at the 1996 Ann Arbor anti-KKK rally, and Emir Abd el-Kader's protection of Christians during the 1860 Damascus massacre.
- **Kept / added:** “The Body She Made a Roof,” based on Keshia Thomas, who at eighteen threw herself over a fallen man wearing Confederate / SS symbols while anti-KKK protesters were beating him. The source spine is especially stable across the BBC/Journey to Justice account, Michigan Public's local recap, the Congressional Record tribute from days after the event, and Mark Brunner's photo-page note.
- Why it cleared the bar: the moral turn is not vague tolerance. It is a body placed between a mob and a hated enemy at the exact moment when righteous anger is about to borrow hatred's hands. The story preserves protest against evil while refusing to let the protest become the thing it condemns.
- Distinct chamber on the shelf: **enemy-shelter from one's own side's violence**. It is adjacent to love-of-enemy and shared-name solidarity entries, but its shape is fresh: a single person becomes a roof over an exposed enemy before the crowd can turn justice into blood.
- Strong control not kept: **Emir Abd el-Kader / Damascus 1860**. Very serious and possibly future-worthy, especially the Muslim leader opening his house and using his armed following to protect Christians. I left it out this pass because the event is larger, more political, and less hand-sized than Keshia's single bodily image; it also needs careful retelling that honors the many other Damascene Muslims who sheltered Christians rather than letting one hero absorb the whole mercy.
- `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged.

## 2026-05-15 — web round (keeper; Four Chaplains last-jacket / Hugh Thompson control)
- Re-opened `rev0492`, checked `Parables.txt` / `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` for chaplains, Dorchester, life jackets, and Hugh Thompson / My Lai; none of those exact chambers were already present.
- **Kept / added:** “The Jackets They Took Off,” based on the Four Chaplains aboard the USAT Dorchester: George L. Fox, Alexander D. Goode, Clark V. Poling, and John P. Washington calmly distributed life jackets, removed their own when the supply ran out, and were last seen joined together on the sinking deck.
- Why it cleared the bar: the image is simple, severe, and non-transactional. Each gives away not surplus but the object saying “you may live,” and they do not distribute it by tribe, denomination, rank, or deservingness—only by the next need in front of them.
- Distinct chamber on the shelf: **mercy as surrendering one’s own last survival-place to the next frightened neighbor, without asking which prayer he prays.** It is adjacent to self-giving and wartime rescue, but cleaner and more liturgical than bureaucratic rescue, more universal than in-group courage, and less revenge/atrocity-shaped than many war stories.
- Strong control not kept this pass: **Hugh Thompson Jr. / My Lai**. This is a very serious near-keeper: a pilot landing his helicopter between civilians and his own side’s soldiers, then arranging evacuation and reporting the killings. I held it back here because the newly added Four Chaplains story opened a fresher shelf-space with less dependence on atrocity detail, while the Thompson lane sits closer to the very recent Keshia Thomas body-shield entry and the archive’s existing battlefield/enemy-mercy cluster.
- Other broad search results around Good Samaritan commentary / generic moral-courage posts were logged only as boundary noise, not keeper material.
- `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged.

## 2026-05-15 — web round (keeper; Mori enemy-dead names / O’Flaherty-O’Connell-Hugh controls)
- Re-opened `rev0493`, checked the current shelf and URL ledger, then pushed into several less-safe / less-used lanes: postwar mercy to an enemy jailer (Hugh O’Flaherty / Herbert Kappler), homeless medicine learned from the feet upward (Jim O’Connell), Corrie ten Boom’s former-guard handshake, Albanian Besa, Desmond Doss’s “one more,” the Hugh Thompson / Mỹ Lai helicopter line, and Shigeaki Mori’s Hiroshima POW memorial work.
- **Kept / added:** “The Marker in the Ashes,” based on Shigeaki Mori, a Hiroshima survivor who spent decades identifying American POWs killed in the atomic bombing, contacting their families, and helping create a memorial marker for them in Hiroshima.
- Why it cleared the bar: the moral center is not generic reconciliation or national apology. It is a wounded person refusing to let grief become tribal property. Mori does not minimize Hiroshima’s suffering; he enlarges the circle of remembered suffering until even the enemy sons are named and their families are given a known sorrow.
- Distinct chamber on the shelf: **mercy as truthful remembrance for the enemy dead**, where memory itself becomes an act of neighbor-love. This differs from rescue, forgiveness of a living offender, hidden charity, or bodily shielding.
- Controls not kept: **Hugh O’Flaherty / Kappler** is profound but conversion-of-war-criminal terrain is complex and sits near existing enemy-prisoner forgiveness; **Jim O’Connell foot-soaking** is beautiful but the archive’s homeless-care and footwashing-adjacent lanes are already thick; **Corrie ten Boom’s handshake** is strong but the forgiveness-of-injurer shelf is already well represented; **Albanian Besa** is morally serious but too broad/communal for this pass; **Desmond Doss** is stirring but too near wartime heroic rescue and the just-added Four Chaplains; **Hugh Thompson** remains an extremely serious near-keeper, but I held it back again because the atrocity frame and “body/helicopter between killers and victims” silhouette sit near the recent Keshia Thomas entry.
- `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged.

## 2026-05-15 — web round (keeper; Assisi protected-prayer / Camillus-LeChambon-Bartali controls)

- **Kept / added:** “The Fast They Guarded,” based on the Assisi rescue network during the Nazi occupation of Italy: Jews were hidden in convents and monasteries, religious objects were protected, and the Yad Vashem / Gilbert strand preserves the 1943 Yom Kippur image of nuns preparing the meal to end the fast.
- Why it cleared the bar: this is not merely hiding endangered bodies or forging rescue papers. The sisters and clergy made room for the hidden ones to remain themselves before God. The rare moral center is **shelter that protects another person’s covenant rather than absorbing the rescued person into the rescuer’s identity**.
- Distinct chamber on the shelf: **protecting the neighbor’s prayer / guarding the inner sanctuary of the endangered.** It complements the archive’s rescue and interfaith hospitality entries without simply repeating them: mercy does not rescue in order to possess.
- Control not kept: **Camillus and the Falerian schoolmaster.** The refusal to win by betrayed children is morally serious, but the punitive beating element and Roman virtue-frame made it feel less cleanly Jesus-grade for this collection today.
- Control not kept: **Le Chambon / “we only know men.”** Deeply worthy, but the quote and communal rescue pattern overlap strongly with the Roddie Edmonds shared-name chamber and the Besa / broad rescue controls.
- Control not kept: **Madeleine Dreyfus / Garel network child transports.** Worthy and courageous, but more biography/rescue-network shaped than one fresh parable image for this pass.
- Control not kept: **Gino Bartali.** Strong bicycle-documents image, but crowded with rescue-paper stories and historically contested enough in recent coverage to avoid using as a keeper here.
- Broad kindness / parable-list searches were logged as noise; no other candidate cleared the threshold.

## 2026-05-15 — web round (keeper; David Tasma window / Shabbat-Geel-Marian controls)
- Re-opened `rev0495`, checked the latest keeper shelf and URL ledger, and deliberately moved away from the recent enemy-mercy / public-solidarity / hidden-charity cluster.
- **Kept / added:** “The House Around the Window,” based on Cicely Saunders and David Tasma. The source spine is unusually tender and stable: Saunders met Tasma while training as a medical social worker; he was a dying Polish-Jewish cancer patient; they discussed a different kind of home for people near death; he left her £500 and the phrase “I will be a window in your home”; she later installed a David Tasma window at St Christopher’s Hospice.
- Why it cleared the bar: the dying man is not treated as merely a recipient of care. Mercy listens until it discovers that even a person near death may still be a giver, founder, and window for future strangers. The caregiver’s kindness is not just bedside consolation but the long obedience of building around the gift hidden in the one she served.
- Distinct chamber on the shelf: **honoring the agency of the dying so completely that their final gift becomes architecture for the living.** This differs from ordinary hospice kindness, grief-comfort, and deathbed witness entries; it is mutual mercy at the edge of death.
- Strong control not kept: **Matthew Stevenson / Derek Black Shabbat dinners.** Morally serious and possibly future-worthy: an Orthodox Jewish student invites a white nationalist to his Shabbat table, while friends keep truth and relationship in the same room. I held it back because the archive already carries strong enemy-racist mercy shapes (`The Body She Made a Roof`) and table-hospitality shapes, and because this story needs careful guardrails against implying that targets of hate are obliged to convert haters by proximity.
- Strong control not kept: **Geel, Belgium community psychiatric foster-care tradition.** Deeply interesting, with a rare “not patients but household boarders” moral center. I left it out because it is a broad social practice with mixed history and caveats rather than one hand-sized act; it may deserve a future pass if a cleaner individual episode emerges.
- Controls not kept: **Marian Anderson / Eleanor Roosevelt / Lincoln Memorial** is a powerful dignity-stage story but more public civil-rights emblem than parable-body; **Edith Cavell** is luminous but overlaps the wartime rescue / no-hatred cluster; **Polycarp feeding arresting soldiers** is beautiful but too close to arrest/martyrdom hagiography and the recent bread-for-soldiers lane; **St Lawrence** was already logged and rejected as too emblematic; **Abdul Ghaffar Khan / Khudai Khidmatgar** is morally serious but too movement-scale for this pass.
- `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged.

## 2026-05-15 — web round (keeper; Friedl Dicker-Brandeis suitcases / stonecatcher control)
- Re-opened `rev0496`, checked the keeper shelf and URL ledger for Friedl / Dicker-Brandeis / Terezín / children's drawings / suitcase terms, and found no existing admitted chamber.
- **Kept / added:** “The Suitcases She Hid,” based on Friedl Dicker-Brandeis in the Terezín ghetto. The source spine is stable enough for the shelf: she taught drawing classes to children in Terezín; the Jewish Museum in Prague holds thousands of their drawings; and before her deportation/murder at Auschwitz she gathered thousands of drawings into two suitcases and hid them in the ghetto.
- Why it cleared the bar: the mercy is not merely “art helps children” or “teacher comforts students.” The rare parable-image is a doomed teacher guarding the inner witness of children when she cannot physically rescue them. The suitcases say: the murderer may take breath, but mercy can still refuse erasure.
- Distinct chamber on the shelf: **preserving the voice, name, and inward world of children when rescue is no longer in one’s power.** It differs from hidden charity, wartime rescue papers, enemy mercy, hospice agency, and protected-prayer: here the protected thing is the child’s testimony and imagination.
- Strong control not kept: **Bryan Stevenson’s stonecatcher courtroom woman** from `Just Mercy`. It is morally serious and could have been retold as a woman whose murdered grandson’s pain becomes a ministry of comfort to both victims’ families and accused families. I left it out because the “stonecatcher” frame is explicitly derived from the Gospel stoning scene and therefore sits too close to direct Jesus-scene re-use for this project’s “not copying old parables directly” rule.
- `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged.

## 2026-05-15 — web round (keeper; Adadevoh closed-door public-health mercy / Phule-Salk-Banting-Naoto-Maharaja controls)
- Re-opened `rev0497`, checked the keeper shelf and URL ledger, and deliberately hunted for a moral shape that was not another rescue-paper, hidden-gift, enemy-bread, or preserved-child-witness entry.
- Fresh lanes searched: Savitribai / Jyotirao Phule extra sari and open-well material; Jonas Salk and the “patent the sun” polio vaccine lane; Banting / Best / Collip insulin patent rights for one dollar; Naoto Matsumura and the Fukushima abandoned-animal care lane; the Good Maharaja / Balachadi Polish children; and Dr. Ameyo Stella Adadevoh’s Ebola containment in Lagos.
- **Kept / added:** “The Door She Kept Closed,” based on Dr. Ameyo Stella Adadevoh refusing, under threats and pressure, to discharge Nigeria’s index Ebola patient in 2014 while she and her team cared for him.
- Why it cleared the bar: this story adds a rare shelf-space: **mercy as a protective boundary.** Most kindness stories open the door, invite in, or give away; this one says that love may also hold a dangerous door shut for the sake of unseen neighbors who will never know they were spared. It is not cruelty to the patient; the patient is treated. The refusal is toward the fever, the political pressure, and the temptation to let one important person’s convenience endanger a city.
- Controls not kept: **Phule’s open well** is beautiful and probably future-worthy, but the water/dignity/social-inclusion lane is already thick and today’s pass found a rarer boundary-mercy shape; **Salk / insulin one-dollar patent stories** are morally serious, but the patent/legal/institutional complexities and aphoristic fame make them less clean as one hand-sized parable; **Naoto Matsumura** is luminous for animal mercy but less central to the human-kindness aim here; **Good Maharaja / Balachadi** is deeply worthy but overlaps the orphan-belonging / refugee-shelter shelf, especially “The Garland Around His Neck.”
- `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged.

## 2026-05-16 — web round (keeper; Bob Fletcher fields-held-for-return / Dirk-Gilmore-Ivan-Luz controls)
- Re-opened `rev0498`, checked the current shelf and URL ledger, then searched for a moral shape that was not simply more rescue, hidden charity, substitutionary sacrifice, protected prayer, or public-health boundary.
- Fresh / rechecked lanes searched: Iván Fernández Anaya / Abel Mutai finish-line honesty; a duplicate-control recheck of Dirk Willems turning back across ice for the pursuer; Georgia Gilmore and the Club from Nowhere; Luz Long / Jesse Owens as a reliability-control because the advice portion is contested; and Bob Fletcher safeguarding Japanese American farms during wartime incarceration.
- **Kept / added:** “The Fields He Kept for Their Return,” based on Bob Fletcher tending the Tsukamoto, Nitta, and Okamoto farms while the families were incarcerated. The source spine is strong through Densho, Smithsonian, and obituary/reporting accounts: Fletcher quit his agricultural-inspector work, managed about ninety acres, paid mortgages and taxes, faced hostility including a shot into a barn, kept only part of the profits, and returned the farms and saved proceeds when the families came home.
- Why it cleared the bar: the story is not rescue from exile, but protection of return. Mercy here does not only say, “I am sorry you were taken”; it keeps vines, taxes, houses, accounts, and harvests from being devoured by the injustice in the neighbors’ absence. The rare parable-image is **holding another person’s field as a promise while the state calls him a stranger**.
- Distinct chamber on the shelf: **guarding the future/home/property of the exiled until they can return**, without treating their loss as an opportunity. It differs from hidden-gift dignity, war rescue, protected prayer, and enemy-mercy: this is stewardship against opportunism under public injustice.
- Strong controls not kept: **Dirk Willems** remains almost painfully Jesus-aligned, but the archive already keeps that chamber as **“The Footsteps That Turned Back,”** so this was duplicate-control only; **Georgia Gilmore / Club from Nowhere** is likely future-worthy, especially the hidden cooking that made walking justice possible, but it stayed movement-scale rather than one hand-sized act today; **Iván Fernández Anaya** is a clean honesty parable but smaller beside Fletcher; **Luz Long / Jesse Owens** was not kept because the famous advice-before-qualifying scene is historically contested, though the public friendship/congratulation remains meaningful.
- `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged.

## 2026-05-18 — web round (keeper; Princess Alice deafness-as-shelter / Hosenfeld-Helmy-Diana controls)
- Re-opened the current archive and URL ledger first, then searched broadly but with the usual severe filter: generic “modern parable” hits, true kindness anecdotes, Oseola McCarty, Wilm Hosenfeld and Szpilman, the Grand Mosque of Paris, Mohamed Helmy, Princess Diana’s AIDS handshake, and Princess Alice of Battenberg.
- **Kept / added:** **“The Door That Could Not Hear,”** based on Princess Alice sheltering the Cohen family in wartime Athens. The source spine was strong enough through the Guardian, Holocaust Centre North, and Yad Vashem materials/search result: the Cohens stayed hidden in her Athens residence until liberation, and when suspicion came she used her deafness / apparent non-comprehension to keep the searchers from the truth.
- Why it cleared the bar: the moral image is unusually exact. A condition the world would call lack becomes a wall for the endangered; the wound becomes a door mercy can lock. It is not merely rescue, not merely courage, and not merely royal exception, but a hidden act where personal vulnerability is transfigured into shelter for another.
- Distinct chamber on the shelf: **weakness/disability-as-refuge under interrogation**. This is adjacent to Sugihara’s papers, Roddie Edmonds’ shared-name formation, Korczak’s accompaniment, and Bob Fletcher’s stewardship, but its mechanism is different: she protects not by documents, numbers, fields, or public solidarity, but by letting her own silence confuse the hunters.
- Controls not kept: **Hosenfeld / Szpilman** is a powerful reserve candidate, especially the bread, coat, and “can you play?” moment, but the rescuer’s Nazi-system entanglement and the archive’s already-strong enemy-rescue lanes kept it just below today’s keeper. **Mohamed Helmy** is morally serious but sprawling and deception-heavy; **Grand Mosque of Paris** remains beautiful but historically and evidentially complex in the broad versions; **Oseola McCarty** is luminous but close to widow’s-mite / life-savings giving; **Princess Diana’s AIDS handshake** is true and important but too close to public stigma-breaking and touch-the-untouchable symbolism already near the Gospel itself.
- `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged; no original parable felt more necessary than the keeper.

## 2026-05-18 — web round (keeper; secret whisper before theft / Guardian-HiddenBrain-Grueninger-Rwanda controls)
- Re-opened `rev0500`, checked the current shelf and URL ledger, then searched recent Guardian *Kindness of Strangers* stories, Hidden Brain / NPR *My Unsung Hero* entries, Paul Grüninger border-rescue sources, and Rwanda / Mbaye Diagne rescue-forgiveness controls.
- **Kept / added:** “The Shirt He Folded Back,” based on Everett Miller’s account: as a 15-year-old about to shoplift a shirt, he was stopped by an older teenage girl who crouched beside him and whispered, privately and without judgment, that it was not worth it; he put the shirt back, bought it, and remembered the intervention for decades.
- Why it cleared the bar: the act is small but morally exact. It is neither accusation nor permissiveness. The girl enters the hidden place before the boy’s act hardens into a name, corrects him without making a spectacle, and keeps watch until his own hands choose the clean path. The rare moral chamber is **preventive mercy that guards a person’s future before shame, law, and identity have closed around him**.
- Controls not kept: **Paul Grüninger** is powerful, especially the legal-calendar mercy of backdated documents, but it stands too close to the archive’s existing visa / papers / border-rescue shelf, particularly Sugihara. **Mbaye Diagne** remains a serious future reserve because of the small car making repeated rescue trips through roadblocks, but today it felt more heroic-rescue than hand-sized parable. The new Guardian stories were humane, but most overlapped existing lanes: public-safety intervention, small travel hospitality, body-shame covering, practical repair, or grief-comfort. Other Hidden Brain candidates were warm but less singular than the secret anti-shame correction.
- `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged.

## 2026-05-18 — web round (keeper; rivals became legs / Stigler-Rabe-Harrison controls)
- Re-opened `rev0501`, checked the latest keeper shelf and URL ledger, then searched away from the recent hidden-rescue / anti-shame lanes into sportsmanship, enemy-mercy in war, atrocity-shelter, complicated rescuer biographies, and lifesaving bodily donation.
- **Kept / added:** “The Rivals Who Became Her Legs,” based on the 2008 Central Washington / Western Oregon softball game: Sara Tucholsky hit the first home run of her career, tore her ACL after missing first base, and could not be helped by her own side without losing the home run; opponents Mallory Holtman and Liz Wallace carried her around the bases, stopping so she could touch each one.
- Why it cleared the bar: the image is almost impossibly clean. The only people able to preserve the truth of the fallen runner's joy were the people who stood to lose by it. Mercy here does not ignore the rules or the contest; it fulfills the rules by refusing to profit from another person's helplessness.
- Distinct chamber on the shelf: **opponent-as-legs / scoreboard surrendered for rightful joy.** This differs from enemy-forgiveness, public shame-covering, hidden charity, and heroic rescue because the kindness takes place inside fair competition, where victory itself becomes morally smaller than carrying the rival home.
- Controls not kept: **Franz Stigler / Charlie Brown** is moving and strongly shaped as enemy-mercy, but it remains war-chivalry terrain and sits near existing battlefield/enemy-mercy entries; **John Rabe / Minnie Vautrin / Nanjing Safety Zone** are morally grave and important but too atrocity-scale and biography-complex for a hand-sized parable this pass; **Albert Göring** was left out because the evidence/recognition trail is contested enough to make a clean retelling unsafe; **James Harrison's blood donations** are beautiful and possibly reserve-worthy, but the softball story felt more sharply parabolic today. **Paul Cotter's Alzheimer’s dignity-job story** resurfaced but is already represented in the archive as “The Firm That Let Him Keep Working.”
- `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged.

## 2026-05-18 — web round (keeper; chicken at the door / Arland-StoryCorps controls)
- Re-opened `rev0502`, checked the current shelf and URL ledger, then searched in several lanes: StoryCorps stranger-kindness / Holocaust-rescue stories, recent Guardian kindness pieces, public heroism and self-sacrifice around Air Florida Flight 90 / Arland Williams, enemy-mercy controls, and the Rob Parsons / Ronnie Lockwood hospitality story.
- **Kept / added:** “The Chicken at the Door,” based on Rob and Dianne Parsons welcoming Ronnie Lockwood in Cardiff near Christmas 1975: he arrived with his belongings and a frozen chicken, they cooked it, gave him a bath and a bed, and the one-night welcome became forty-five years of shared household life. Later accounts emphasize that Ronnie became part of the family, worked as a dustman, volunteered, helped the household in its weakness, and after his death left money that helped complete the roof / centre connected with welcoming others.
- Why it cleared the bar: the first image is hand-sized and unforgettable — a knock, a bag, a frozen chicken, and the word “Come in.” But the deeper shape is not one-night charity; it is hospitality allowed to become mutual belonging. The welcomed man is not frozen forever as a recipient or project; he becomes a member, helper, giver, and finally even a roof for others.
- Distinct chamber on the shelf: **stranger-as-hidden-household-member / hospitality becoming mutual roof.** This is adjacent to abandoned-child and lifelong-carrying stories such as “The Garland Around His Neck,” but it differs because the center is an adult guest first received as need and later revealed as gift to the very household that received him.
- Strong control not kept: **Arland Williams / “the man in the water.”** The story is morally severe and likely worthy in isolation — the trapped survivor passing the rescue line to others until he drowned — but the archive has just admitted “The Jackets They Took Off,” and this would have risked crowding the shelf with another cold-water survival-device self-sacrifice. I logged the source spine for possible later reconsideration.
- Other controls not kept: StoryCorps’ Philip Lazowski/Ruth account was moving but stayed in wartime-child-rescue terrain already heavily represented; Guardian stranger-kindness items about a job lead and bikers changing a tire were humane but not Jesus-grade for this shelf; broad “forgiveness / mother and murderer” search results were too noisy or too close to existing forgiveness-of-killer entries.
- `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged; no original parable felt more necessary than the human keeper.

## 2026-05-18 — web round (keeper; Mister Rogers prayer request / Albert-Dale-Narayanan controls)
- Re-opened `rev0503`, checked the current shelf and URL ledger, then moved into dignity-through-receiving rather than another hidden-rescue or anti-shame lane. The strongest pass compared Fred Rogers' request for prayer from a suffering boy, Albert Lexie's shoeshine-tip giving, Dale Schroeder's carpenter-scholarship bequest, and Narayanan Krishnan's chef-to-homeless-feeding story.
- **Kept / added:** “The Prayer He Asked For,” based on Tom Junod's *Esquire* profile of Fred Rogers. A boy with cerebral palsy, long accustomed to being prayed for and treated as an object of care, met Rogers; Rogers waited through the boy's fear, then asked the boy to pray for him. Rogers later resisted the idea that this was merely a therapeutic trick, saying he asked because someone who had suffered so much might be close to God.
- Why it cleared the bar: the mercy is not pity from above but reverent dependence. The wounded child is not merely soothed, entertained, or served; he is needed. The turn is rare: kindness becomes complete by receiving from the one everyone else only gives to.
- Distinct chamber on the shelf: **recipient-as-intercessor / burden revealed as bearer of blessing.** It is adjacent to `The Office He Gave the Child` and other dignity-restoration entries, but different because the helper does not merely assign a role; he asks for help he genuinely believes he needs.
- Controls not kept: **Albert Lexie** is very beautiful, especially the kneeling shoeshine man giving all tips to sick children, but it overlaps the archive's life-savings / widow's-mite / humble-donor lanes more than the Rogers story does. **Dale Schroeder** is worthy generosity, but also lives in the hidden-life-savings scholarship chamber. **Narayanan Krishnan** has a strong first conversion image and real service, but the later institutional controversy makes it unsuitable for this shelf without a narrower, more cautious treatment.
- `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged; no original parable felt more necessary than the human keeper.

## 2026-05-18 — web round (keeper; candles she could feel / Bzeek-Dashrath-StoryCorps-ICU controls)
- Re-opened `rev0504`, checked the protected shelf and URL ledger, then searched anti-shame kindness, StoryCorps small mercies / LGBTQ dignity stories, restorative forgiveness controls, Julio Diaz / mugger mercy, Dashrath Manjhi / road-through-mountain, David Deutchman / ICU Grandpa, and Mohamed Bzeek's terminal foster-care sources.
- **Kept / added:** “The Candles She Could Feel,” based on Mohamed Bzeek's care for terminally ill foster children in Los Angeles County, especially the sixth-birthday scene in the L.A. Times account: the child was blind, deaf, medically fragile, and her biological parents did not come; Bzeek lit six candles in a cheesecake, held the cake close so she could feel the warmth, and she smiled when the smoke reached her.
- Why it cleared the bar: the mercy is not merely caregiving or heroic endurance. It is joy translated into the language of one small child’s remaining senses. The feast is not canceled because the guest cannot see, hear, answer, or perform gratitude; instead the feast is carried close enough to become real to her.
- Distinct chamber on the shelf: **celebration adapted to the least / joy by touch.** This sits near abandoned-child and deathbed-dignity entries such as “The Garland Around His Neck,” but differs because the center is not lifelong carrying or rescue from death; it is making a celebration intelligible to a child whom many would have reduced to pity.
- Strong controls not kept: **Mohamed Bzeek’s broader life** could have been told through the empty plate and spoon for the boy who could not eat, or the language of touch for children who cannot see/hear/speak, but the birthday-candle moment felt cleanest and least generic. **David Deutchman / ICU Grandpa** is beautiful but broader, with less singular moral turn. **Dashrath Manjhi** remains a possible reserve: grief carved into a public road, but it felt more perseverance/legacy than immediate Jesus-grade tenderness today. **Julio Diaz and the mugger** was logged as a control, but the archive already has strong thief-mercy stories, including “The Shoes He Tried to Steal,” “The Supper After the Purse,” and “The Shirt He Folded Back.” **Mary Johnson / Oshea Israel / Azim Khamisa** overlaps directly with “The Promise Kept” and nearby murder-forgiveness entries. **StoryCorps Aunt Yaya / Mother Bryant / switchboard stories** were moving and humane, especially in dignity-protection and identity-shelter lanes, but did not outshine the Bzeek candle image for this pass.

## 2026-05-20 — web round (keeper; tea across road / Banya-Homeboy-Guardian controls)

- Why it cleared the bar: the mercy is a prepared table within sight of despair. He did not merely notice one crisis; he arranged his ordinary house as a standing answer to those who had run out of road. The moral center is not “hero saves victim,” but **hospitality made ready before the stranger knows how to ask**.
- Distinct chamber on the shelf: **table beside the abyss / watchful hospitality as life-preserving invitation.** It sits near `The Woman Who Met His Eyes` and wrong-number night-watch kindness, but differs because the image is spatial and sacramental: a home, a road, a kettle, and a neighbor who has decided that the edge must never be left without a door nearby.

The clean parable-image is the victim's father sitting at the bomber's father's kitchen table, seeing the family photographs, and refusing to curse the household that had also been wounded.

- Distinct chamber on the shelf: **innocent family of the wrongdoer / mercy that refuses inherited guilt.** This sits near murder-forgiveness entries such as “The Dinner With the Enemy,” “The Mother Who Made Room for Another Son,” and “The Daughter Who Sat With the Driver,” but differs because the mercy is aimed first at the perpetrator's father and sister, not the perpetrator himself.

## 2026-05-20 — web round (keeper; veil laid aside / Hardaga-Kabiljo return mercy; Imam-Srdjan controls)

The hand-sized image that cleared the bar was twofold: the Muslim household laying aside the veil before Josef because he had been made brother, and mercy returning fifty years later through Jewish papers and a convoy to Jerusalem.
- Why it cleared the bar: this is not only hidden rescue. The story becomes a parable of kinship made before repayment is possible, and of a good deed remembering the road home after half a century. It carries a rare mutuality without becoming transactional: the first mercy is gratuitous; the second is remembrance, not payment.
- Distinct chamber on the shelf: **interfaith kinship returned across generations / hospitality that makes future refuge.** It sits near wartime shelter entries, but differs from them by centering reciprocal rescue over time rather than the hiding act alone.

Broad My Unsung Hero and anti-shame searches surfaced humane stories but nothing that surpassed the keeper.

- Why it cleared the bar: the strongest image is not philanthropy after surplus, but payroll from ashes. The owner could have let money first save itself; instead he treated wages, medicine, and children's suppers as more urgent than the safest retreat of capital.
- Distinct chamber on the shelf: **profit made servant to workers / stewardship that counts households before treasure.** It sits near dignity-through-work and hidden-wages entries, but differs by centering the owner at the moment of business catastrophe, when the temptation is to save assets while letting families bear the disaster.
